The Book of Elon: A Guide to Purpose and Success

NOTES ON THIS BOOK关于本书的说明

This is a book of Elon’s ideas in his own words. I built this book entirely out of transcripts, tweets, and interviews over his entire life to date. The body of this book comes directly from him. On this, a few important points:

这是一本用埃隆本人话语汇编的思想集。我完全基于他迄今为止的文字记录、推文和采访整理而成。本书内容直接来源于他本人。关于这一点，有几点重要说明：

Everything in this book is taken out of context. To create this book, I recontextualized ideas in an attempt to make them more accessible, useful, and memorable.

本书中的所有内容均被摘录出上下文。为了创作本书，我对这些思想进行了重新语境化，试图使其更易理解、更实用且更令人印象深刻。

As ideas passed through time, space, and medium, phrasing may have shifted in flight. Every effort was made to keep the original intent, but errors are possible. His original intent may be different from your interpretation in a different time, format, and context.

随着时间、空间和媒介的变迁，表达方式可能有所变化。我们尽力保持原意，但仍可能存在错误。他的原意可能与你在不同时间、形式和语境下的理解有所不同。

All brilliance in this book is Elon’s; any mistakes are mine.

本书中的所有精彩内容均出自埃隆；任何错误均由我承担。

Highlights

重点摘录

Highlights are used to summarize or punctuate an idea. You could flip through the book reading just these and find valuable ideas. Highlights are easy to spot because they look like this:

重点摘录用于总结或强调某个观点。你可以翻阅本书，仅阅读这些内容，就能获得宝贵的思想。重点摘录易于识别，因为它们看起来像这样：

I am a highlight.

我是一个重点摘录。

Bolded Questions

加粗的问题

Many excerpts are from interviews; some are written to provide proper context. The questions help create the feeling of an intimate dialogue between you and Elon.

许多摘录来自采访；有些则是为了提供恰当的语境而撰写。问题部分帮助营造你与埃隆之间亲密对话的感觉。

Skip Around

随意跳读

Jump to anything that interests you and skip anything that doesn’t. This is a choose-your-own-adventure book, though some ideas build on previous examples.

跳转到任何你感兴趣的内容，跳过不感兴趣的部分。这是一本“选择你自己的冒险”书，尽管有些观点是建立在之前例子的基础上的。

FOREWORD前言

—by Naval Ravikant

——纳瓦尔·拉维坎特

You’re holding in your hands the only book an entrepreneur needs. A lone founder takes a unique journey to discover and encapsulate the truth into a product. Weighted by responsibility and expectation, the founder seeks out mentors and books—but the best mentors can’t be hired, and the books are either anecdotal or academic. The only reliable teacher is bitter experience.

你手中拿着的是创业者唯一需要的书。孤独的创始人踏上一段独特的旅程，将真理发现并凝练成产品。肩负责任与期望，创始人寻求导师和书籍——但最好的导师无法雇佣，书籍要么是轶事，要么是学术性的。唯一可靠的老师是苦涩的经验。

In this book, Eric put his ego aside and did the thankless work of compiling Elon’s best lessons, in Elon’s own words, as practical as possible. This isn’t a tedious biography or recounting of events, it’s the explanation and the manual. An eager co-founder stole my first copy, and my coworkers will get the next twenty.

在这本书中，埃里克放下自我，做了那份不被感激的工作，汇编了埃隆最好的教训，用埃隆自己的话，尽可能实用。这不是枯燥的传记或事件回顾，而是解释和手册。一位热心的联合创始人偷走了我的第一本，我的同事们将得到接下来的二十本。

Elon is the greatest entrepreneur since Steve Jobs, but he isn’t just a copy. History doesn’t repeat, but it does rhyme, and it’s the occasional Napoleon, whether in science, art, or politics, who appears and reroutes the inexorable forces of history with a unique vision and drive. Hard work is common, intelligence uncommon, courage rare—and Elon combusts all three.

埃隆是自史蒂夫·乔布斯以来最伟大的企业家，但他不仅仅是一个复制品。历史不会重演，但会押韵，偶尔会有拿破仑式的人物，无论在科学、艺术还是政治领域出现，以独特的视野和驱动力改写历史的必然力量。努力工作很常见，智慧不多见，勇气更罕见——而埃隆兼具这三者。

The banal observation is that he’s a founder of Tesla, SpaceX, OpenAI, and Neuralink. These companies, however, are just means to products—the self-driving cars, the autonomous robots, the thinking machines, and the awe-inspiring, ground-shattering, heavy rockets that thunder into space and land on their toes. Even the products are just a means unto the mission. If it’s allowed by the laws of physics, and takes us into our sci-fi dreams, Elon will drag us there.

平凡的观察是他是特斯拉、SpaceX、OpenAI 和 Neuralink 的创始人。然而，这些公司只是通向产品的手段——自动驾驶汽车、自主机器人、思考机器，以及令人敬畏、震撼的重型火箭，它们轰鸣着飞向太空并精准着陆。即使这些产品也是实现使命的手段。如果物理定律允许，且能带我们进入科幻梦想，埃隆会带我们去那里。

The wealth, the fame, and the envy are a byproduct. Crabs in the gravity well, blinded by politics, will point to his father’s and the government’s money. But envy says more about the envious than the envied. Elon sleeps on the factory floor, doubles down every time, and never gives up. The people worth impressing are impressed, and millions of children are inspired to make real change. Culture is downstream of the technology that enables us to be generous and free, and Elon will be remembered long after the big talkers and small doers are forgotten.

财富、名声和嫉妒只是副产品。被政治蒙蔽的“引力井里的螃蟹”会指责他的父亲和政府的钱。但嫉妒更多反映的是嫉妒者而非被嫉妒者。埃隆睡在工厂地板上，每次都加倍努力，永不放弃。值得打动的人被打动，数百万孩子受到启发去实现真正的改变。文化是技术的下游产物，而技术使我们慷慨和自由，埃隆将被铭记，远超过那些空谈者和小打小闹者。

As Chief Engineer, Elon reminds us that building value is building things, not financing or managing them. It’s a better world when the richest man is one who creates wealth, rather than shuffles it around, or seizes it in the name of the people.

作为首席工程师，埃隆提醒我们，创造价值就是创造事物，而不是融资或管理它们。当最富有的人是创造财富的人，而不是转移财富或以人民名义夺取财富时，世界会更美好。

Wealth, as the physicist David Deutsch wrote, is the set of physical transformations that we can effect—true for both individuals and societies. The main component of wealth is knowledge, not capital. By creating new knowledge, and then instantiating it in products that are duplicated and distributed, Elon and his fellow entrepreneurs are engines of wealth creation and distribution.

正如物理学家大卫·多伊奇所写，财富是我们能够实现的一系列物理变换——这对个人和社会都适用。财富的主要组成部分是知识，而非资本。通过创造新知识，然后将其体现在可复制和分发的产品中，埃隆和他的企业家伙伴们是财富创造和分配的引擎。

You may have the opportunity to ask yourself: When humanity went to the stars, what were you doing? When men walked on Mars, were you mocking them or exhorting them? When they mined the first asteroids and built the first space stations? When fleets of self-driving cars reclaimed cities from parking and traffic? When the paraplegics rose up and walked alongside us? When the robots took over the miserable and repetitive work? Were you in the front row, cheering? Or sour-faced in the bleachers, jeering?

你或许会问自己：当人类走向星辰时，你在做什么？当人类登陆火星时，你是在嘲笑他们还是鼓励他们？当他们开采第一批小行星，建造第一座空间站时？当自动驾驶车队从停车和交通中解放城市时？当截瘫者站起来与我们同行时？当机器人接管了枯燥重复的工作时？你是在前排欢呼，还是在看台上板着脸嘲讽？

There is even a third option—young or old, your life isn’t over yet. Elon’s methods are copy-able, in matters big and small. Eric has laid them out for you as plainly as possible. If your motives are pure and greater than yourself, the world will conspire in its subtle ways to help you. When one man shows what’s possible, a million arise, not as followers, but as missionaries in their own right.

还有第三种选择——无论年轻或年长，你的生命尚未结束。埃隆的方法可以复制，无论大小事务。埃里克已尽可能清晰地为你呈现。如果你的动机纯粹且超越自我，世界将以微妙的方式助你一臂之力。当一个人展示了可能性，百万人成为传教士，而非追随者。

You don’t have to do what Elon does, and you don’t have to do it his way, but this short life is best spent in the arena, on something other than the mundane and insatiable self. Reject the craving for comfort and social approval. Reorient to the optimism of youth. Leave those talking and dividing, and get on the path to learning and building.

你不必做埃隆所做的事，也不必用他的方式，但这短暂的人生最好在竞技场上度过，做些非平凡且不满足于自我的事。拒绝对舒适和社会认可的渴望。重新定位于青春的乐观。离开那些空谈和分裂的人，走上学习和创造的道路。

Your energy is best spent with like-minded people who are unstoppable on a mission to make something beautiful. Don’t make the thing to make the money, make the money so that you can make the thing. Don’t get paid for work, get paid so that you can do your best work. Rise up, choose inspiration over envy, and show your younger and older selves that the desires of this life were completely and totally exhausted into the universal will.

你的精力最好花在志同道合、不可阻挡、致力于创造美好事物的人身上。不要为了赚钱而做事，要为了做事而赚钱。不要为工作拿报酬，要为能做最好的工作拿报酬。振作起来，选择灵感而非嫉妒，向你年轻和年长的自己展示，这一生的渴望已完全融入宇宙意志。

ERIC’S WELCOME TO THIS BOOK埃里克对本书的欢迎词

Hello, and welcome!

你好，欢迎你！

I’m delighted you picked up this book. I’ve put thousands of hours into collecting, curating, and editing Elon’s most useful ideas for you.

我很高兴你拿起了这本书。我花费了数千小时，收集、整理并编辑了埃隆最有用的想法，献给你。

You may be seeking purpose, a mission worthy of your life’s effort. You might already have a clear purpose and seek the tools for success. You will find both in this book.

你可能正在寻找人生的目标，一项值得你毕生努力的使命。你也许已经有了明确的目标，正在寻找成功的工具。这本书里你都能找到。

Elon’s purpose is to help humanity survive and thrive. He thinks in sweeping timescales, like a sci-fi epic. By funding and leading SpaceX and Tesla, Elon Musk has done the entrepreneurial equivalent of running a two-minute mile. Twice. At the same time.

埃隆的目标是帮助人类生存并繁荣。他思考的是宏大的时间尺度，像一部科幻史诗。通过资助并领导SpaceX和特斯拉，埃隆·马斯克完成了创业领域相当于两分钟跑完一英里的壮举，而且是两次，同时进行。

Regardless of your industry, mission, or beliefs—there is something to learn from this feat.

无论你的行业、使命或信仰如何——从这一壮举中都有值得学习的东西。

After seeing what Elon and his teams have accomplished, we stand taller, and the universe seems smaller.

看到埃隆和他的团队所取得的成就后，我们变得更加自信，宇宙似乎也变得更小了。

To me, Musk represents the idea that I am capable of more than I ever imagined. Even more importantly, he shows that together we are capable of the currently impossible.

对我来说，马斯克代表着我能够超越自己想象的能力。更重要的是，他展示了我们共同能够实现目前看似不可能的事情。

He models our moral duty to grab the impossible and drag it into the possible through massive effort, ingenuity, and force of will. This is how we build an ever-better future.

他示范了我们的道德责任——通过巨大的努力、创造力和意志力，将不可能变为可能。这就是我们建设更美好未来的方式。

In the past, the richest man on Earth was a monarch, oil heir, or financier. Now, the richest man on Earth is an engineer, entrepreneur, and American immigrant who has taken massive personal risks to build new things that solve problems on a planetary—and interplanetary—scale. We are learning to respect the builders more than the rulers.

过去，地球上最富有的人是君主、石油继承人或金融家。现在，地球上最富有的人是一位工程师、企业家和美国移民，他承担了巨大的个人风险，创造了在地球乃至星际范围内解决问题的新事物。我们正在学会更加尊重建设者，而非统治者。

Elon Musk has joined a lineage of legendary entrepreneurs who transformed our culture. Henry Ford’s greatest impact was creating car culture. Steve Jobs’s greatest impact was on computer culture. Elon Musk’s greatest impact will be advancing the culture of engineering, entrepreneurship, and adventure over the next century.

埃隆·马斯克加入了传奇企业家的行列，这些人改变了我们的文化。亨利·福特最大的影响是创造了汽车文化。史蒂夫·乔布斯最大的影响是计算机文化。埃隆·马斯克未来一个世纪最大的影响将是推动工程、创业和冒险文化的发展。

Elon’s energy, vision, and his team’s achievements have stoked a fire in my chest. I feel activated to advance human prosperity, knowing that humans are a few years into an inspiring million-year journey of knowledge and construction. Working on this book gave me a new level of spiritual satisfaction and inspiration. You and I may be ants, but we are building an intergalactic cathedral.

埃隆的能量、远见以及他团队的成就点燃了我内心的火焰。我感到被激励去推动人类繁荣，知道人类正处于一段激动人心的百万年知识与建设旅程的初期。参与这本书的创作给了我新的精神满足和灵感。你我或许只是蚂蚁，但我们正在建造一座星际大教堂。

I want you to stare down the biggest problems humanity faces and attack them with furious ingenuity. If the next generation has one million Musks, our grandchildren will flourish beyond our wildest dreams.

我希望你直面人类面临的最大问题，并以狂热的创造力去攻克它们。如果下一代有一百万个马斯克，我们的孙辈将超越我们最狂野的梦想而繁荣。

One million Musks. That’s my dream for this book.

一百万个马斯克。这是我对这本书的梦想。

Seeing life-changing ideas dissolve into the algorithms breaks my heart. I build these books to rescue knowledge and preserve it in our most proven, permanent format.

看到改变人生的想法消散在算法中让我心碎。我写这些书是为了拯救知识，并将其保存在我们最可靠、最持久的形式中。

When I create books, I focus on making them as useful as possible. No fluff, no filler. I hope you have a highlight on every single page.

当我创作书籍时，我专注于让它们尽可能有用。没有废话，没有填充。我希望你能在每一页都找到值得标记的内容。

As you read, imagine you are out to dinner with Elon Musk. You’re deep in conversation as he shares stories and ideas you can apply in your own life and work. I hope you feel personally tutored by the greatest living entrepreneur.

阅读时，想象你正与埃隆·马斯克共进晚餐。你们深入交谈，他分享的故事和想法可以应用到你自己的生活和工作中。我希望你能感受到这位当代最伟大企业家的亲自指导。

I focus entirely on Elon’s most useful ideas. We spend zero pages on Elon’s family life or politics. This is not a biography or a gossip column. My books are about sharing timeless ideas that work.

我完全聚焦于埃隆最有用的想法。我们不会涉及埃隆的家庭生活或政治。这不是传记，也不是八卦专栏。我的书是关于分享经久不衰且有效的理念。

This book has four parts:

这本书分为四个部分：

The first half is filled with principles and tactics Elon uses to guide his life and build his companies. I guarantee you will get valuable ideas in the first few pages.

前半部分充满了埃隆用来指导生活和建设公司的原则与策略。我保证你在前几页就能获得宝贵的想法。

The second half has more stories. They share Elon’s adventures and how he applied his principles to choose worthy missions and built SpaceX and Tesla. We finish with Elon’s view of the years ahead and how you can make an impact.

后半部分更多是故事，讲述埃隆的冒险经历，以及他如何应用原则选择有价值的使命，打造SpaceX和特斯拉。最后我们会介绍埃隆对未来几年的看法，以及你如何产生影响。

When you’re done reading (maybe a few times!), I hope you enact more of Elon’s hardcore work ethic, boundless optimism, and unabashed courage.

读完（也许不止一次！）后，我希望你能践行更多埃隆那种严苛的工作作风、无限的乐观和无畏的勇气。

If this book resonates with you, know that we’re fast friends. You’re joining a global community of allies in pushing progress. I’d love to hear from you and hope you will help us spread these ideas far and wide. Let’s leave humanity better than we found it.

如果这本书与你产生共鸣，说明我们是快朋友。你正在加入一个推动进步的全球盟友社区。我很想听到你的声音，也希望你能帮助我们将这些理念传播得更远更广。让我们让人类比我们接手时更美好。

Forward. Together.

前进。携手同行。

Eric

埃里克

PART I第一部分

Part I: Pursue Purpose

第一部分：追求目标

I don’t mind if my legacy is accurate or inaccurate, as long as I die feeling I’ve done the right thing for the future of Consciousness.2

我不介意我的遗产是准确还是不准确，只要我死时觉得自己为意识的未来做了正确的事情。2

LIVING A PURPOSEFUL LIFE有使命感地生活

You can choose to be not ordinary.

你可以选择不做一个普通人。

You can choose not to conform to the conventions taught by your parents.

你可以选择不去顺从父母教给你的那些惯例。

It’s possible for ordinary people to choose to be extraordinary.3

普通人也可以选择成为非凡的人。3

BE USEFUL做有用的人

Don’t aspire to glory; aspire to work.4

不要以荣耀为目标；要以做事为目标。4

The measure of success in my life is: “How many useful things can I get done?”

我衡量自己人生成功的标准是：“我能做成多少有用的事？”

On a day-to-day basis, I wake up in the morning and ask, “How can I be useful today?”

每天早晨醒来，我都会问自己：“今天我怎样才能有用？”

I want to maximize my utility. It’s difficult to be useful at scale.5

我想最大化自己的效用。要在大规模层面做到有用，是很难的。5

I can’t always get it right, but I aspire to make our future good. Sometimes I make mistakes. But, I try to take the set of actions most likely to improve the probability that the future will be good.6

我不可能总是做对，但我希望让我们的未来变好。有时我会犯错。但我会努力采取那组最有可能提高未来向好概率的行动。6

Try to be useful. Do useful things for your fellow human beings and the world. It’s hard to be useful, to contribute more than you consume. Can you have a positive net contribution to society? Aim for that.7

努力做一个有用的人。为你的同类和这个世界做有用的事。要真正有用很难，也就是贡献多于消耗。你能对社会产生正的净贡献吗？就朝这个方向努力。7

I have a lot of respect for someone who puts in an honest day’s work to do useful things. I admire anyone making a positive contribution to humanity. Whether that is in farming, technology, entertainment, or whatever else. To anyone useful to the rest of humanity: I admire you greatly.8

我非常尊重那些踏踏实实做一天工、去做有用事情的人。我钦佩任何为人类作出正向贡献的人。无论是在农业、科技、娱乐，还是其他任何领域。对任何对人类其他成员有用的人：我都由衷钦佩你。8

Q: How do you know if you’re helping?

问：你怎么知道自己是在帮忙？

I think about it mathematically. How many people you helped, multiplied by how much help you provided each person, on average. How many people you helped, and how much—that’s the total utility (usefulness).9 It’s almost like the physics definition of true work. If you aspire to do true work, your probability of success is much higher.10

我会用数学方式思考。你帮助了多少人，乘以你给每个人带来了多少帮助，取平均值。你帮助的人数，以及帮助的程度，这就是总效用（有用性）。9 这几乎就像物理学里对真实做功的定义。如果你志在做真实的功，你成功的概率会高得多。10

For any product you’re trying to create, ask yourself the utility improvement compared to the current state of the art, multiplied by how many people it would affect.

对于你想创造的任何产品，都问问自己：相对于当前最先进状态，它带来的效用提升是多少，再乘以它会影响多少人。

Building something that makes a big difference to a small number of people is just as great as something that makes a small difference for a vast number of people. Mathematically, the total positive impact would be roughly similar for those two things. It’s about trying to be useful.11

创造一个能给少数人带来巨大改变的东西，和创造一个能给大量人带来小幅改变的东西一样了不起。从数学上看，这两种事情产生的总正向影响大致相近。关键是努力变得有用。11

This is the mathematical first principles perspective—utility and numbers. Is some simple app really making people’s lives better? If it’s affecting a lot of people positively, even in a small way, then yes, that is good.12

这就是数学化的第一性原理视角：效用和人数。某个简单的应用真的让人们的生活变好了吗？如果它以很小的方式正向影响了很多人，那答案就是：是的，那就是好事。12

Not every product needs to change the world. Many people do lots of useful things. Just ask yourself, is what I’m doing as useful as it could be? The goal of an organization should be usefulness to society. Not every product will change the world, but if it’s making people’s lives better, that’s great.13

不是每个产品都需要改变世界。很多人在做许多有用的事。你只要问自己：我正在做的事，是否已经尽可能有用？一个组织的目标应该是对社会有用。不是每个产品都会改变世界，但只要它让人们的生活变好，那就很棒。13

This is the same advice I give to my own children: “Follow your heart in terms of what you find interesting or fulfilling to do.”14 I hope they will work extremely hard and become productive contributors to society. I’m also hopeful they will do things like engineering, writing books, or just in some way, adding more than they take from the world.15

这也是我给自己孩子的建议：“跟随你的内心，去做你觉得有意思或有成就感的事。”14 我希望他们会极其努力地工作，成为对社会有生产力的贡献者。我也希望他们去做工程、写书，或者以某种方式，给这个世界增加的东西多于索取的东西。15

A useful life is worth having lived.16

有用的一生，值得被度过。16

FIGHT FOR THE FUTURE为未来而战

Fight for the things that make you excited about the future.

为那些让你对未来感到兴奋的事情而战。

The future will not get here fast enough unless we force it.17

如果我们不推动未来，它不会足够快地到来。17

I want to make sure there is a good future for humanity. We’re on a path to understanding the nature of the universe, the meaning of life. Why are we here; how did we get here?

我想确保人类拥有一个好的未来。我们正走在理解宇宙本质、理解生命意义的道路上。我们为什么在这里；我们是怎么来到这里的？

I came to the conclusion that if we can advance the knowledge of the world—if we can do things that expand the scope and scale of consciousness—then we’re better able to ask the right questions and become more enlightened. That’s the only way forward.

我得出的结论是：如果我们能推进世界的知识，如果我们能做一些扩展意识范围和规模的事情，那么我们就更能提出正确的问题，并变得更加开明。这是唯一的前进道路。

I use scale and scope because we benefit from more consciousness and more variety in consciousness. If everyone is thinking about exactly the same things the same way, that may not create new knowledge.18 I’m motivated by curiosity more than anything.19

我说规模和范围，是因为更多的意识，以及更丰富多样的意识，会让我们受益。如果每个人都以完全相同的方式思考完全相同的事情，那可能不会创造新知识。18 比起其他任何东西，好奇心更能驱动我。19

I almost had an existential crisis trying to figure out “What does it all mean? What’s the purpose of things?”

我曾经几乎陷入存在主义危机，试图弄明白：“这一切到底意味着什么？事物的目的是什么？”

In college, I would wonder about the future and what areas were really going to have an important impact on the future of humanity as a whole. This wasn’t for a paper, just what I thought about in the shower. I came up with five areas.20

在大学时，我会思考未来，思考哪些领域会对整个人类的未来产生真正重要的影响。这不是为了写论文，只是我洗澡时会想的事。我想出了五个领域。20

The three areas I thought would have the biggest positive impact on the future of humanity were the internet, the transition to sustainable energy, and space exploration—in particular the extension of life to multiple planets.21

我认为对人类未来会产生最大正面影响的三个领域是互联网、向可持续能源转型，以及太空探索，尤其是把生命扩展到多个星球上。21

The other two, artificial intelligence and rewriting genetics, were a little more uncertain in terms of net benefit. They could be a double-edged sword, and we’re not sure which edge is the worst.22

另外两个领域，人工智能和改写基因，在净收益方面更不确定。它们可能是双刃剑，而我们还不确定哪一刃更糟。22

I didn’t ever expect to be involved in space exploration and expansion, but it seemed important to me even then. Eventually, with the capital from selling PayPal, I was able to work on all three.23

我从没想过自己会参与太空探索和扩张，但即使在那时，它对我来说也显得很重要。最终，凭借出售 PayPal 得到的资本，我得以参与这三个领域。23

Q: What would you say is your most core skill?

问：你认为自己最核心的技能是什么？

I don’t worship anything, but I devote myself to the advancement of humanity using technology.

我不崇拜任何东西，但我把自己投入到用技术推动人类进步这件事上。

My core personal competence is technology. If something has to be designed or invented and you must ensure the value of the thing you create is greater than the cost of the inputs—that’s my core skill.24

我个人的核心能力是技术。如果某个东西必须被设计或发明出来，而你必须确保你创造之物的价值大于投入成本，那就是我的核心技能。24

I look at the future from the standpoint of probabilities. It’s a branching stream of probabilities, and actions we can take now affect those probabilities: Accelerate something, slow down another thing, or maybe introduce something new.25

我从概率的角度看未来。未来是一条不断分岔的概率之流，我们现在采取的行动会影响这些概率：加速某件事，放慢另一件事，或者引入某种新东西。25

You have to ask, What are we doing to move toward the paths likely to make for a good future? That is what I care about. We’re all projecting out to the future in some way, and if you think we’re going to end up in some terrible situation, that’s depressing.26

你必须问：我们正在做什么，能让我们走向更可能创造美好未来的路径？这就是我关心的事。我们都在以某种方式把视线投向未来；如果你认为我们最终会陷入某种糟糕处境，那会令人沮丧。26

I’m interested in affecting the future in positive ways. I want to build wondrous new technologies where you feel awe when you see it: “How does that even happen? How is that possible?”27

我感兴趣的是以积极方式影响未来。我想建造奇妙的新技术，让你看到时产生敬畏感：“这到底是怎么发生的？这怎么可能？”27

I’m not trying to be some sort of savior; that’s not my goal. The importance of these things just seems obvious to me. If it seems like the obvious thing to do, I’m not sure why you would do anything else. We want to maximize the happiness of our population, propagate into the future as far as possible, and understand the nature of reality. Everything else follows from that.28

我并不是想成为某种救世主；那不是我的目标。这些事情的重要性在我看来只是显而易见。如果它看起来就是显而易见应该做的事，我不确定你为什么还会做别的。我们想最大化人类群体的幸福，让人类尽可能远地延续到未来，并理解现实的本质。其他一切都由此而来。28

Remember the future.29

别忘了未来。29

OBSESS FOR SUCCESS为成功而痴迷

Doing something I enjoy, which is useful for other people—that gives me satisfaction.30

做我喜欢、并且对别人有用的事，这会让我感到满足。30

I didn’t aspire to create companies as a kid. I just liked computers. Don’t start a company because you want to be an entrepreneur or because you want to make money. It is better to approach from this angle: What is a useful thing you could build that you wish existed in the world?31

我小时候并没有立志创建公司。我只是喜欢电脑。不要因为你想当企业家，或者因为你想赚钱，就去创业。更好的切入角度是：有什么你希望世界上存在、并且你可以建造出来的有用东西？31

I do not start companies from the standpoint of asking, “What’s the best risk-adjusted rate of return?” or what I think could be successful. I just find things that need to happen, and try to make them happen. I thought these things needed to get done. If the money was lost, okay. It was still worth trying.32

我创办公司，并不是从“最佳风险调整回报率是什么”这个角度出发，也不是从我认为哪件事可能成功出发。我只是发现有些事情需要发生，然后试着让它们发生。我觉得这些事情必须被做成。如果钱亏掉了，那也可以。它仍然值得一试。32

Try to find an overlap of your talents and what you’re interested in. You may have skill in something but don’t like doing it. Try to find work that is a good combination of things you are inherently good at but also like doing.33

试着找到你的才能和你的兴趣之间的交集。你可能擅长某件事，但并不喜欢做它。努力寻找一种工作，它既是你天生擅长的事情的良好组合，也是你喜欢做的事情。33

Then, try to get other people to work with you to create that thing. Keep making it better and better. If you create something useful, money will be the result. That’s the way a properly working economy rewards the creation of useful goods and services.34

然后，试着让其他人和你一起创造那个东西。不断把它做得更好。如果你创造了有用的东西，钱会成为结果。一个正常运转的经济体，就是这样奖励有用商品和服务的创造。34

Successful entrepreneurs come in all sizes, shapes, and flavors. I’m not sure there’s any one particular trait that makes them. However, if there is one to focus on, it would be to have an obsessive nature about the quality of the product. In this context, being obsessive-compulsive is a good thing.35

成功的创业者有各种大小、形态和风格。我不确定是否有某一种特质可以定义他们。不过，如果一定要聚焦一种，那就是对产品质量近乎执念的追求。在这个语境下，带点强迫式的执着是一件好事。35

Given that, really really, really liking what you do is a big advantage. It’s important to like whatever you’re doing. Life is too short to spend it doing something you don’t like.36

正因如此，真的、真的、真的喜欢你正在做的事，是一个很大的优势。喜欢自己正在做的事情很重要。人生太短，不该把它花在你不喜欢的事情上。36

If you like what you’re doing, you think about it even when you’re not working. It’s something your mind is drawn to. If you don’t like it, it’s much harder to make yourself work.37

如果你喜欢正在做的事，即使不工作时也会想着它。你的心智会被它吸引。如果你不喜欢它，就很难逼自己工作。37

If you’re creating something you love and think other people will love, it’s much easier to sacrifice the time and effort. If it doesn’t work out, you won’t regret it.38

如果你在创造一个你热爱、并且认为别人也会热爱的东西，那么牺牲时间和精力会容易得多。即使最后没有成功，你也不会后悔。38

My way of dealing with mental problems is to make sure you really care about what you’re doing—and take the pain.39

我处理心理问题的方式，是确保你真的在乎自己正在做的事情，然后承受痛苦。39

START BEFORE THE WORLD IS READY在世界准备好之前就开始

We say the things we believe, even when sometimes those things we believe are delusional.40

我们说出自己相信的事，即使有时我们相信的那些事是妄想。40

When you’re building a radically new product, people don’t know they want it yet, because it’s just not in their scope. Around 1946–1948, when they first started making TVs, they did a famous nationwide survey: “Will you ever buy a TV?” and around 96 percent of respondents said, “No.”41

当你在打造一种彻底全新的产品时，人们还不知道自己想要它，因为它还不在他们的认知范围内。大约在 1946 到 1948 年，当人们刚开始制造电视时，曾有一项著名的全国调查：“你以后会买电视吗？”大约 96% 的受访者说：“不会。”41

In the beginning of Tesla, no one told us they wanted an electric car…I heard that zero times.42

在特斯拉早期，没有人告诉我们他们想要一辆电动车……我一次也没听到过。42

If you need encouragement, don’t start a company.43

如果你需要鼓励，那就不要创业。43

Getting people to believe in you and what you’re doing is important. In the beginning, very few people will believe in you or what you’re doing. Over time, as you make progress, evidence will build. More and more people will start to believe.

让人们相信你以及你正在做的事很重要。一开始，很少有人会相信你或相信你正在做的事。随着时间推移，当你取得进展，证据会逐渐累积。越来越多的人会开始相信。

CREATE MORE THAN YOU CONSUME创造多于消耗

Examine your beliefs of the economy.

审视你对经济的信念。

The economy is a positive-sum game, a “grow the pie” situation. Those who assume the economy is zero-sum believe the only way to get ahead is by taking things from another.

经济是一个正和游戏，是一种“把蛋糕做大”的局面。那些认为经济是零和的人，会相信获得优势的唯一方式就是从别人那里拿走东西。

But obviously the economy today is much, much greater than it was in the past. The economic output per person is massively greater than the past. Obviously the pie has grown, and grown much faster than the population has grown.44

但显然，今天的经济规模比过去大得多、大得多。人均经济产出远远高于过去。显然，蛋糕已经变大了，而且增长速度远远快于人口增长。44

I put a lot of stock in having a grow-the-pie mindset, not a zero-sum mindset.45

我非常看重“把蛋糕做大”的心态，而不是零和心态。45

When I see people (even some smart people) with a bad attitude or doing things that seem morally questionable, it’s often because they have a zero-sum mindset.

当我看到一些人（甚至包括一些聪明人）态度很糟，或者做一些看起来道德上有问题的事，通常是因为他们持有零和心态。

They don’t realize they have a zero-sum mindset, or at least don’t realize it consciously. Those who have a zero-sum mindset believe the only way to get ahead is by taking things from others. Those who believe the pie is fixed believe the only way to have more pie is to take someone else’s pie. This is obviously false.46

他们没有意识到自己有零和心态，或者至少没有在有意识层面意识到。持零和心态的人相信，获得领先的唯一方式就是从别人那里拿走东西。那些相信蛋糕是固定的人，会认为想要更多蛋糕的唯一办法就是拿走别人的蛋糕。这显然是错误的。46

In reality, there’s a lot of pie. The economic pie is not fixed. It has grown dramatically over time. Make sure you’re not operating from a zero-sum mindset, especially without realizing it. It will result in you trying to take things from others, which is not good. It won’t benefit you.

事实上，蛋糕很多。经济蛋糕不是固定的。它随着时间大幅增长。确保你不是从零和心态出发，尤其是在自己都没有意识到的情况下。那会导致你试图从别人那里拿东西，而这并不好。它也不会让你受益。

It’s much better to work on adding to the economic pie. Create more than you consume.47

更好的做法，是努力增加经济蛋糕。创造多于消耗。47

WORK LIKE HELL像拼命一样工作

I am wired for war.48

我像是天生被设定为战斗模式。48

Q: Why doesn’t the world have more Elon Musks?

问：为什么世界上没有更多埃隆·马斯克这样的人？

If you think you want to be me or do the things I’ve done…I would say you’re probably mistaken. Long periods of my life have been very painful and difficult. I’m not sure people would want to go through that.49

如果你以为自己想成为我，或者想做我做过的事……我会说，你很可能想错了。我生命中有很长一段时间都非常痛苦、非常艰难。我不确定人们真的想经历那些。49

The amount I torture myself is next level.50 You need to have some kind of rage demon in your skull that drives you.51

我折磨自己的程度是另一个级别。50 你脑子里得有某种狂怒的东西在驱动你。51

Q: Are you always working?

问：你一直都在工作吗？

How many days a year do I not put in some meaningful amount of work? Maybe two or three.52

一年里，有多少天我没有投入某种有意义的工作量？也许两三天。52

You must be extremely tenacious. Work like hell. You have to put in eighty- to one-hundred-hour weeks every week. This will improve your odds of success.53

你必须极其顽强。像拼命一样工作。你每周都得工作八十到一百小时。这会提高你成功的概率。53

You need to work. Work hard. Like every waking hour. Particularly if you’re starting a company, you need to work super hard. Do the simple math: Somebody else is working fifty hours a week and you’re working one hundred. You’ll get twice as much done in a year.54 If other people are putting in forty-hour workweeks and you’re putting in one hundred, what takes them a year, you will achieve in four months.55

你需要工作。努力工作。几乎在每个清醒的小时里都工作。尤其是如果你在创业，就需要超级努力地工作。做个简单的数学题：别人每周工作五十小时，而你工作一百小时。一年下来，你能完成两倍的工作量。54 如果别人每周工作四十小时，而你工作一百小时，他们一年才能完成的事，你四个月就能完成。55

You’re not going to create revolutionary cars or rockets in forty hours a week. It just won’t work. Colonizing Mars isn’t going to happen on forty hours a week.56

你不可能每周工作四十小时就造出革命性的汽车或火箭。这行不通。每周四十小时，火星殖民不会发生。56

Nobody ever changed the world on forty hours a week.57

没有人靠每周四十小时改变过世界。57

I’ve done these things because I felt a strong compulsion to do them.58 I’ve been burning the candle at both ends with a flamethrower for a very long time.

我做这些事，是因为我感到一种强烈的冲动，非做不可。58 很长时间以来，我一直在用喷火器从蜡烛两头烧。

From 2007 until 2022 was nonstop pain. There was a gun to my head to make Tesla work. Pull a rabbit out of your hat, then pull another rabbit. A stream of rabbits flying through the air. If the next rabbit does not come out, you’re dead. It took a toll.

从 2007 年到 2022 年，是持续不断的痛苦。为了让特斯拉活下来，就像有一把枪顶着我的头。你得从帽子里变出一只兔子，然后再变出另一只兔子。空中飞过一连串兔子。如果下一只兔子变不出来，你就死了。这付出了代价。

You can’t be in a constant fight for survival, always in adrenaline mode, and not have it hurt you. Fighting to survive keeps you going for quite a while.59

你不能一直处在生存之战中，总是肾上腺素模式，还指望它不会伤到你。为生存而战，确实能让你撑很久。59

With that said, I would say this to twenty-something me: I think there’s some merit to not being too intense, and enjoying the moment a bit. Occasionally stopping to smell the roses would probably be a good idea.60

话虽如此，如果让我对二十多岁的自己说，我会说：不要过于强烈，也稍微享受一下当下，这有一定道理。偶尔停下来闻一闻玫瑰，可能是个好主意。60

When we were developing the Falcon One rocket on the Kwajalein Atoll, this beautiful little island in the middle of the Pacific, not once during that entire time did I pause and have a drink on the beach. Now, I realize I should have had a drink with the team on the beach…that would have been fine.61

当我们在夸贾林环礁开发猎鹰一号火箭时，那里是太平洋中间一个美丽的小岛。在那整段时间里，我一次也没有停下来，在海滩上喝一杯。现在我意识到，我本该和团队在海滩上喝一杯……那样也没问题。61

If heat death will inevitably end the universe, it actually is all about the journey.62

如果热寂终将不可避免地终结宇宙，那么真正重要的其实就是旅程本身。62

FEEL THE FEAR; DO IT ANYWAY直面恐惧，并照样行动

Look fear straight in the eye and it will disappear.

直视恐惧，它就会消失。

The nature of fear is that people don’t look at it.

恐惧的本质在于，人们不去看它。

Look at it directly and it will be gone.63

直接看着它，它就会消失。63

Q: How do you think about making a decision when everyone tells you it is a crazy idea?

问：当所有人都说你的想法很疯狂时，你如何思考决策？

First of all, I feel fear. It’s not as though I have the absence of fear. I feel it quite strongly. But when something is important enough and you believe in it enough, you do it in spite of fear.

首先，我会感到恐惧。并不是说我没有恐惧。我非常强烈地感到恐惧。但当某件事足够重要，而你又足够相信它时，你会带着恐惧去做它。

You shouldn’t think, “I feel fear about this and therefore I shouldn’t do it.” It’s normal to feel fear. If you don’t feel fear, you definitely have something mentally wrong. Just feel it and let the importance of your mission drive you to do it anyway.

你不应该这样想：“我对这件事感到害怕，所以我不该做。”感到恐惧是正常的。如果你不感到恐惧，那你肯定在心理上有点问题。感受它，然后让使命的重要性驱动你照样去做。

I also think fatalism is helpful to some degree. If you accept the true probabilities, it diminishes fear. When starting SpaceX, I thought the odds of success were less than 10 percent and I accepted I would probably lose everything. But maybe we would make some progress. If we could just move the ball forward—even if we died—maybe some other company could pick it up and keep moving and our work would still have done good.64

我还认为，某种程度上的宿命感是有帮助的。如果你接受真实的概率，恐惧就会减弱。创办 SpaceX 时，我认为成功概率低于 10%，也接受自己很可能会失去一切。但也许我们能取得一些进展。只要我们能把球向前推进一点，即使我们死了，也许另一家公司能接着推进，我们的工作仍然会产生好的作用。64

We should not be afraid of doing something just because some amount of tragedy is likely to occur.

我们不应该仅仅因为某种悲剧有可能发生，就害怕去做一件事。

If our forefathers had taken that approach, the United States wouldn’t exist.65

如果我们的先辈采取那种态度，美国就不会存在。65

Q: How do you persevere through these hard challenges? Where do you find the strength?

问：你如何在这些艰难挑战中坚持下去？你从哪里获得力量？

That’s not how I think. I think: “This is simply something important. It must get done. We will keep doing it or die trying.” I don’t need a source of strength for that. Quitting is not in my nature. I don’t care about optimism or pessimism. Fuck that. We’re going to get it done.66

我不是这样想的。我想的是：“这只是一件重要的事。它必须被做成。我们会继续做下去，或者死也要试。”我不需要什么力量来源来做到这一点。放弃不在我的天性里。我不在乎乐观还是悲观。去他的。我们一定会把它做成。66

I definitely emboldened over time.67

随着时间推移，我确实变得更大胆了。67

SEEK THE NATURE OF THE UNIVERSE探寻宇宙的本质

I don’t know the meaning of life. I don’t think we can answer that question well…yet.68

我不知道生命的意义。我不认为我们现在能很好地回答这个问题……还不能。68

We must expand humanity and consciousness to the point where we are able to answer that question.69

我们必须把人类和意识扩展到某个程度，使我们能够回答那个问题。69

It may be uncomfortable for a lot of people, but I think this is a rational and logical philosophy. Basically saying, “We don’t know what the answer is, but let’s try to find out.”70

这可能会让很多人感到不舒服，但我认为这是一种理性而合乎逻辑的哲学。基本意思是：“我们不知道答案是什么，但让我们试着找出来。”70

I think we as humans collectively know part of the answer, but only a tiny part at this point. If we can make civilization last for a million years, we will probably know much more of the answer.71

我认为我们人类作为一个整体，知道答案的一部分，但在此刻只知道非常小的一部分。如果我们能让文明延续一百万年，我们很可能会知道更多答案。71

If we date civilization from the first written language, it was about five thousand years ago. This is practically no time at all. If civilization were to last a million more years, we’re barely even at the beginning. Future humans will think of us as ancient ancients, like cavemen.72

如果从最早的书面语言算起，文明大约只有五千年历史。这几乎根本不算什么时间。如果文明还能再延续一百万年，我们甚至还只是刚刚开始。未来的人类会把我们看作远古中的远古人，就像穴居人一样。72

This is the foundation of my philosophy: I am curious about the nature of the universe.

这是我哲学的基础：我对宇宙的本质感到好奇。

We can see the archaeological evidence in the fossil record, based on what we know of physics, how we came to be at this point on Earth step by step. But that doesn’t explain how the universe came to exist in the first place.73

我们可以在化石记录中看到考古证据；基于我们对物理学的了解，我们知道自己是怎样一步一步在地球上走到今天的。但这并不能解释宇宙最初是如何存在的。73

How do a bunch of molecules develop consciousness and feelings? From a physics standpoint, the chain of events from the beginning of the universe to now is quite well understood. There was a bunch of hydrogen gas that turned into complex molecules and now an assemblage of complex molecules—us humans who can feel, talk, and think. Apparently if you leave enough hydrogen gas sitting around long enough, it starts talking to itself. That’s basically what happened here.74

一堆分子是如何发展出意识和感受的？从物理学角度看，从宇宙开始到现在的事件链条已经被理解得相当清楚。曾经有一团氢气，它变成了复杂分子，而现在又变成了一组复杂分子的集合，也就是我们这些能够感受、说话和思考的人类。显然，如果你把足够多的氢气放在那里足够久，它就会开始和自己说话。这基本上就是这里发生的事。74

Assuming you believe the physics, which appear to be true, the universe started off as quarks and electrons and quickly became hydrogen, helium, lithium, most of the periodic table. Mostly hydrogen, though. Then, over a long period of time—13.8 billion years—that hydrogen became sentient.75

如果你相信看起来真实的物理学，那么宇宙一开始是夸克和电子，很快变成氢、氦、锂，以及元素周期表中的大多数元素。不过主要还是氢。然后，在漫长的时间里，经过 138 亿年，那些氢变得有知觉了。75

Where along the path from a bunch of hydrogen to human beings did consciousness start? It’s crazy.76 I wonder, is everything conscious or is nothing conscious? Maybe it’s just degrees of consciousness or concentrations of consciousness.77

从一堆氢到人类的这条路径上，意识是从哪里开始的？这很疯狂。76 我会想：是万物都有意识，还是一切都没有意识？也许只是意识的程度不同，或者意识的浓度不同。77

Total collective consciousness is how many people there are times the average amount of consciousness per person. If we can expand consciousness by creating more humans and more digital intelligence, then our opportunity to understand the meaning of life is that much greater. I would call this the philosophy of curiosity—to better understand the nature of reality.78

总体的集体意识，等于人数乘以每个人平均拥有的意识量。如果我们能通过创造更多人类和更多数字智能来扩展意识，那么我们理解生命意义的机会就会大得多。我会把这称为好奇心哲学，也就是更好地理解现实的本质。78

What I think Douglas Adams was saying in his book The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy is “The universe is the answer.” We need to figure out what questions to ask about the answer that is the universe. The question is the hard part, and if we can properly frame the question then the answer, relatively speaking, is easy.

我认为道格拉斯·亚当斯在《银河系漫游指南》中想表达的是：“宇宙就是答案。”我们需要弄清楚，面对“宇宙”这个答案，我们应该提出什么问题。问题才是困难的部分；如果我们能恰当地框定问题，那么相对而言，答案就容易了。

We need to expand the scope and scale of consciousness in order to understand what questions to ask of the universe. This is the path forward. If we do, we will be better able to understand the nature of the universe and understand the meaning of life. As just one example, we have to get back to the moon and build a science base there. I think we could learn a lot about the nature of the universe.79

我们需要扩展意识的范围和规模，以便理解应该向宇宙提出什么问题。这就是前进的道路。如果我们做到这一点，就能更好地理解宇宙的本质，并理解生命的意义。仅举一个例子，我们必须重返月球，并在那里建立一个科学基地。我认为我们可以借此学到很多关于宇宙本质的东西。79

THINK LIKE A PHYSICIST像物理学家一样思考

I try to be hyperrational.

我尽量做到超理性。

If the reasoning fits, and you’re not violating the laws of physics, that’s the thing you should try to do.

如果推理合理，且没有违反物理定律，那就是你应该尝试做的事。

These things just don’t seem that crazy to me.80

这些事情对我来说并不那么疯狂。80

OBSESS OVER TRUTH执着于真相

Start somewhere. Then be prepared to question your assumptions, fix what you did wrong, and adapt to reality.81

从某处开始。然后准备好质疑你的假设，修正错误，适应现实。81

I am obsessed with truth. Obsessed.82 If you’re going to come up with a good solution, the truth is really, really important.83

我对真相着迷。非常着迷。82 如果你想提出一个好的解决方案，真相真的非常重要。83

This obsession with truth is why I studied physics, because physics attempts to understand the truth of the universe. Physics is about finding the provable truths of the universe, and finding truths that have predictive power.84

这种对真相的执着是我学习物理的原因，因为物理试图理解宇宙的真相。物理学是寻找宇宙中可证明的真理，以及具有预测能力的真理。84

Physics is law. Everything else is a recommendation. I’ve met many people who can break the laws of man, but I have never met anyone who could break the laws of physics.

物理是定律。其他一切都是建议。我遇到过许多能打破人类法律的人，但我从未遇到过能打破物理定律的人。

For me, physics was a natural thing to study. It was intrinsically interesting to understand the nature of the universe. I also studied computer science and information theory to understand logic. (Information theory studies the transmission, processing, extraction, and use of information.) There’s an argument that information theory is actually operating at a more fundamental level than physics.85

对我来说，学习物理是自然而然的事情。理解宇宙的本质本身就很有趣。我还学习了计算机科学和信息理论来理解逻辑。（信息理论研究信息的传输、处理、提取和使用。）有人认为信息理论实际上比物理学更为基础。85

Truth matters to me a lot. Pathologically, it matters to me.86

真相对我非常重要。病态地重要。86

No matter how smart you are, you will make some number of mistakes. Everyone makes mistakes. It’s just a question of how many and how often.87

无论你多么聪明，你都会犯一定数量的错误。每个人都会犯错。问题只是错误的数量和频率。87

In business and personal life, wishful thinking causes a lot of mistakes. You have to ask whether something is true or not. If something ever feels too easy or doesn’t quite make sense…it is probably wishful thinking.88

在商业和个人生活中，幻想思维导致了许多错误。你必须问某件事是否真实。如果某件事感觉过于轻松或不太合理……那很可能是幻想思维。88

Wishful thinking is a natural human tendency. It’s a challenge to tell the difference between believing in a new idea and persevering or pursuing an unrealistic dream. You need to be rigorous in your self-analysis. Focus on something you’re confident will have high value to someone else. Be rigorous in making that assessment too.89

幻想思维是人类的自然倾向。区分相信一个新想法和坚持不切实际的梦想是一项挑战。你需要严谨地进行自我分析。专注于你有信心对别人有高价值的事情。在做出评估时也要严谨。89

The real test of any startup is how well it responds to adversity and adapts. When most things start out they don’t make much sense, but as long as you adapt quickly you can make the company work.

任何初创企业的真正考验是它如何应对逆境并适应。当大多数事情刚开始时，它们并不太合理，但只要你快速适应，就能让公司运转起来。

Being tenacious and super focused on the truth is extremely important. Look for feedback from all sources.90

坚持不懈并高度专注于真相极其重要。寻求来自各方的反馈。90

If you have beliefs that are incompatible with a rocket getting to orbit, the rocket will not get to orbit. Physics is a harsh judge.91

如果你的信念与火箭进入轨道不兼容，火箭就无法进入轨道。物理是严厉的裁判。91

Do you have the right fundamental axioms, or truths? Are they relevant? Are you making the right conclusions based on those truths? That’s the essence of critical thinking, and yet it is amazing how often people fail to do that. Wishful thinking is innate in the human brain. You want things to be the way you wish them to be, so you tend to filter out information you shouldn’t.92

你是否拥有正确的基本公理或真理？它们是否相关？你是否基于这些真理做出了正确的结论？这就是批判性思维的本质，然而人们常常未能做到这一点。幻想思维是人脑的天性。你希望事情如你所愿，因此倾向于过滤掉不该过滤的信息。92

That’s why I always assume we’re losing, even when it looks like we might win.93

这就是为什么即使看起来我们可能会赢，我也总是假设我们在输。93

FIRST-PRINCIPLES THINKING第一性原理思维

Q: Where does first-principles thinking come from?

问：第一性原理思维源自哪里？

When you want to do something new, you have to apply the physics approach. Physicists discover counterintuitive new things, like quantum mechanics. They do that by thinking from “first principles”: building their reasoning from the ground up.94

当你想做一些新的事情时，你必须采用物理学的方法。物理学家发现了许多违反直觉的新事物，比如量子力学。他们通过“第一性原理”思考：从最基础的原理开始构建推理。94

I would encourage people to use the mental tools of physics and apply them broadly in life. They are the best tools.95

我鼓励大家使用物理学的思维工具，并广泛应用于生活中。这是最好的工具。95

The normal way we conduct our lives is reasoning by analogy. That means we do something because it’s similar to something else, or what other people are doing.

我们日常生活中通常是通过类比推理。这意味着我们做某事是因为它与其他事物相似，或者因为别人都在这么做。

When you think this way, you only get slight iterations. It’s easier to reason by analogy rather than from first principles, so that’s what we do most of the time.96 And in most of life, we should reason by analogy. Otherwise, mentally, you wouldn’t be able to get through the day. It would be too much thinking.97

用这种思维方式，你只能做出微小的改进。类比推理比第一性原理推理更容易，所以我们大多数时候都是这样做的。96在大多数生活场景中，我们应该用类比推理。否则，心理负担太重，根本无法度过一天。97

But for important things, that kind of thinking is too bound by convention or prior experiences. You will hear, “It’s always been done this way,” or “Nobody’s ever done it.” That is a ridiculous way to think.

但对于重要的事情，这种思维方式过于受限于惯例或以往经验。你会听到“这一直都是这么做的”或“没人做过”的说法。这种思维方式很荒谬。

Don’t just follow the trends. You can avoid following trends by thinking with the physics approach, first principles. It’s a powerful, powerful method for life in general.

不要盲目跟随潮流。通过物理学的方法——第一性原理思考，你可以避免随波逐流。这是一种非常强大的生活方法。

Look at the fundamentals and construct your reasoning from there. Then see if you have a conclusion that works or doesn’t work.98 It might or might not be different from what people have done in the past.99

关注基本原理，从那里构建你的推理。然后看看你的结论是否成立。98它可能与过去人们的做法不同，也可能相同。99

Q: How do you apply first-principles thinking?

问：如何应用第一性原理思维？

Break something down to the most fundamental principles. Start by asking: What am I most confident is true at a foundational level? That sets your axiomatic base. Then you reason up from there. Then you check your conclusions against the axiomatic truths.

将事物分解到最基本的原理。首先问自己：我最有信心的基础真理是什么？这就确定了你的公理基础。然后从这里向上推理，最后将结论与公理真理进行核对。

For instance, to approach any new technology problem, make sure you’re not violating physics with a first-principles analysis. A basic question in physics would be: Am I violating conservation of energy or momentum? If so, it’s not going to work. That’s just to establish if this idea is possible.100

例如，解决任何新技术问题时，要确保你的第一性原理分析没有违反物理定律。物理学中的基本问题是：我是否违反了能量守恒或动量守恒？如果是，那就行不通。这只是为了确定这个想法是否可行。100

It’s hard to think this way. It takes a lot of effort. But if you’re trying to do something new, it’s the best way to think.101

这样思考很难，需要付出很多努力。但如果你想做新事物，这是最好的思考方式。101

Q: How have you applied first-principles thinking to building companies?

问：你如何将第一性原理思维应用于创业？

Here’s an example from early in building Tesla. People said battery packs were too expensive to make cheap electric cars. They assumed they would always be expensive, because they had been in the past. That’s pretty dumb. If you applied that reasoning to everything new, then you would never try anything new. “Oh, nobody wants a car. Horses are great; we’re used to them. They can eat grass. There’s lots of grass all over the place. There’s no gasoline available. So people will never buy gas cars.” People did say that, a lot.

这里有一个特斯拉早期的例子。有人说电池组太贵，无法制造廉价电动车。他们认为电池永远会很贵，因为过去一直如此。这种想法很愚蠢。如果你对所有新事物都这样推理，那你永远不会尝试新事物。“没人想要汽车，马很好，我们习惯了。马能吃草，到处都是草，没有汽油供应，所以人们永远不会买汽油车。”人们确实这么说过很多次。

People assumed batteries for electric vehicles would always cost $600 per kilowatt hour. The first-principles approach to battery costs is this: What are the batteries made of? What are the materials that make up the batteries? What is the market value of those material constituents?

人们认为电动车电池的成本永远是每千瓦时600美元。第一性原理分析电池成本的方法是：电池由什么组成？构成电池的材料是什么？这些材料的市场价值是多少？

It’s got cobalt, nickel, aluminum, carbon, some polymers for separation, and a steel can. Okay, what if we bought that amount of material at the London Metal Exchange? What would each of those things cost? Oh, geez, it’s only $80 per kilowatt hour. So clearly, we just need to think of clever ways to take those materials and combine them into the shape of a battery cell. That’s how I knew it was possible to build batteries much much cheaper than anyone else realized.102

电池含有钴、镍、铝、碳、一些用于隔离的聚合物和一个钢制外壳。好，如果我们在伦敦金属交易所购买这些材料，每种材料的价格是多少？哦，天哪，只有每千瓦时80美元。所以显然，我们只需要想出巧妙的方法将这些材料组合成电池单元的形状。这就是我知道制造电池成本远低于别人预期的原因。102

Great differences in technology exist in the world, which even hardcore technologists are unaware of.

世界上存在巨大的技术差异，甚至连资深技术人员也未必察觉。

What is simple in one arena is often profound in another.103

一个领域中简单的东西，在另一个领域往往是深刻的。103

The first-principles approach is a good way to understand what new things are possible. It doesn’t mean you’ll be successful, but at least you can determine if success is one of the possibilities, and that is important. This is how I decided to start SpaceX.104

第一性原理方法是理解新事物可能性的好方法。它不保证你一定成功，但至少能判断成功是否可能，这很重要。这就是我决定创办SpaceX的原因。104

First-principles thinking built SpaceX. Most people think, “Historically, all rockets have been expensive. Therefore, in the future, all rockets will be expensive.” But that’s not true.105 This is where it’s helpful to use the analytical approach again.106

第一性原理思维造就了SpaceX。大多数人认为“历史上所有火箭都很贵，因此未来所有火箭也会很贵。”但这不是真的。105这时再次使用分析方法非常有帮助。106

The way we applied first-principles thinking to rocketry was asking, “What are the materials that go into a rocket?”107 A rocket is made from aluminum, titanium, copper, and carbon fiber. Break it down further and ask, “How much of each material is used? Now, what is the cost of all these raw components?”108

我们应用第一性原理思维于火箭时，问：“火箭由哪些材料组成？”107火箭由铝、钛、铜和碳纤维制成。进一步拆解，问：“每种材料用量是多少？这些原材料的成本是多少？”108

If you have them stacked on the floor and could wave a magic wand to create the rocket, what would the cost of the rocket be? We imagine the cost of rearranging the atoms was zero.109

如果你把这些材料堆在地上，挥动魔杖就能造出火箭，火箭成本是多少？我们假设重新排列原子成本为零。109

That’s going to set the floor of the cost of the rocket. I call this the “magic wand number,” the hypothetical best-case scenario. For rockets, that turned out to be a relatively small number, well under 5 percent of the current cost, in some cases closer to 1 or 2 percent.110 The manufacturing must be very inefficient if the raw material cost is only 1 or 2 percent of the finished product.111

这将设定火箭成本的底线。我称之为“魔杖数字”，即假设的最佳情况。对于火箭来说，这个数字相对较小，某些情况下甚至不到当前成本的5%，接近1%或2%。110如果原材料成本只占成品的1%或2%，那么制造过程一定非常低效。111

I was able to see a great deal of room for improvement.112 Now our challenge was to figure out how to get the atoms in the right shape more efficiently.113

我看到了巨大的改进空间。112现在我们的挑战是如何更高效地将原子组合成正确的形状。113

That first-principles thought process around the rocket became general purpose for all parts. I call it “The Idiot Index.” How much more does a finished product cost than the cost of its materials? If a part or product had a high Idiot Index, we could cut the cost with more efficient manufacturing techniques.114

围绕火箭的第一性原理思考过程后来成为所有零件的通用方法。我称之为“白痴指数”。成品成本比材料成本高多少？如果某个零件或产品的白痴指数很高，我们就可以通过更高效的制造技术降低成本。114

A component that costs $1,000 when the aluminum it was made of costs only ten dollars likely has a design that is too complex or an inefficient manufacturing process. If the ratio is high, you’re an idiot.

如果一个零件成本1000美元，而它所用铝材仅值10美元，那么设计可能过于复杂或制造效率低下。如果比例很高，那你就是个白痴。

One part of the rocket, the half nozzle jacket, cost $13,000. But it was only made of $200 worth of steel. I expect all my engineers to know all the best and worst parts in their systems as judged by the idiot index at all times.115

火箭的一个部件——半喷嘴护套，成本13000美元，但只用了价值200美元的钢材。我期望所有工程师随时了解他们系统中按白痴指数评判的最佳和最差零件。115

That’s what I mean by thinking about things from a first-principles standpoint. If I had analyzed it by analogy and said, “What are all other rocket companies doing? What do their rockets cost? What historically have other rockets cost?” That is reasoning by analogy, but it really doesn’t illustrate what the true potential is.116

这就是我所说的从第一性原理角度思考。如果我用类比推理，问“其他火箭公司都做什么？他们的火箭成本是多少？历史上其他火箭成本是多少？”这就是类比推理，但它并不能真正说明潜力所在。116

The first-principles approach is a good way to figure out counterintuitive solutions. It was a helpful thing to learn.117

第一性原理方法是找出违反直觉解决方案的好方法。学会这一点很有帮助。117

THINKING IN LIMITS极限思维

Another good physics tool is thinking about things “in the limit.” Take a particular idea and imagine scaling it to a very large or very small number. How do things change?118

另一个很好的物理学工具是用“极限”思维来考虑问题。拿一个具体的想法，想象将它放大或缩小到非常大的或非常小的数量。事情会如何变化？118

The Boring Company is a great example. A common criticism of the idea of tunnels is: The tunnel will get used up and there will still be traffic congestion. They don’t realize there’s no real limit to how many levels of tunnel you can have. You can go much farther deep underground than you can build up. The deepest mines are much deeper than the tallest buildings are tall.

无聊公司（The Boring Company）是一个很好的例子。人们对隧道这个想法常见的批评是：隧道会被用尽，交通拥堵依然存在。他们没有意识到隧道层数实际上没有真正的限制。你可以挖得比建得高得多。最深的矿井远比最高的建筑还要深。

Ever notice that cities are built in 3D, but roads are only built in 2D? You could build roads in 3D by building tunnels under cities.119 You can alleviate any amount of urban congestion with a 3D tunnel network.120

你有没有注意到城市是三维建造的，但道路却只是在二维上建造？你可以通过在城市地下建造隧道来实现三维道路。119用三维隧道网络可以缓解任何程度的城市拥堵。120

But, it’s difficult and expensive to dig tunnels the way they’re currently done. The LA subway extension cost roughly a billion dollars per mile to build.121

但是，按照目前的方式挖隧道既困难又昂贵。洛杉矶地铁延伸线的建设成本大约是每英里十亿美元。121

If you do just two things, you can get approximately an order of magnitude improvement in tunneling.

“如果你只做两件事，就能在隧道挖掘上获得大约一个数量级的改进。”

But I think you can even go beyond that.122

“但我认为你甚至可以超越那个水平。122”

We need at least a tenfold improvement in the cost per mile of tunneling. The first thing to do is to cut the tunnel diameter. According to current regulations, a single road-lane tunnel has to be twenty-six to twenty-eight feet in diameter. But if you shrink that diameter to twelve feet, the area goes down by a factor of four. This is a huge improvement, because the cost of tunneling scales with the area. That’s almost a half-order of magnitude (4–5x) improvement in cost per mile right there.123

我们需要至少在每英里隧道挖掘成本上实现十倍的改进。第一件事是缩小隧道直径。根据现行规定，单车道隧道的直径必须是二十六到二十八英尺。但如果将直径缩小到十二英尺，面积就减少了四倍。这是一个巨大的改进，因为隧道挖掘的成本与面积成正比。这几乎是成本每英里减少半个数量级（4–5倍）的提升。123

Currently, tunneling machines work for half the time, then stop. The other half of the time is putting in reinforcements for the tunnel wall. If you design the machine instead to do continuous tunneling and reinforcing, that will give you a factor of two improvement. Combine those and that’s a factor of eight.124

目前，隧道掘进机工作一半时间，然后停止。另一半时间用于安装隧道壁的加固。如果你设计机器实现连续掘进和加固，这将带来两倍的改进。两者结合就是八倍的提升。124

These machines are also far from being at their power or thermal limits, so we should jack up the power to the machine. We can get at least another factor of two there, maybe even four or five. Currently, we’re not even close to the limits of tunneling technology.125

这些机器的功率和热量限制还远未达到极限，所以我们应该提升机器功率。我们至少可以再获得两倍，甚至四到五倍的提升。目前，我们离隧道技术的极限还差得远。125

Q: How does thinking in limits apply to manufacturing?

问：极限思维如何应用于制造？

Let’s say we’re trying to figure out why a part or product is expensive. Is it because of something foolish, or just because production volume is too low?126

假设我们正在试图找出为什么某个零件或产品昂贵。是因为某些愚蠢的原因，还是仅仅因为生产量太低？126

Ask, “If our volume was a million units per year, would it still be expensive?” If it’s still expensive at a million units a year, then volume is not the reason why your part is expensive. Maybe something’s wrong with the design. Maybe you can change the part to something not fundamentally expensive. That’s thinking about things to the limit.127

问自己，“如果我们的年产量达到一百万件，它还会贵吗？”如果即使在年产一百万件时仍然昂贵，那么产量就不是零件昂贵的原因。可能设计有问题，或者你可以把零件改成本质上不昂贵的东西。这就是极限思维。127

If you are really good at manufacturing and producing at a high volume, you can make anything for a cost that asymptotically approaches the raw material value of the components, plus any intellectual property you need to license. It’s a hard thing to do, but it is possible.128

如果你非常擅长制造并实现高产量生产，你可以让任何东西的成本渐近接近其原材料价值，加上你需要授权的任何知识产权。这很难做到，但确实可能。128

Q: How does thinking in the limit apply to design?

问：极限思维如何应用于设计？

When designing a product, people often start designing with the tools, parts, and methods they are familiar with. That’s their default. That will lead to a product that can be made with those tools and methods, but it is unlikely to be the perfect product.129

在设计产品时，人们通常从他们熟悉的工具、零件和方法开始设计。这是他们的默认方式。这会导致产品只能用那些工具和方法制造，但不太可能是完美的产品。129

The other way to think is to imagine the platonic ideal of the perfect product or technology. What is the perfect arrangement of atoms that would be the best possible product? Now try to figure out how to get the atoms in that shape.130

另一种思考方式是想象完美产品或技术的柏拉图理想。什么是原子排列的完美形态，能成为最佳产品？然后试着找出如何让原子排列成那种形状。130

Think through things in both directions. What can we build with the tools that we have? But also, what does the “theoretically perfect” product look like?131

从两个方向思考。我们用现有工具能建造什么？但同时，“理论上的完美”产品是什么样子？131

The idea of the “theoretically perfect” product is going to be a moving target because as you learn more, the definition for that perfect product will change. You don’t actually know what the perfect product is, but you can approximate a more perfect product. Then ask, “What tools, methods, or materials do we need to create to get the atoms in that shape?” People rarely think this way. But thinking in limits is a powerful tool.132

“理论上的完美”产品的概念会不断变化，因为随着你学到更多，对完美产品的定义也会改变。你实际上不知道完美产品是什么，但你可以逼近更完美的产品。然后问，“我们需要创造什么工具、方法或材料来让原子排列成那种形状？”人们很少这样思考。但极限思维是一个强大的工具。132

Impossible is a strong word. It’s just a strong word. I approach things from a physics standpoint, and the word impossible is more or less banned in physics.133

“不可能”是一个强烈的词。它只是一个强烈的词。我从物理学角度看问题，在物理学中，“不可能”这个词或多或少是被禁止的。133

At SpaceX especially, we often consider what’s possible within the absurd. If my team says something is impossible, I try to open their minds to new potential solutions by asking, “What would it take?”134

尤其是在SpaceX，我们经常考虑荒谬范围内的可能性。如果我的团队说某事不可能，我会试图通过问“需要什么条件？”来打开他们对新潜在解决方案的思路。134

ASPIRE TO BE LESS WRONG努力减少错误

The mental tools of physics are powerful. They tell us to assume we’re wrong and that our goal is to be less wrong. Aspire to be less wrong. I don’t think you’re going to succeed every day in being less wrong. But if you can succeed in being less wrong most of the time, you’re doing great.135

物理学的思维工具非常强大。它们告诉我们要假设自己是错的，我们的目标是减少错误。努力减少错误。我不认为你每天都能成功减少错误，但如果你大多数时候都能减少错误，那你做得很棒。135

It’s OK to be wrong. Just don’t be confident and wrong.136

犯错没关系。只是不要自信地犯错。136

Aspirationally, you want to believe things proportionate to the evidence. Not inversely proportional to the evidence.137

理想情况下，你应该根据证据的比例来相信事物，而不是与证据成反比。137

Most people can learn a lot more than they think they can.

大多数人能学到的远比他们想象的多。

They sell themselves short by not trying.138

他们因为不尝试而低估了自己。138

Read books, because the data rate of reading is much greater than when somebody is speaking. What’s the output rate of speech? A couple hundred bits per second, maybe a few thousand per second if you’re going full tilt. You can get several times that by reading. The main reason I didn’t go to lectures in college was because the data rate was too slow.139

多读书，因为阅读的信息传输速度远远超过别人说话时的速度。说话的信息输出率是多少？每秒几百比特，最多几千比特，如果你全速输出的话。阅读可以达到几倍的速度。我大学时不去听讲座的主要原因就是信息传输太慢。139

I encourage you to read a lot of books. Just read. Try to ingest as much information as you can.

我鼓励你多读书。就读吧。尽量吸收尽可能多的信息。

I was always really interested in reading. When I was a kid, I read everything I could get my hands on.140 Around nine or ten I ran out of things to read in our house, so in desperation I read the encyclopedia—which turned out to be a good idea. I found all sorts of things I didn’t even know existed—a lot, obviously.

我从小就非常喜欢阅读。小时候，我读了所有能拿到手的书。140大约九十岁时，我家里的书读完了，于是我绝望地开始读百科全书——事实证明这是个好主意。我发现了许多我甚至不知道存在的东西——显然很多。

I’d recommend everyone read or skim through the condensed version of the Encyclopedia Britannica. You can always skip subjects. If you read a few paragraphs and know you’re not interested, just jump to the next one.141

我建议大家阅读或浏览《大英百科全书》的简明版。你总可以跳过不感兴趣的主题。如果读了几段觉得没兴趣，就直接跳到下一个。141

Develop good general knowledge, so you at least have a rough “lay of the land” of the full knowledge landscape. Read a broad range of material. How can you know what you’re really interested in if you’re not at least doing a broad, light exploration of the knowledge landscape?142

培养良好的通识知识，这样你至少能对整个知识领域有一个大致的“地形图”。阅读广泛的材料。如果你不做广泛而轻松的知识探索，怎么知道自己真正感兴趣什么？142

As a kid, I played historical strategy video games, like Civilization. This shows you how civilization formed. Through the technology tree, you invent different things. You’d invent literacy, democracy, and gunpowder. You start to realize, “Oh wow, there are stages to technology. You can’t have democracy without creating literacy.” There are stages of technology and development of ideas. That’s a helpful framework.143

小时候，我玩历史策略游戏，比如《文明》。这能展示文明是如何形成的。通过科技树，你发明不同的东西。你会发明识字、民主和火药。你开始意识到，“哇，技术是有阶段的。没有识字就没有民主。”技术和思想的发展是分阶段的。这是一个有用的框架。143

It is important to view knowledge as a semantic tree. Make sure you understand the fundamental principles (the trunk and big branches) before you get into the leaves (the details), then there is something for them to hang on to.144

把知识看作一棵语义树很重要。确保你理解基本原理（树干和大枝）后，再进入叶子（细节），这样细节才有依托。144

Some ideas come from reading about a sad trend in technology. When I read about the Concorde being retired, I was like, “Jeez, we don’t even have supersonic transport anymore. That’s terrible.” I never had a chance to fly on the Concorde. That seemed like a terrible thing, so I started reading about it.

有些想法来自于阅读关于技术悲观趋势的文章。当我读到协和飞机退役时，我想，“天哪，我们连超音速交通工具都没有了。这太糟糕了。”我从没机会坐过协和飞机。这似乎是件很糟糕的事，于是我开始研究它。

I learned the Concorde was designed back in the sixties. Aerodynamics have improved a great deal since then, with computational fluid dynamics. Engine efficiencies have improved massively. Even if you just change the engines on the Concorde, you could double the range or thereabouts. I thought, Well, what if we could figure out an efficient design that could make it economically competitive to have a supersonic aircraft? I started looking into it more and did the math on all of it.145

我了解到协和飞机是在六十年代设计的。自那以后，空气动力学有了很大进步，计算流体力学的发展尤为显著。发动机效率也大幅提升。即使只是更换协和飞机的发动机，航程也能翻倍左右。我想，如果我们能设计出一种高效且经济竞争力强的超音速飞机怎么办？我开始深入研究并计算所有相关数据。145

You can get super efficient and super fast when electric aircraft have vertical takeoff and landing, and go supersonic. We could make a breakthrough aircraft several generations beyond what currently exists.146

当电动飞机实现垂直起降并达到超音速时，可以实现超高效率和超高速。我们可以制造出比现有飞机先进好几代的突破性飞机。146

Diving into SpaceX and Tesla, I had to learn how to make hardware. I’d never seen a CNC machine or laid out carbon fiber. I didn’t know any of those things, but if you read books and talk to experts, you can pick them up quickly.147 I started going to the Palo Alto public library to read about rocket engineering and started calling experts, asking to borrow their old engine manuals.148

深入SpaceX和特斯拉，我必须学会制造硬件。我以前从未见过数控机床，也没铺设过碳纤维。我对这些一无所知，但只要读书和与专家交谈，你可以很快掌握。147我开始去帕洛阿尔托公共图书馆阅读火箭工程书籍，并开始联系专家，借阅他们的旧发动机手册。148

Most people self-limit their ability to learn. It’s pretty straightforward—just read books and talk to people.149

大多数人限制了自己的学习能力。这很简单——只要多读书，多与人交流。149

The physics background is helpful as the foundation. But in rocketry I am self-taught, meaning I don’t have an aerospace degree. I just read a lot of books and talked to a lot of people.150

物理学背景是有帮助的基础。但在火箭领域我是自学成才，没有航空航天学位。我只是读了很多书，和很多人交流。150

Talk to people from different walks of life, in different industries, professions, and skills. Try to learn as much as possible. Search for meaning.151

与来自不同生活背景、行业、职业和技能的人交谈。尽量多学东西。寻找意义。151

THE VALUE OF ENGINEERING工程的价值

I spend 80 percent of my time on engineering.152

我将80%的时间花在工程上。152

ENGINEERING IS MAGIC工程即魔法

Engineering is, for all intents and purposes, magic, and who wouldn’t want to be a magician?153

工程在所有实际意义上都是魔法，谁不想成为魔法师呢？153

Q: What drew you to engineering?

问：是什么吸引你进入工程领域？

I have a physics background and grew up in an engineering-centric household. I’m still more an engineer than anything else.154

我有物理学背景，成长于一个以工程为中心的家庭。我仍然更像是一个工程师。154

My dad is an extremely talented electrical and mechanical engineer. We built model airplanes and circuit boards together when I was a kid. He tutored me, and I didn’t even know it at the time.155 I also did things like make model rockets. In South Africa, there were no premade rockets. I had to go to the chemist to get the ingredients for rocket fuel, mix it, and put it in a pipe.156

我爸爸是一位极具天赋的电气和机械工程师。小时候我们一起制作模型飞机和电路板。他辅导我，那时我甚至都没意识到。155我还做过模型火箭。在南非，没有现成的火箭。我得去药店买火箭燃料的原料，自己混合，然后装进管子里。156

There were lots of “engineery” things around me. When I asked for an explanation, I got the true explanation of how things work.157 When I was a little kid, I was really scared of the dark. But I came to understand “dark” just means the absence of photons in the visible wavelength—four hundred to seven hundred nanometers. I thought, well it’s silly to be afraid of a lack of photons. Then I wasn’t afraid of the dark anymore.158

我周围有很多“工程味”的东西。当我询问原因时，我得到了事物运作的真实解释。157小时候我很怕黑，但后来我明白了“黑暗”只是可见波长范围内——四百到七百纳米——光子的缺失。我想，害怕没有光子真是可笑。于是我不再害怕黑暗了。158

I was very technology oriented as a kid growing up in South Africa.159 With our first computer came books to teach yourself programming. This was the coolest thing I’d ever seen. When I was about twelve, I started programming and selling my own games to buy new games. I was hooked. I’d spend money on better computers, Dungeons & Dragons modules, and things like that. I was nerdmaster3000.160

我小时候在南非成长，非常喜欢技术。159我们有了第一台电脑时，附带了自学编程的书籍。这是我见过的最酷的东西。大约十二岁时，我开始编程并出售自己的游戏来买新游戏。我深陷其中。我会花钱买更好的电脑、龙与地下城的模块之类的东西。我是个超级书呆子。160

I wasn’t that much of a loner, but I was quite bookish. I was reading all the time. I would be reading, working on my computer, reading comics, that kind of thing.161

我不算孤僻，但很爱读书。我总是在读书、用电脑、看漫画，诸如此类。161

When I was young, I wasn’t sure what I was going to do when I got older. I thought the idea of inventing things would be cool, because I read a quote from Arthur C. Clarke: “A sufficiently advanced technology is indistinguishable from magic.” That’s really true.

小时候，我不确定长大后要做什么。我觉得发明东西很酷，因为我读过阿瑟·克拉克的一句名言：“足够先进的技术与魔法无异。”这是真的。

If you go back three hundred years, you’d be burned at the stake for things we take for granted today. Being able to fly is crazy. Being able to see over long distances, communicate, and instantly access all the world’s information from almost anywhere on the earth. This would all be considered magic in times past. It actually goes beyond that. Many things we take for granted today weren’t imagined in times past, even in the realm of magic.

如果回到三百年前，今天我们视为理所当然的东西会让人被当作巫术烧死。能飞行简直疯狂。能远距离观察、沟通，并几乎在地球任何地方即时获取全球信息，这些在过去都被视为魔法。实际上，这还不止于此。许多我们今天理所当然的事物，在过去甚至魔法领域都无法想象。

I thought if I could advance technology, that would be like being a magician. That would be really cool.162

我想如果我能推动技术进步，那就像成为魔法师一样。那真是太酷了。162

At one point, I was thinking about doing physics as a career. But to really advance physics these days, you need new data. Physics is fundamentally governed by the progress of engineering.

曾经我考虑过以物理学为职业。但如今要真正推动物理学发展，你需要新的数据。物理学根本上受工程进步的驱动。

There is always debate about, “Which is better, engineers or scientists? Aren’t scientists better? Wasn’t Einstein the smartest person?” Personally, I think engineering is better because without engineering, you do not have any new data. You hit a limit.

总有人争论“工程师和科学家哪个更好？科学家不是更好吗？爱因斯坦不是最聪明的人吗？”我个人认为工程更重要，因为没有工程，就没有新数据。你会遇到瓶颈。

You can be smart within the context of the limits of the data you have, but unless you have a way to get more data, you can’t make progress. Galileo engineered the telescope, which allowed him to see Jupiter had moons. If you want to advance civilization, you must address the limiting factor. The limiting factor is the engineering. Therefore, you must address the engineering.163

你可以在已有数据的限制范围内很聪明，但如果没有获取更多数据的方式，就无法取得进展。伽利略制造了望远镜，这让他发现了木星有卫星。要推动文明进步，必须解决限制因素。限制因素就是工程。因此，必须解决工程问题。163

I certainly admire the discoveries of the great scientists. They’re creating a deeper understanding of how the universe already works. That’s cool—but the universe, in a way, already knows that. They’re discovering what already exists.164

我当然钦佩伟大科学家的发现。他们在加深对宇宙运作方式的理解。这很酷——但宇宙在某种程度上早已知道这些。他们发现的是已存在的东西。164

Science is discovering the essential truths about what exists in the universe.

科学是在发现宇宙中存在的本质真理。

Engineering is about creating things that have never existed before.165

工程是创造前所未有的事物。165

ENGINEERING WINS WARS工程赢得战争

In Sun Tzu’s The Art of War, there is no chapter on technology. It is an interesting book I’ve read many times. It’s packed with wisdom, but there should be a chapter saying: “If you have a decisive technological advantage, you can win with minimal casualties to your side.”166

在孙子兵法中，没有专门论述技术的章节。这是一本我读过多次的有趣书籍，充满智慧，但应该有一章写道：“如果你拥有决定性的技术优势，你可以以最小的己方伤亡获胜。”166

Technology plays a much stronger role in war than is generally understood: technology viewed in the broadest sense, including a better phalanx, or spears made of bronze, iron, or steel. These can be big differences.167

技术在战争中的作用远比人们普遍理解的要强大得多：这里的技术是最广义的，包括更好的方阵，或者由青铜、铁或钢制成的长矛。这些都可能带来巨大差异。167

The Romans won their wars through technology.168

“罗马人通过技术赢得了战争。”168

One of the advantages Romans had was good metallurgy. Their swords were martensitic (an improvement over austenitic metallurgy), so they were stronger. The Romans were often fighting opponents whose swords would basically bend over a Roman sword. If you’re in a sword fight and your sword bends like a noodle…big disadvantage.169

罗马人的优势之一是优秀的冶金技术。他们的剑是马氏体钢（比奥氏体钢更优），因此更坚固。罗马人经常与那些剑会在罗马剑面前弯曲的对手作战。如果你在剑斗中，剑像面条一样弯曲……那是巨大的劣势。169

The Romans were great engineers. Even things like building roads gave them a military advantage. If you’re trying to march an army somewhere fast, roads beat the heck out of a small winding path through the forest.170

罗马人是伟大的工程师。即使是修建道路这样的事情，也给他们带来了军事优势。如果你想快速调动军队，道路远胜过穿越森林的小径。170

But when fighting outside the Roman empire, they sometimes lost their wars because of their opponent’s technology. When the Romans fought the Scythians, they did not have a good counter to mounted war archers, especially if they got lured into flat terrain. They were pretty much helpless against the technology of a mounted archer.171

但在罗马帝国外作战时，他们有时会因为对手的技术而失败。当罗马人与斯基泰人作战时，他们没有有效的对策来应对骑射手，尤其是在被引诱到平坦地形时。他们几乎对骑射手的技术无能为力。171

When there’s a rapid change in the rate of technology, engineering plays a pivotal role.

“当技术变革速度加快时，工程发挥着关键作用。”

If there is a big difference in the technologies—even if the other side has more people, better generals, and is smarter—the side with the advanced technology will win.172

如果技术差距很大——即使对方人数更多、将领更优秀、智力更高——拥有先进技术的一方也会获胜。172

The technology war of fighters and bombers during World War II is fascinating. The US completely crushed it on bombers at the end of the war, but they didn’t start out that way. At the start of World War II in the Pacific, entire US squadrons were sometimes shot down with zero Japanese losses. A total KO. The US fighters at the beginning of World War II were not good either. Their tactics were terrible, the aircraft were terrible, and the training was incorrect, too.173

二战期间战斗机和轰炸机的技术战争非常引人入胜。美国在战争末期完全压倒了轰炸机领域，但起初并非如此。二战初期太平洋战场上，美国整个中队有时会被击落，而日本一方没有损失。完全被击倒。美国战斗机在二战初期表现也不好，战术糟糕，飞机性能差，训练也不正确。173

It’s interesting to see where they started out and how fast things innovated. There was impressive design work by many countries: Japan, the US, Germany, UK, Russia, and others had some impressive fighter designs.174

看到他们的起点以及技术创新的速度非常有趣。许多国家都有令人印象深刻的设计：日本、美国、德国、英国、俄罗斯等都有出色的战斗机设计。174

It was a constant technological rock-paper-scissors game. One country would make a plane, another made a new plane to beat that one, then another country would make an even newer plane. What really matters is the pace of innovation.175

这是一场不断变化的技术“石头剪刀布”游戏。一个国家制造一款飞机，另一个国家制造新飞机来击败它，然后又有国家制造更先进的飞机。真正重要的是创新的速度。175

When the rate of change of technology is high enough, or there is a big technological difference between one side and the other, then that technology dominates and you get a lopsided victory.176

当技术变化速度足够快，或者双方技术差距巨大时，技术将主导战局，带来一边倒的胜利。176

Many books on the strategy of war actually don’t address technology, or do only in a tangential manner. But obviously, if there is an overwhelming technological advantage, that side will win even if the odds are dramatically stacked against them.177

许多关于战争战略的书籍实际上并未涉及技术，或者只是稍作提及。但显然，如果有压倒性的技术优势，即使形势极其不利，该方也会获胜。177

To use an extreme example (a limit case), if you can shoot lasers from space to any spot on the ground by just pointing at it, it would not matter if you’re fighting Julius Caesar, Heinz Guderian, or Napoleon. They just got lasered from space.178

举个极端例子（极限情况），如果你能从太空用激光瞄准地面任何地点射击，那么无论你是与凯撒大帝、海因茨·古德里安还是拿破仑作战，都无关紧要。他们都会被太空激光击中。178

Most battles in history, because technology moved slowly, were more about maneuvering, tactics, and strategy. But when there’s technological discontinuity, it fundamentally changes the whole situation. Wars in the modern era are very much technology race wars. How fast can we create new technology? The best example would be the nuclear bomb. Anyone who made nuclear bombs first, won. That’s it. End of story.179

历史上的大多数战役，由于技术进步缓慢，更多是关于机动、战术和战略。但当出现技术断层时，整个局势会被根本改变。现代战争本质上是技术竞赛。我们能多快创造新技术？最好的例子是核弹。谁先制造出核弹，谁就赢了。就是这么简单。179

That was the reason for the US Manhattan Project. People think it was a government project. I’d like to emphasize that it was a creation of the physics community more than it was the government. The government supported it, but it was a decision and creation from the physics community. Without them, it would not have happened.180

这就是美国曼哈顿计划的原因。人们认为这是一个政府项目，但我想强调的是，它更多是物理学界的创造，而非政府主导。政府支持了它，但这是物理学界的决策和创造。没有他们，这一切不会发生。180

They simply came to the conclusion that they couldn’t let Hitler have the bomb. So they made it first to be certain of it. There isn’t a better example of a super weapon—anyone who gets it wins.181

他们得出的结论是，不能让希特勒拥有核弹。所以他们必须先制造出来以确保领先。没有比这更好的超级武器例子了——谁拥有它，谁就赢。181

Play to win, or don’t play at all.182

“要么全力以赴赢，要么根本不玩。”182

ENGINEERING CREATES VALUE工程创造价值

My mind feels like a wild storm. I have a fountain of ideas. I have more ideas than I could possibly execute. Innovation is not the problem. Execution is the problem.183

我的思绪如同狂风暴雨。我有源源不断的想法，想法多得远远超过我能执行的数量。创新不是问题，执行才是问题。183

There is never a shortage of ideas. I find ideas to be somewhat trivial, but the execution of good ideas is extremely difficult. Prototypes are easy; production is hard. Production and being cash flow positive is excruciating pain. Product ideas are nearly irrelevant.184

想法永远不会短缺。我觉得想法有些微不足道，但把好想法付诸实施极其困难。原型制作很容易，生产才难。生产并实现现金流正向令人痛苦不堪。产品想法几乎无关紧要。184

You only build value in a company if you do hard work to solve tough problems. That’s why companies are valuable. It’s why they should be valuable, and largely why they are.

只有通过艰苦努力解决难题，才能在公司中创造价值。这就是公司有价值的原因，也是它们应当有价值的原因，更是它们大致如此的原因。

Tesla is a hardcore technology company. We do serious engineering. We do real manufacturing as well. We do hardcore manufacturing. Coils of aluminum and plastic pellets go into one end of the factory and cars come out the other. We did all the vehicle engineering, all the powertrain engineering, all the software. The evidence for us solving hard engineering problems is that Toyota, Daimler, and Mercedes buy electric powertrains from us. If it was easy, they would do it.185

特斯拉是一家硬核科技公司。我们做严肃的工程工作，也做真正的制造。我们做硬核制造。铝卷和塑料颗粒从工厂一端进入，汽车从另一端出来。我们完成了所有车辆工程、所有动力总成工程和所有软件开发。我们解决复杂工程问题的证据是丰田、戴姆勒和梅赛德斯都从我们这里购买电动动力总成。如果这很容易，他们自己会做。185

The idea of going to Mars is not hard; that’s irrelevant.

“去火星的想法并不难；那无关紧要。”

Getting to Mars is the hard part.

“到达火星才是难点。”

PART II第二部分

Part II: Ultra Hardcore Work

第二部分：超硬核工作

To those who quietly help advance the causes we mutually believe in, knowing advancing the cause is the only reward: thank you.186

致那些默默推动我们共同信仰事业的人，明白推动事业本身就是唯一的回报：谢谢你们。186

WHAT IT TAKES所需条件

If conventional thinking makes your mission impossible, then unconventional thinking is necessary.187

如果传统思维让你的使命变得不可能，那么非常规思维就是必需的。187

TAKE RESPONSIBILITY承担责任

I am CEO of these companies because I feel I’m responsible for them, not because it’s the best thing for my quality of life.188

我之所以成为这些公司的首席执行官，是因为我觉得自己对它们负有责任，而不是因为这对我的生活质量最有利。188

I want to emphasize that sometimes—in fact, most of the time—I get way too much credit or attention for what I do. I’m just the visible element. The reason these companies are successful is because we have extremely talented people at all levels making it happen.189

我想强调的是，有时候——实际上大多数时候——我为自己所做的事情获得了过多的赞誉或关注。我只是那个可见的元素。这些公司之所以成功，是因为我们在各个层级都有极其有才华的人才在推动事情的发生。189

I didn’t expect to be CEO of these big companies. It just sort of happened that way.190 It was never my intent to run Tesla. Running two companies is quite a burden. Sometimes when people think of being a CEO, they imagine granting themselves lots of vacation and doing fun things. It doesn’t work that way. What you actually get as CEO is a distillation of the worst things going on in the company.191

我没想到自己会成为这些大公司的首席执行官。这只是自然而然发生的事。190 我从未打算经营特斯拉。经营两家公司是一种相当沉重的负担。有时候，当人们想到成为首席执行官时，他们会想象自己可以放很多假，做很多有趣的事情。事实并非如此。作为首席执行官，你实际上得到的是公司中所有最糟糕问题的浓缩体现。191

Particularly, if you’re the CEO, you work on all the worst problems in the company. There’s no point in spending time on things that are going right, so you only spend time on things that are going wrong. Specifically, the things other people can’t fix. The most pernicious and painful problems.192

尤其是，如果你是首席执行官，你要处理公司中所有最棘手的问题。没有必要花时间在那些运转良好的事情上，所以你只花时间处理出错的事情。具体来说，是那些别人无法解决的最恶劣、最痛苦的问题。192

Q: How do you manage through failure?

问：你如何应对失败？

Managing through big failures is painful and difficult. It feels terrible. The company looks at me to rally them, so I do. But I feel terrible. Failure is a punch in the gut. Even when you’ve got a lot of smart people working super hard to minimize the probability of failure, it’s still there. And it’s quite significant.193

应对重大失败既痛苦又困难。感觉非常糟糕。公司的人都看着我，希望我能鼓舞他们，所以我会这么做。但我内心感到很难受。失败就像一记重拳击打在胃部。即使你有很多聪明人非常努力地工作以尽量减少失败的可能性，失败依然存在。而且影响相当大。193

Given the options, I prefer to learn from success.194

鉴于各种选择，我更愿意从成功中学习。194

EARN DEEP UNDERSTANDING深入理解

I wanted to know how the universe works and how the economy works. So, I studied physics and economics. You need to be able to bring a lot of people together to create something. It’s difficult to do something as just an individual, especially for a significant technology.195

我想知道宇宙如何运作，经济如何运行。所以，我学习了物理和经济学。你需要能够把很多人聚集在一起创造一些东西。作为个人单独完成一项重要技术是很困难的。195

I was concerned if I didn’t study business I would be forced to work for someone who did, because they would know special things I didn’t know. I didn’t like the sound of that, so I made sure I knew those things, too.196

我担心如果我不学商业，就会被迫为那些学过商业的人工作，因为他们知道我不知道的特殊知识。我不喜欢那种感觉，所以我也确保自己掌握那些知识。196

Physics gave me the basics, a good analytical framework.197 Physics was rigorous, and we learned a lot of math. Later, I was in an advanced securities analysis class in the business school, and they were teaching people matrix math. I realized if you can do physics math, then business math is super easy.198

物理给了我基础，一个良好的分析框架。197物理学很严谨，我们学了很多数学。后来，我在商学院参加了一个高级证券分析课程，他们教的是矩阵数学。我意识到如果你能做物理数学，那么商业数学就非常简单了。198

My goals were to engineer products by having a feel for the physics and to never work for a boss with a business degree.199

“我的目标是通过对物理的理解来设计产品，并且永远不为拥有商业学位的老板工作。”199

One reason SpaceX could move so quickly is I made both the engineering decisions and the spending decisions together in one brain. In most companies, those decisions require at least two different people.

SpaceX能够快速推进的一个原因是我同时做工程决策和开支决策，集中在一个大脑里。在大多数公司，这些决策至少需要两个人。

There’s some engineering guy who’s trying to convince a finance guy that this money should be spent. But the finance guy doesn’t understand engineering, so he can’t tell if this is a good way to spend money or not. Plus, they may not trust each other. I’m making the engineering and spending decisions together with all the information in one place. My brain trusts itself.200

通常会有一个工程师试图说服财务人员花这笔钱，但财务人员不懂工程，所以无法判断这是不是合理的开支。此外，他们可能互不信任。我把工程和开支决策结合在一起，所有信息都集中在一个地方。我的大脑信任自己。200

I’m head engineer, chief designer, and CEO at SpaceX, so I don’t have to cave to some money guy. I encounter CEOs who don’t know the details of their technology, and that’s ridiculous to me.201

我既是SpaceX的首席工程师、首席设计师，也是CEO，所以我不必向某个财务人员低头。我遇到过那些对自己技术细节一无所知的CEO，这让我觉得很荒谬。201

To make the right decisions, you need to understand something at a detailed level.202

“要做出正确的决策，你需要对某件事有详细的理解。”202

SLEEP ON THE FACTORY FLOOR在工厂地板上睡觉

I’ve done many many stretches of one-hundred-hour weeks—true one-hundred-hour weeks, sleeping roughly six hours per day. I would not recommend that. That’s for emergencies, not all the time.

我曾经连续工作过许多百小时周——真正的百小时周，每天大约睡六个小时。我不推荐这样做。这只适用于紧急情况，而不是长期如此。

During difficult times at Tesla I’ve had to do it, and sometimes at the beginning of my earliest startups I did that. I wouldn’t leave the office. I would sleep under my desk and work seven days a week. Sometimes it’s necessary for success, or to avoid failure.203

在特斯拉的艰难时期，我不得不这样做，有时在我最早的创业初期也是如此。我不会离开办公室，会睡在桌子底下，每周工作七天。有时候这是成功所必需的，或者是为了避免失败。203

Tesla is pretty far up there in terms of work ethic, anywhere in the world. The Tesla work ethic is substantially greater than any other large car or manufacturing company in the US, that I’m aware of.204

特斯拉的工作伦理在全球范围内都相当高。我所知道的美国任何大型汽车或制造公司，特斯拉的工作伦理都远远超过它们。204

If there was a crisis situation, I slept on the floor. Most of the time I did not sleep in a conference room because people could not see me in the conference room—I slept on the floor in the factory. Otherwise how would people know? They wouldn’t. Seeing is believing. I slept on the floor outside the conference room so they could see I was there.205

如果遇到危机情况，我就在地板上睡觉。大多数时候我不会睡在会议室里，因为别人看不到我——我睡在工厂的地板上。否则人们怎么会知道呢？他们不会知道。眼见为实。我睡在会议室外的地板上，让他们看到我在那里。205

When the team is being asked to work super hard, I have to be right there with them and they have to see it. If I fall asleep in the middle of the factory floor at four in the morning and wake up four hours later, they see that.
