They are like, “If the CEO is willing to take that level of pain, I can do it too.”206

当团队被要求非常努力工作时，我必须和他们在一起，他们必须看到这一点。如果我凌晨四点在工厂地板上睡着，四小时后醒来，他们会看到。他们会想，“如果CEO愿意承受这样的痛苦，我也能做到。”206

It was a world of hurt. I would wake up smelling like oil and iron filings. It was rough. But I was asking people to really go all out. I can’t expect them to go all out if I’m not doing the same.207

那是一段痛苦的经历。我醒来时身上带着油味和铁屑味。非常艰难。但我要求大家全力以赴。如果我自己不这样做，就不能指望他们全力以赴。207

FRONTLINE LEADERSHIP一线领导力

Think about war. Do you want the general in some ivory tower or on the front lines? The troops fight harder if they see the general on the front lines. Nobody bleeds for the prince in the palace. Get out there on the front line. Show them that you care and that you’re not in some plush office somewhere.208

想想战争。你希望将军待在象牙塔里，还是站在前线？如果士兵们看到将军在前线，他们会更加奋力作战。没有人会为宫殿里的王子流血。走到前线去，向他们展示你关心他们，而不是待在某个舒适的办公室里。208

If they see the general out on the battlefield, the troops are going to be motivated. Wherever Napoleon was, that’s where his armies would be at their best. Even if I don’t do anything but show up, they’ll look at me and know I wasn’t spending all night partying.209

如果他们看到将军在战场上，士兵们会更有动力。无论拿破仑在哪里，他的军队都会发挥出最佳状态。即使我什么都不做，只是出现，他们也会看着我，知道我没有整晚去狂欢。209

Never ask your troops to do something you’re not willing to do.

永远不要让你的部队做你自己不愿意做的事。

Whatever the people at the front lines are doing, I try to do it at least a few times myself.210

无论前线的人们在做什么，我都会至少亲自做几次。210

It is my firm belief that the separation of the workplace into “executives” and “employees” does not create a good working environment. We want to create a system of equality without artificial barriers, so someone can start as a trainee and one day lead the entire company.

我坚信，将职场划分为“高管”和“员工”并不能创造良好的工作环境。我们希望建立一个没有人为障碍的平等体系，让有人可以从实习生起步，最终领导整个公司。

This is why we eliminate all special privileges of executives. Everyone has equal access to parking, eating at the same tables, and there are no management offices. I am convinced that managers should work at the forefront, in the same work environment as the entire team. Even though I run a company, I still do not have my own office and often move my workplace to the most challenging area in the factory.

这就是为什么我们取消所有高管的特殊特权。每个人都平等使用停车位，一起在同一张桌子吃饭，没有管理办公室。我坚信管理者应该在最前线工作，与整个团队处于同一工作环境。虽然我经营公司，但我仍没有自己的办公室，经常将工作场所搬到工厂最具挑战性的区域。

Managers should always take care of their team before they take care of themselves. The supervisor is there to serve his team, not the other way round.211

管理者应始终先照顾团队，再照顾自己。主管是为团队服务，而不是相反。211

All technical managers must have hands-on experience. For example, managers of software teams must spend at least 20 percent of their time coding. Solar roof managers must spend time on the roofs doing installations. Otherwise, they are like a cavalry leader who can’t ride a horse or a general who can’t use a sword.212

所有技术经理都必须具备实际操作经验。例如，软件团队经理必须至少花20%的时间编写代码。太阳能屋顶经理必须亲自在屋顶进行安装。否则，他们就像不会骑马的骑兵长或不会用剑的将军。212

Always be smashing your ego. Internalize responsibility. Whether you’re a CEO or any other role, do whatever it takes to succeed.213

不断打破自我。内化责任。无论你是CEO还是其他角色，都要竭尽全力取得成功。213

A major failure mode is a high ego-to-ability ratio. If your ego-to-ability ratio gets too high, then you’ve broken the feedback loop to reality.214

一个主要的失败模式是自我膨胀与能力不匹配。如果你的自我膨胀远超能力，就断裂了与现实的反馈循环。214

In AI terms, you’ll break your reinforcement learning (RL) loop. You want to have a strong RL loop, which means internalizing responsibility and minimizing ego.215

用人工智能的说法，你会破坏你的强化学习（RL）循环。你需要有强大的RL循环，也就是内化责任并最小化自我。215

Do whatever the task is, whether grand or humble.216

无论任务多大或多小，都要全力以赴。216

ADVERSITY FORGES STRENGTH逆境锻造力量

There is a great quote from Winston Churchill: “If you’re going through hell, keep going.”217

温斯顿·丘吉尔有一句名言：“如果你正在经历地狱，那就继续前行。”217

Q: Do you think overcoming challenges is something innate in your personality?

问：你认为克服挑战是你性格中与生俱来的特质吗？

I was driven as a kid, very willful. One story I remember: At age six, I was grounded for some reason. I don’t remember why, but I felt it was unjust. I really wanted to go to my cousin’s birthday party.218 I escaped from my nanny and walked across the city.

我小时候很有冲劲，非常倔强。我记得一个故事：六岁时，我因为某个原因被罚禁足。我不记得为什么，但我觉得很不公平。我非常想去我表弟的生日派对。218我逃离了保姆，独自穿过了整个城市。

I was just learning to read, so I could barely read the road signs. This was foolish, and something terrible could have happened. But I was so determined I walked clear across the capital city of South Africa at six years old.219

我刚学会阅读，路标几乎看不懂。这很愚蠢，也可能发生可怕的事情。但我非常坚定，六岁时就走遍了南非的首都。219

It was ten or twelve miles, much farther than I realized, and it took me about four hours. As my mom was leaving the party with my brother and sister, she saw me walking down the road and freaked out. So I climbed a tree and refused to come down until she promised not to punish me.220

那大约有十到十二英里，比我想象的远得多，花了我大约四个小时。当我妈妈带着我哥哥和姐姐离开派对时，她看到我走在路上，吓坏了。于是我爬上一棵树，拒绝下来，直到她答应不惩罚我。220

I seem to have a high, innate drive, and that’s been true even since I was a little kid.

我似乎有很强的天生驱动力，这从我小时候起就一直如此。

When I was five or six, I thought I was insane. It became clear other people’s minds weren’t exploding with ideas all the time. I felt strange. It’s hard to turn it off. It might sound great when it is on. But what if it doesn’t turn off? It’s like a never-ending explosion.221

五六岁时，我觉得自己疯了。很明显，别人的脑子并不是一直充满了各种想法。我觉得自己很奇怪。很难关闭这种状态。它开启时听起来很棒，但如果关不掉呢？这就像永无止境的爆炸。221

I was the youngest kid in my grade, so I was quite small. I was kind of a smart aleck, too. It was a recipe for disaster. I’d get called every name in the book and beaten up. Being small and having violent bullies is a bad situation.222

我在年级里是最小的孩子，所以个头很小。我也有点自作聪明。这是灾难的配方。我被叫各种难听的名字，还被打。个头小又遭遇暴力欺凌，情况很糟糕。222

There was a level of violence in my childhood that wouldn’t be tolerated in any American school. It was like Lord of the Flies. There were a couple of gangs that were pretty evil, and I was one of their chosen victims. Partly because I stuck up for another kid they were relentless on, that made me a target.223

我童年经历的暴力程度，在任何美国学校都无法容忍。那简直像《蝇王》里的情景。有几个帮派非常邪恶，而我成了他们选中的受害者。部分原因是我为另一个被他们无情欺负的孩子挺身而出，这让我成了目标。223

The place where I grew up in South Africa was very violent. Fighting was normal, it was part of the culture.224 It’ll certainly toughen you up; that’s for sure. I was in a lot of fights I didn’t want to be in. I got beaten up really badly in a few of them. I was in real hardcore street fights, from about six to sixteen.225

我在南非长大的地方非常暴力。打架很常见，是文化的一部分。224这肯定会让你变得坚强；这点毋庸置疑。我参加了很多我不想参与的打架。有几次我被打得很惨。我从六岁到十六岁经历了真正的街头恶斗。225

I was almost beaten to death—within an inch of my life at one point.226

我差点被打死——有一次几乎命悬一线。226

I got to a reasonably good size around fifteen, and around sixteen they stopped trying to beat me up, because it didn’t work out so well for them anymore.227

十五岁左右我长得还算壮实，十六岁时他们不再试图打我了，因为对他们来说效果不佳了。227

People say they’re worried about the damage of words. People worried about words have never been punched in the face. Once you’ve been punched in the face, real hard, right on the nose—you’ll take any words over that.228

有人说他们担心言语的伤害。担心言语伤害的人从没被人狠狠地打过脸。一旦你被人狠狠地打在鼻子上，你会宁愿接受任何言语伤害。228

Adversity shaped me. My pain threshold became very high.229

逆境塑造了我。我的痛阈变得非常高。229

EAT GLASS AND STARE INTO THE ABYSS吃玻璃，凝视深渊

When starting a company, usually the beginning is fun.

创业初期，通常是充满乐趣的。

Then, it’s hellish for a number of years.230

然后，接下来几年简直像地狱一样。230

You have to be focused on the short term and money coming in when creating a company, because otherwise the company will die. People think, “Oh, creating a company will be fun.” It’s not. There are periods of fun, and there are periods where it’s just awful.

创建公司时，你必须专注于短期目标和现金流入，否则公司会死掉。人们以为“创业会很有趣”，其实并非如此。有快乐的时刻，也有非常糟糕的时期。

You have to feel quite compelled to start a company. You must have a high pain threshold. My friend Bill Lee says, “Starting a company is like eating glass and staring into the abyss.” There’s some truth to that.231

你必须有强烈的动力去创业。你需要有很高的痛苦承受能力。我的朋友比尔·李说：“创业就像吃玻璃，凝视深渊。”这话有一定道理。231

“Staring into the abyss’” means you’re going to be constantly facing the extermination of the company. Most startups fail. It’s like 90 percent—it could be 99 percent of startups fail. You’re constantly saying, “If I don’t get this right, the company will die.” This can be quite stressful.232

“凝视深渊”意味着你将不断面对公司被淘汰的风险。大多数初创公司都会失败，比例大约是90%，甚至可能高达99%。你不断告诉自己，“如果我做不好，公司就会死。”这会非常有压力。232

“Eating glass” means you’ve got to work on the problems the company needs you to work on, not the problems you want to work on. You end up working on problems you wish you weren’t working on. That’s “eating glass,” and it goes on for a long time.233

“吃玻璃”意味着你必须处理公司需要你解决的问题，而不是你想解决的问题。你最终会处理那些你希望自己不用处理的问题。这就是“吃玻璃”，而且这种状态会持续很长时间。233

We were facing imminent death at SpaceX, Tesla, and SolarCity for a long time. We’re no longer staring directly into the abyss, which is great. It is there in the distance, but I’m not staring right at it. There’s always going to be some amount of glass that has to be chewed. But it’s less and less as time goes by.234

在SpaceX、特斯拉和SolarCity，我们曾长期面临即将倒闭的危机。现在我们不再直视深渊，这很好。深渊依然存在于远方，但我不再直视它。总会有一些玻璃需要咀嚼，但随着时间推移，这种情况越来越少。234

If you don’t eat the glass, you’re not going to be successful.235

“如果你不吃玻璃，你就不会成功。”235

Q: How do you keep your focus on the big picture when you’re constantly faced with being bankrupt in a month?

问：当你不断面临一个月内破产的危机时，如何保持对大局的关注？

A very small percentage of mental energy is spent on the big picture. You know where you’re generally heading, and the actual path is going to be a zigzag in that direction. You’re trying not to deviate too far from the path you want to be on, but you have to, to some degree.236

只有极少的精神能量用于关注大局。你知道自己大致的方向，实际路径会在那个方向上曲折前进。你尽量不偏离想走的路太远，但在某种程度上不得不偏离。236

Failure is not good. Failure is bad. But if something is important enough, then you do it, even though the risk of failure is high. My advice for somebody who wants to start a company: Bear in mind, the most likely outcome is failure. Reconcile yourself to that strong possibility, and only if you still feel compelled to, do it.237

失败不好，失败是坏事。但如果某件事足够重要，即使失败风险很高，你也会去做。我给想创业的人建议：记住，最可能的结果是失败。接受这一强烈可能性，只有当你仍然感到必须去做时，才去做。237

That said, many people fear starting a company too much. What’s the worst that could happen? You’re not gonna starve to death; you’re not gonna die of exposure—really, what’s the worst that could happen?238

话虽如此，很多人过于害怕创业。最坏的情况是什么？你不会饿死，也不会冻死——真的，最坏的情况是什么？238

BUILDING EXCEPTIONAL TEAMS打造卓越团队

The most important thing is to attract great people. Whether you are creating a company or joining a company, find an amazing group that you really respect.239

最重要的是吸引优秀的人才。无论你是在创建一家公司还是加入一家公司，都要找到一个你真正尊敬的出色团队。239

A GROUP WITH A GOAL有目标的团队

A company is just a bunch of people coming together to create a product or service. There’s no such thing as “a business,” just a group pursuing a goal.240

公司不过是一群人聚在一起创造产品或服务。不存在“一个企业”这种东西，只有一个追求目标的团队。240

How talented and hardworking that group is, and the degree to which they are focused cohesively in a good direction, will determine the success of the company. If you’re creating a company, or if you’re joining a company, the most important thing is to attract great people.241

这个团队的才华和勤奋程度，以及他们是否凝聚一致朝着正确方向努力，将决定公司的成功。如果你正在创建一家公司，或者加入一家公司，最重要的是吸引优秀的人才。241

A company is essentially a cybernetic collective of people and machines. This collective is far smarter than an individual.242 That’s what a company is, and there are different levels of complexity in the way companies are formed.243

公司本质上是由人和机器组成的控制论集体。这个集体远比个人聪明。242这就是公司的本质，而公司形成的方式有不同的复杂层次。243

Obviously, one person can’t build a whole rocket, engines, launchpad, and everything. That’s impossible. We have thousands of people at SpaceX. By piecing it out to different people and using our collection of computers, tools, and machines we can make lots of rockets, launch them into orbit, dock with the International Space Station, and more.244

显然，一个人不可能建造整枚火箭、发动机、发射台和所有设备。这是不可能的。SpaceX 有成千上万的人。通过将任务分配给不同的人，并利用我们的计算机、工具和机器集合，我们能够制造许多火箭，将它们发射入轨道，与国际空间站对接，等等。244

People sometimes forget a company is just a group of people gathered together to make products for fellow human beings.

人们有时会忘记，公司不过是一群人聚集在一起为其他人类制造产品。

As long as they make great products, the company will have great value.245

只要他们制造出优秀的产品，公司就会拥有巨大的价值。245

To create a company, you have to convince others to join you in your effort. You have to convince them there is a reasonable chance of success and if there is success, the reward will be commensurate with the effort.

要创建一家公司，你必须说服他人加入你的努力。你必须让他们相信成功的可能性是合理的，并且如果成功，回报将与付出相称。

If you’re going to create a company, the first thing you should try to do is create a working prototype.246

如果你要创建一家公司，首先应该做的是打造一个可行的原型。246

When starting a company, create a demonstration, a mock-up, or a sketch. This helps people envision it. Try to get to that point as soon as possible, then iterate to make it as real as possible as fast as possible.247

创业时，制作一个演示、模型或草图。这有助于人们形成直观印象。尽快达到这个阶段，然后快速迭代，使其尽可能真实。247

It doesn’t sink in for people until you actually have a physical object they can use. Even when you can show something works on paper, and the calculations are clear, it’s not the same.248

直到你真正拥有一个人们可以使用的实物，大家才会真正理解。即使你能在纸上展示某物可行，计算也清晰，但效果还是不同。248

Anything can look good on PowerPoint. If you have an actual demonstration, even in primitive form, it is much more effective in convincing people.249

任何东西放在PPT上都能看起来不错。如果你有一个实际的演示，即使是原始形式，也更能说服人们。249

Fundamentally, if you don’t have a compelling product at a compelling price, you don’t have a great company.250

根本上讲，如果你没有一个有吸引力的产品和有竞争力的价格，你就没有一家伟大的公司。250

CREATE A CULTURE OF BUILDERS营造建设者文化

I consider one of my core responsibilities as CEO is to have an environment where great engineers can flourish.251

我认为作为首席执行官，我的核心职责之一就是营造一个优秀工程师能够茁壮成长的环境。251

I don’t think I manage smart people; they manage themselves. If someone is smart and talented, they can go anywhere and do anything, anytime.252

我不认为我是在管理聪明人；他们管理自己。如果一个人聪明且有才华，他们随时都能去任何地方做任何事。252

I say, “Look, this is the goal we’re after. Do you agree with this goal? If you do, then let’s try to get it done.”

我会说，“看，这是我们追求的目标。你同意这个目标吗？如果同意，那我们就努力去实现它。”

I’ll provide my opinion along the way. But it’s rare for me to actually insist on a particular thing. Once in a while I’ll say, “You have to trust me on this one. We have to do this, and if it turns out to be a bad decision, we can all hold it against me in the future.”253

我会在过程中提供我的意见。但我很少坚持某个特定的事情。偶尔我会说，“这次你们得信我。我们必须这么做，如果结果是个错误决定，未来大家都可以怪我。”253

Another important principle: You want everyone to be able to think like the chief engineer. They need to understand the system at a high level, well enough to know when they are making a bad optimization.254

另一个重要原则是：你希望每个人都能像总工程师一样思考。他们需要对系统有较高层次的理解，足以知道自己何时做出了错误的优化。254

Since a company is a group of people assembled to create a product or service, the ability to attract and motivate great people is critical to the success of a company. That’s the purpose of a company. People sometimes forget this elementary truth.

既然公司是为创造产品或服务而组建的一群人，吸引和激励优秀人才的能力对公司的成功至关重要。这就是公司的目的。人们有时会忘记这个基本真理。

If you’re able to get talented, hard-working people to join the company, work together, and have a relentless sense of perfection toward a common goal, you will end up with a great product. If you have a great product, lots of people will buy it, and the company will be successful.255

如果你能让有才华、勤奋的人加入公司，共同努力，并对共同目标保持不懈的完美追求，你最终会拥有一款出色的产品。拥有好产品，很多人会购买，公司也会成功。255

That’s why our most important consideration is recruiting the best people. The output of any company is the vector sum of the people within it. If we attract the most talented people over time and our direction is correctly aligned, we will prevail.256

这就是为什么我们最重要的考虑是招聘最优秀的人才。任何公司的产出都是其内部人员向量的总和。如果我们随着时间吸引最有才华的人才，并且方向正确一致，我们就会取得胜利。256

A small group of technically strong people will always beat a large group of moderately strong people.257

一小群技术过硬的人，总能战胜一大群中等水平的人。257

What you see at SpaceX is the result of an incredible team. Supertalented people who work like crazy to advance technology. They make this happen. My role is to make sure they have an environment where their talents can come to the fore. I can’t tell you how honored and grateful I am to work with such a great team.258

你在SpaceX看到的是一支令人难以置信的团队成果。超级有才华的人们拼命工作推动技术进步。他们让这一切成为可能。我的角色是确保他们拥有一个能够发挥才华的环境。我无法表达与这样一支伟大团队共事的荣幸和感激。258

Do everything you can to gather great people.259

尽你所能去聚集优秀人才。259

Many companies suppress their talented, driven engineers. Some are suppressed by being so comfortable they don’t produce much. There are a few places in Silicon Valley with good engineers—but what are they producing? The output of that engineering talent seems low, though maybe they are enjoying themselves.

许多公司压制了他们有才华、积极进取的工程师。有些人被过于舒适的环境压制，导致产出不多。硅谷有几个地方有不错的工程师——但他们产出了什么？这些工程人才的产出似乎很低，虽然他们可能很享受其中。

Tesla is not like that. We’re demanding. You’re going to get a lot done and it’s going to be cool work, but it’s not going to be easy. At Tesla, a superb engineer’s talents are used to a greater degree than anywhere else.260

特斯拉不是那样。我们要求严格。你会完成很多工作，而且是很酷的工作，但不会轻松。在特斯拉，顶尖工程师的才华被发挥到比任何地方都更大的程度。260

Tesla has been like a whole series of startups. So far almost all have been quite successful, so we must be doing something right.261

特斯拉就像一系列创业公司。到目前为止几乎所有都相当成功，所以我们一定做对了些什么。261

Only exceptional performance constitutes a passing grade.262

只有卓越的表现才算及格。262

RECRUIT FOR EXCEPTIONAL ABILITY招聘卓越人才

When hiring, I look for evidence of exceptional ability, or at least exceptional aspiration.

招聘时，我寻找卓越能力的证据，或者至少是卓越的志向。

Sometimes these things get messed up in recruiting. I sometimes wonder, “If Nikola Tesla applied to Tesla, would we even give him an interview?” It’s not clear. They might say, “This guy came from some weird college in Eastern Europe, he’s got some odd mannerisms, we don’t know if we should give him an interview.” I worry that’s what we’d do. Our response should be, “Man, Nikola Tesla, this kid’s super smart. What does he want? We’ll pay him anything!”263

有时候招聘过程中会出现混乱。我有时会想，“如果尼古拉·特斯拉申请特斯拉公司，我们会给他面试机会吗？”这并不确定。他们可能会说，“这家伙来自东欧某个奇怪的大学，举止怪异，我们不知道是否该给他面试机会。”我担心我们会这样做。我们的反应应该是，“伙计，尼古拉·特斯拉，这孩子非常聪明。他想要什么？我们愿意付出任何代价！”263

I always ask my teams to give a lot of thought to who should join. I recommend paying close attention to people who haven’t completed their grad or even undergrad, but are obviously brilliant. Better to have them join before they achieve a breakthrough.264

我总是要求我的团队认真考虑应该让谁加入。我建议特别关注那些还没完成研究生甚至本科教育，但显然非常聪明的人。最好让他们在取得突破之前加入。264

You’ve got to be willing to recruit hard for excellent people. When I was interviewing Bülent Altan for SpaceX, I told him, “I heard you don’t want to move to Los Angeles because your wife works for Google in San Francisco. Well, I just talked to Larry Page, and they’re going to transfer your wife down to LA. So what are you going to do now?” He said he would come work for SpaceX.265

你必须愿意为优秀人才努力招聘。当我面试SpaceX的布伦特·阿尔坦时，我告诉他，“我听说你不想搬到洛杉矶，因为你妻子在旧金山的谷歌工作。好吧，我刚和拉里·佩奇谈过，他们会把你妻子调到洛杉矶。那么你现在打算怎么办？”他说他会来SpaceX工作。265

I have a great ability to tell if someone is a good engineer or not. And I am good at optimizing the efficiency of an engineering team. It helps that I’m quite good at engineering in general.266

我很擅长判断一个人是否是优秀的工程师。我也擅长优化工程团队的效率。这得益于我整体上对工程的良好理解。266

In interviews, I ask people to tell me the story of their career and some of the tougher problems they dealt with, how they dealt with those, and how they made decisions at key transition points. Usually that’s enough for me to get a good gut feeling about someone. What I’m really looking for is evidence of exceptional ability. Did they face difficult problems and overcome them?

在面试中，我会让应聘者讲述他们的职业经历以及他们处理过的一些棘手问题，如何应对这些问题，以及在关键转折点如何做出决策。通常，这足以让我对某人有一个良好的直觉判断。我真正寻找的是卓越能力的证据。他们是否面对过困难的问题并克服了它们？

Usually the person who had to struggle with the problem really understands it and they don’t forget if it was difficult. Ask them detailed questions about it, and they’ll know the answers. A person who was not responsible for that accomplishment will not know the details.267

通常，真正为解决问题而努力的人会真正理解问题，如果问题困难，他们不会忘记。问他们详细的问题，他们会知道答案。没有负责完成该成就的人不会知道细节。267

RETAIN ONLY SPECIAL FORCES只保留特种部队

I worked very hard to collect the right expertise when starting SpaceX. I tried hard to find a great chief engineer for the rocket, but good chief engineers wouldn’t join and there is no point in hiring the bad ones. I ended up being chief engineer of the rocket. If I could have found somebody better, maybe we would have had fewer than three failures.268

我在创办SpaceX时非常努力地收集合适的专业知识。我努力寻找一位优秀的火箭总工程师，但优秀的总工程师不愿加入，雇佣不合格的人也毫无意义。最终，我自己成了火箭的总工程师。如果我能找到更好的人，也许我们失败的次数会少于三次。268

Wherever the smartest, most driven people are choosing to work, that company is going to win.269

“无论最聪明、最有干劲的人选择在哪里工作，那家公司就会赢。”269

My philosophy for companies in the startup phase is a “Special Forces” approach. The minimum passing grade is excellent. I believe that’s the culture companies need to have if they’re going to become successful.270

我对初创阶段公司的理念是“特种部队”策略。最低及格标准是卓越。我相信这是公司若想成功必须具备的文化。270

Q: Is building great teams and products just a question of being well funded?

问：组建优秀团队和产品只是资金充足的问题吗？

It’s not like if we just had more money we could spend it effectively on R&D. That’s not how it works. If there was a factory producing excellent engineers, that would be true. Except this factory doesn’t exist. It’s incredibly difficult to find the right talent, integrate them into an organization, and have it work effectively. Money is not the constraint.

并不是说我们有更多钱就能有效地花在研发上。事情不是那样运作的。如果有一个生产优秀工程师的工厂，那倒是真的。但这样的工厂并不存在。找到合适的人才、将他们融入组织并高效运作极其困难。资金不是限制因素。

There are only a small number of excellent people, and they’re hard to find. Engineers especially.271 At the end of the day, the competitiveness of any company is a function of the most talented and driven people. That is the team that’s going to win.272

优秀人才数量有限，且难以寻找。尤其是工程师。271归根结底，任何公司的竞争力取决于最有才华和最有干劲的人。这支团队才会赢。272

The fundamental limitation is exceptional engineers. There are not that many.273

“根本的限制是杰出的工程师。他们并不多。”273

Having a strong sense of purpose will attract the very best talent in the world. If the work is enjoyable, the financial rewards are good, and the product will change the world—that’s a pretty powerful set of motivators.274

拥有强烈的使命感会吸引世界上最优秀的人才。如果工作令人愉快，经济回报丰厚，且产品将改变世界——这是一套非常强大的激励因素。274

I’ve made several hiring decisions where I valued intellect over heart and I think that was a mistake. I have tried to adjust accordingly. It matters whether somebody is a good person.275

我曾多次在招聘时更看重智力而非品格，我认为那是个错误。我已尝试做出调整。一个人是否为好人很重要。275

One test for assessing someone’s character is to look at the character of their friends and associates. While people can put up a mask themselves for their character, their friends and associates will not. You can judge a person’s character by their associates and to some degree by their enemies. If evil people hate you, you might be doing something right.276

评估一个人品格的一个方法是观察其朋友和同事的品格。人们可以自己伪装品格，但他们的朋友和同事不会。你可以通过一个人的朋友圈，甚至在某种程度上通过敌人来判断其品格。如果坏人讨厌你，说明你可能做对了什么。276

When hiring, look for people with the right attitude. Skills can be taught. Attitude changes require a brain transplant.

“招聘时，要寻找态度正确的人。技能可以教，态度的改变需要换脑子。”

FEEDBACK OVER FEELINGS反馈重于感受

Physics does not care about hurt feelings. It cares about whether you got the rocket right.277

物理学不在乎受伤的感情。它关心的是你火箭是否做对了。277

All bad news should be given loudly and often. Good news can be said quietly and once.278

所有坏消息都应该大声且频繁地传达。好消息可以轻声且只说一次。278

I give people hardcore feedback, but I try to always focus on the substance of the discussion. I try to criticize the action, not the person. We all make mistakes. What matters is whether a person has a good feedback loop, can seek criticism from others, and can improve.279

我给人们严厉的反馈，但我总是尽量关注讨论的实质。我试图批评行为，而不是人。我们都会犯错。重要的是一个人是否有良好的反馈循环，能寻求他人的批评，并能改进。279

It’s not your job to make people on your team love you. In fact, that’s counterproductive. I had a manager who would not fire anyone. I told him, “You can’t tell people they have to get their shit together, and when they don’t get their shit together—nothing happens to them.”

让团队成员爱你不是你的工作。事实上，那是适得其反的。我曾有个经理不肯解雇任何人。我告诉他，“你不能告诉别人必须振作起来，而当他们没振作起来时——什么事都不发生。”

Camaraderie is dangerous. It makes it hard for people to challenge each other’s work. There is a tendency to not want to throw a colleague under the bus. That needs to be avoided.

同志情谊是危险的。它让人们难以挑战彼此的工作。人们倾向于不想把同事推向风口浪尖。这种情况必须避免。

Wanting to be everyone’s friend leads you to care too much about the emotions of the individual in front of you rather than caring about the success of the whole enterprise. Focusing on that one individual can lead to a far greater number of people being hurt.280

想成为每个人的朋友会让你过于在意眼前个体的情绪，而不是关心整个企业的成功。专注于那个个体可能导致更多人受到伤害。280

I think it’s a real weakness to want to be liked. A real weakness.

我认为渴望被喜欢是一个真正的弱点。一个真正的弱点。

And I do not have that.281

而我没有那种弱点。281

DESIGNING THE ORGANIZATION组织设计

It must be okay for people to talk directly and make the right thing happen.282

人们可以直接交流并促成正确的事情发生，这是必须被允许的。282

REMOVE ORGANIZATIONAL BOUNDARIES打破组织边界

In any product, you can see the errors in the organization’s structure. They will manifest themselves in the product.

在任何产品中，你都能看到组织结构中的错误。这些错误会在产品中显现出来。

A major source of issues is poor communication between departments. The way to solve this is to allow free flow of information between all levels.

问题的一个主要来源是部门之间沟通不畅。解决方法是允许信息在各个层级之间自由流动。

There are two schools of thought about how information should flow within companies. The most common way is chain of command. That means you always flow communication through your manager. The problem with this approach is that, while it serves to enhance the power of the manager, it fails to serve the company.

关于信息在公司内部如何流动，有两种思路。最常见的是指挥链方式。这意味着你总是通过你的经理传递沟通。这个方法的问题在于，虽然它增强了经理的权力，但却未能服务于公司整体。

If, in order to get something done between departments, an individual contributor has to talk to their manager, who talks to a director, who talks to a VP, who talks to another VP, who talks to a director, who talks to a manager, who talks to someone doing the actual work, then superdumb things will happen. Then the info has to flow back the other way again. This is incredibly dumb.

如果为了完成跨部门的工作，个别贡献者必须先与自己的经理沟通，经理再与主管沟通，主管再与副总裁沟通，副总裁再与另一位副总裁沟通，另一位副总裁再与主管沟通，主管再与经理沟通，经理再与实际执行工作的人沟通，那么就会发生非常愚蠢的事情。然后信息还得再反方向传回去。这非常愚蠢。

Problems get solved quickly when a person just talks to a person in another department and makes the right thing happen. Anyone can and should talk to anyone else according to what they think is the fastest way to solve a problem for the benefit of the whole company. You can talk to your manager’s manager without his permission. You can talk directly to a VP in another department. You can talk to me.

当一个人直接与另一个部门的人沟通并促成正确的事情发生时，问题就能迅速解决。任何人都可以且应该根据他们认为解决问题最快的方法，与任何人沟通，为整个公司的利益着想。你可以在没有经理许可的情况下与经理的上级沟通。你可以直接与另一个部门的副总裁沟通。你也可以直接和我沟通。

Communication should travel via the shortest path necessary to get the job done, not through the “chain of command.”

沟通应沿着完成工作所需的最短路径进行，而不是通过“指挥链”。

Any manager who attempts to enforce “chain of command” communication will soon find themselves working elsewhere.283

任何试图强制执行“指挥链”沟通的经理，很快就会发现自己在别处工作。283

You can talk to anyone without anyone else’s permission. Moreover, you should consider yourself obligated to do so until the right thing happens.

你可以在不经任何人许可的情况下与任何人沟通。此外，你应该认为自己有义务这样做，直到正确的事情发生。

Always view yourself as working for the good of the company and never your department.284

始终把自己看作是为公司的利益工作，而不是仅仅为你的部门工作。284

How can it possibly help Tesla for departments to erect barriers between themselves or see their success as relative within the company instead of collective? We are all in the same boat.

部门之间设立障碍，或者把成功看作是相对的而非集体的，这怎么可能对特斯拉有帮助？我们都在同一条船上。

Managers should work hard to ensure they are not creating silos within the company that create an us-versus-them mentality or impede communication in any way. This is a natural tendency and needs to be actively fought.285

经理们应努力确保他们不会在公司内部制造孤岛，导致“我们对他们”的心态或以任何方式阻碍沟通。这是一种自然倾向，需要积极抵制。285

The point is ensuring we execute ultrafast and well.286

关键是确保我们执行得超快且出色。286

Q: How do boundaries between departments affect product development?

问：部门之间的边界如何影响产品开发？

You can see the organizational boundaries in the product. You’ll often get a box in a box. You realize, “Why is this thing in two boxes?” Turns out, because both teams thought they needed an enclosure, the product ends up with an enclosure in an enclosure.

你可以在产品中看到组织边界。你经常会看到盒中套盒。你会想，“为什么这个东西有两个盒子？”原来是因为两个团队都认为需要一个外壳，结果产品就成了套壳中的套壳。

One case from the Tesla Model 3: The battery pack had a top enclosure and the car also has an underbody, right above it. What’s the point of that? That doesn’t make any sense, but because one team enclosed the battery pack and another team wanted to have an enclosed body, it happened.

以特斯拉Model 3为例：电池组有一个顶部外壳，车身底部正好在它上面。那有什么意义呢？这没有任何道理，但因为一个团队给电池组加了外壳，另一个团队想要封闭的车身，结果就这样了。

It makes sense from an individual team’s perspective, but you don’t need a cover on the battery—there’s a car on top of it. Putting the cover on the battery pack adds mass and cost, so it should be deleted.

从单个团队的角度看这是合理的，但你不需要给电池加盖——上面已经有车身了。给电池组加盖会增加重量和成本，所以应该去掉。

Find the design necessity of every part and every process.287

找出每个零件和每个工艺的设计必要性。287

Occasionally something seems necessary at the design level but turns out unnecessary in manufacturing. Connect designers and manufacturers to make sure they communicate often. The people on the assembly line should be able to immediately grab a designer or engineer and say, “WHY DID YOU MAKE IT THIS WAY!?’”

有时某些东西在设计层面看似必要，但在制造中却没必要。要让设计师和制造商保持频繁沟通。装配线上的人员应该能立即找到设计师或工程师，问：“你为什么要这样设计！？”

If your hand is on a stove and it gets hot, you pull it right off. But if it’s someone else’s hand on the stove, it will take you longer to do something about it.288

如果你的手放在炉子上变热了，你会马上缩回去。但如果是别人的手放在炉子上，你可能会花更长时间去处理。288

You cannot separate design, engineering, and manufacturing. They need to be together because you are going to make mistakes. You want to identify and fix those mistakes today, right now. And if you separate them, the mistakes will fester. Let the manufacturers put the designers’ hands on the stove too.289

你不能把设计、工程和制造分开。它们需要在一起，因为你会犯错。你希望今天、现在就发现并修正这些错误。如果分开，错误会恶化。让制造商也把设计师的手放在炉子上吧。289

Whenever there are problems to solve, don’t just meet with your managers. Do a skip level, where you meet with the level right below your managers.

每当有问题需要解决时，不要只和你的经理开会。要跳过一个层级，和经理下一级的人开会。

Physically go to where the problem is, immediately.290

立刻亲自去问题发生的地方。290

One of my rules is “Go as close to the source as possible.”

我的一条规则是“尽可能靠近问题源头”。

We were trying to determine how thick Starship’s walls should be. Rather than only talking to the company’s executives, I talked to some of the workers actually doing the welding. I asked what they thought was safe. The line workers thought the tank walls could get as thin as 4.8 millimeters.

我们当时在确定星舰的壁厚。除了和公司高管沟通，我还和一些实际焊接的工人聊了聊。我问他们觉得什么厚度安全。线上的工人认为罐壁可以薄到4.8毫米。

“What about four?” I asked.

“那四毫米呢？”我问。

“That would make us pretty nervous,” the workers replied.

“那会让我们很紧张，”工人们回答。

“Okay,” I said, “Let’s try four millimeters.”

“好吧，”我说，“那我们试试四毫米。”

It worked.291

结果成功了。291

SIMPLE COMMUNICATION简单沟通

Don’t use acronyms or nonsense words for objects, software, or processes. In general, anything that requires an explanation inhibits communication. We don’t want people to have to memorize a glossary just to function.

不要为物体、软件或流程使用缩略词或无意义的词汇。一般来说，任何需要解释的东西都会阻碍沟通。我们不希望人们为了正常工作而必须记住一大堆术语表。

I noticed a creeping tendency to use made-up acronyms at SpaceX and Tesla. Excessive use of made-up acronyms is a significant impediment to communication, and keeping communication clear as we grow is incredibly important.

我注意到在SpaceX和特斯拉有一种逐渐流行起来的自创缩略词的趋势。过度使用自创缩略词严重阻碍沟通，而随着公司发展，保持沟通清晰极为重要。

A few acronyms here and there may not seem so bad, but if a thousand people are making these up, over time the result will be a huge glossary that we have to issue to new employees. No one actually remembers all these acronyms, and people don’t want to seem dumb in a meeting, so they just sit there in ignorance.

偶尔使用几个缩略词似乎没什么大问题，但如果有一千人在不断创造这些缩略词，时间一长，结果就是我们不得不向新员工发放一份庞大的术语表。实际上没人能记住所有这些缩略词，人们也不想在会议上显得愚蠢，所以他们只能默默无知地坐着。

I told people the acronyms needed to stop immediately or I would take drastic action. Unless an acronym is approved by me, it should not enter the SpaceX glossary. If there is an existing acronym that cannot reasonably be justified, it should be eliminated.

我告诉大家必须立即停止使用这些缩略词，否则我将采取严厉措施。除非我批准，否则任何缩略词都不应进入SpaceX的术语表。如果已有的缩略词无法合理解释，也应当被废除。

The key test for an acronym is to ask whether it helps or hurts communication. An acronym that most engineers outside of SpaceX already know, such as GUI (graphical user interface), is fine to use. It is also okay to use a few acronyms or contractions every now and again, assuming I have approved them. For example, we use MVac and M9 instead of Merlin 1C-Vacuum or Merlin 1C-Sea Level. But they need to be kept to a minimum.292

判断一个缩略词的关键是看它是帮助还是妨碍沟通。大多数SpaceX外的工程师已经熟知的缩略词，比如GUI（图形用户界面），可以使用。偶尔使用几个我批准的缩略词或缩写也可以。例如，我们用MVac和M9代替Merlin 1C-Vacuum或Merlin 1C-Sea Level。但必须尽量保持最少。292

The most simple, straightforward, low-ego terms are generally best. You want to close the loop on reality hard.293

“最简单、最直接、最不自负的术语通常是最好的。你要努力让现实闭环。”293

INNOVATION NEEDS PERMISSION TO FAIL创新需要允许失败

Failure is essentially irrelevant unless it is catastrophic.294

失败本质上无关紧要，除非是灾难性的。294

You have to always look at the incentive structure of an organization and ask, “Is that organization properly incentivizing innovation?”295 We’re looking for any possible action that can improve the probability of success, no matter how small. Whether that comes from an intern, me, or anyone else doesn’t matter.296

你必须始终审视一个组织的激励结构，并问：“这个组织是否在正确激励创新？”295 我们在寻找任何可能提高成功概率的行动，无论多么微小。无论是实习生、我，还是其他任何人提出的，都无关紧要。296

When trying new things, you’ve got to have some acceptance of failure…failure must be an option. If failure is not an option, it’s going to result in extremely conservative choices and you may get something even worse than lack of innovation—things may go backward.297

尝试新事物时，你必须接受失败……失败必须是一个选项。如果失败不是选项，就会导致极端保守的选择，甚至可能出现比缺乏创新更糟的情况——事情可能会倒退。297

When we had early failures in the SpaceX flights, I didn’t fire anyone responsible for those particular causes of failure. They could have made better decisions, but they were smart and hardworking. It just wouldn’t have been fair in that situation. Letting people go is only fair if they can’t get themselves motivated around the core mission or they’re really not giving it everything they can.298

当SpaceX早期飞行失败时，我没有解雇那些直接导致失败的人。他们本可以做出更好的决定，但他们聪明且勤奋。在那种情况下解雇他们是不公平的。只有当他们无法围绕核心使命激励自己，或者真的没有全力以赴时，解雇才是公平的。298

It’s important to create an environment that fosters innovation, and you want to let it evolve in a Darwinian way. You don’t want to pick one technology or path and decide that it will win, because it may not be the best option. Let things evolve.299

创造一个促进创新的环境非常重要，你希望让它以达尔文式的方式进化。你不想选择某项技术或路径并决定它必胜，因为它可能不是最佳选择。让事情自然发展。299

With innovation and new technology, you don’t know what the path is—there’s no map. By nature it’s unknown, which means you’re going to make false moves. It must be culturally acceptable to make false moves.300

对于创新和新技术，你不知道路径在哪里——没有地图。它本质上是未知的，这意味着你会犯错。文化上必须接受犯错。300

So to provide support for innovation, make sure the penalty for failure is low. You don’t want the response to failure to be too punitive. That’s one of the keys to Silicon Valley’s success. There are many founders who’ve built successful companies after a previous company failed. They quickly reconstituted. People left and joined other companies. That’s critical.301

为了支持创新，确保失败的惩罚很低。你不希望对失败的反应过于严厉。这是硅谷成功的关键之一。许多创始人在前一家公司失败后建立了成功的公司。他们迅速重组。有人离开，有人加入其他公司。这一点至关重要。301

If you punish people too much for failure, then they will respond accordingly, and the innovation you get will be incremental. Nobody’s going to try anything bold for fear of getting fired or being punished in some way. If you expect innovation, the compensation structure must reflect that. The risk-reward must favor taking bold moves.302

如果你对失败惩罚过重，人们会相应地反应，创新将只是渐进式的。没人会大胆尝试，害怕被解雇或受到某种惩罚。如果你期待创新，薪酬结构必须反映这一点。风险与回报必须倾向于大胆行动。302

Failure is a side effect of iteration. I once told a discouraged engineer, “If you can’t tell me the four ways you fucked something up before you got it right, you weren’t the one doing the real work.”303

失败是迭代的副产品。我曾对一位沮丧的工程师说：“如果你不能告诉我在成功之前你搞砸了哪四次，那你就不是在做真正的工作。”303

If we’re not occasionally blowing up an engine on the test stand, we’re not trying hard enough.304

“如果我们不时在测试台上炸毁发动机，那说明我们还不够努力。”304

Q: Why is it so hard to predict what will work and what won’t?

问：为什么很难预测什么会成功，什么不会？

We do as much as we can on the ground and in simulations, but a lot of factors can’t be simulated. A design might work in simulation or the test stand, but not in a real flight.305 There’s no test stand that can test a rocket at 17,000 mph, doing six Gs in every different orientation. It’s not possible.306

我们尽可能多地在地面和模拟中测试，但许多因素无法模拟。设计可能在模拟或测试台上有效，但在实际飞行中却不行。305 没有测试台能模拟火箭以每小时1.7万英里速度，在各种姿态下承受六倍重力的情况。这是不可能的。306

After we fly a mission, hundreds of changes take place. Not a few. Hundreds. If you go to a detailed level across the ship, booster, and engines, there might be thousands of changes between flights. I’m just talking hardware here; software changes even more.307

飞行任务后，会发生数百项变更。不是几项，而是数百项。如果细看整艘飞船、助推器和发动机，飞行间可能有数千项变更。我这里只说硬件；软件的变更更多。307

Many are small, but a small change could be a big deal. The first issues you find are fundamental design flaws that would make success impossible. Some issues vary. They make success possible sometimes, but not every time. Maybe a design will blow up only once in a while. Things that work only sometimes can throw you off. Just because it worked once does not mean it will work again, because the combination of factors isn’t the same. It requires a lot of flights to figure out which issues cause one in ten failures and which cause one in one hundred failures.308

许多变更很小，但小变更可能影响重大。你首先发现的是根本设计缺陷，会让成功变得不可能。有些问题是变动的。有时它们让成功成为可能，但不是每次都如此。也许设计偶尔会爆炸。偶尔有效的东西会让你迷惑。仅仅因为它曾经成功过一次，并不意味着它会再次成功，因为因素组合不同。需要大量飞行才能分辨哪些问题导致十分之一的失败，哪些导致百分之一的失败。308

You will lose.

你会失败。

It will hurt the first fifty times.

最初五十次都会很痛苦。

When you get used to losing, you will play each game with less emotion.309

当你习惯了失败，你会以更平静的心态面对每一场比赛。309

SIMPLICITY WINS简单取胜

Never use a cruise missile to kill a fly; just use a flyswatter.310

“永远不要用巡航导弹去打苍蝇；只需用苍蝇拍。”310

Every decision we made in early SpaceX designs has been with simplicity in mind. Simplicity both improves reliability and reduces cost. Fewer components means fewer components to buy and fewer components that can go wrong.311

我们在早期SpaceX设计中做的每一个决定，都是以简单为原则。简单既提高了可靠性，也降低了成本。组件越少，采购的组件就越少，出错的可能性也越小。311

The number of lines of code is not a figure of merit. I consider a large number of lines of code to be bad, not good. I would award one point for adding a line of code and two points for deleting a line of code.312

代码行数并不是衡量优劣的标准。我认为代码行数多是坏事，而非好事。增加一行代码我会扣一分，删除一行代码我会加两分。312

In general, always pick common sense as your guide. If following a “company rule” is obviously ridiculous in a particular situation, such that it would make for a great Dilbert cartoon, then the rule should change.313

一般来说，总是以常识为指导。如果遵守某条“公司规定”在特定情况下显得荒谬至极，甚至能成为一则精彩的《迪尔伯特》漫画，那么这条规定就应该被修改。313

Simplicity is our mantra. It creates both reliability and low cost.314

“简单是我们的箴言。它既创造了可靠性，也降低了成本。”314

Q: What are the benefits of simplicity at scale?

问：大规模简化的好处有哪些？

At Tesla, the Model 3 body line in Fremont was only ever meant to make five thousand cars a week, and it’s now doing seven thousand. We achieved that by removing things. We had been doing a lot of foolish things. We’ve changed some of the designs and made them simpler and easier to build. Simplicity comes from hundreds of little changes.

在特斯拉，弗里蒙特的Model 3车身生产线原本只计划每周生产五千辆车，现在却达到了七千辆。我们通过去除不必要的环节实现了这一目标。之前我们做了很多愚蠢的事情。我们改变了一些设计，使其更简单、更易于制造。简单来自于数百个细微的改进。

Being able to get 40 percent more output from the same manufacturing line makes a big difference. It also reduces the marginal cost of production and improves the quality of the car. This is the result of a ton of hard work by a lot of people.315

能够从同一条生产线上多产出40%带来了巨大差异。这也降低了生产的边际成本，提高了汽车的质量。这是许多人辛勤努力的结果。315

For three months I was at the gigafactory trying to help fix battery production. It’s a lot of little things. I look at every tiny part of each process and ask, “Is this process necessary?”

我曾在超级工厂待了三个月，试图帮助解决电池生产问题。这涉及很多细节。我会审视每一个工序的每一个小环节，问：“这个工序有必要吗？”

The best part is no part. The best process is no process.316

“最好的零件是不需要零件。最好的工序是不需要工序。”316

For example, a robot would put a car frame on a turntable. The turntable would rotate and then another robot would pick it up. The problem was the turntable would sometimes break down. So, we eliminated the turntable. With a robot-to-robot handoff, we had one less step, less equipment, and didn’t have turntable breakage to consider. It’s a lot of this really simple stuff, but a thousand times.317

举例来说，一个机器人会把车架放到转盘上，转盘旋转后另一个机器人再接手。问题是转盘有时会坏掉。于是我们取消了转盘。通过机器人之间直接交接，我们减少了一个步骤，减少了设备，也不必考虑转盘故障。这种非常简单的改进有成千上万次。317

It’s a lot of minimizing things that can go wrong and maximizing the efficiency of the simple things.318

“这就是最大限度减少可能出错的环节，最大化简单环节的效率。”318

If you’ve got a whole bunch of separate parts and each of them has a given tolerance—even if that tolerance is tight, like 0.2 millimeter tolerance—but if you’ve got fifty parts…you have to multiply the variances together. You’ll end up with a huge variance between cars. That’s one of the reasons it’s better to combine parts rather than have more individual parts.

如果你有一堆独立零件，每个零件都有一定的公差——即使公差很严格，比如0.2毫米——但如果有五十个零件……你必须将各自的误差叠加起来，最终导致整车间的误差非常大。这也是为什么合并零件比拥有更多单独零件更好的原因之一。

In the Model 3 engineering, there were a lot of right answers to the wrong questions.319

在Model 3的工程设计中，存在许多对错误问题的正确答案。319

Fifty different times for fifty different parts, an engineer would ask, “What’s the best material to make this part out of?” Of course, they would get fifty different answers. They were all true individually, but not true collectively.

五十次不同的场合，针对五十个不同零件，工程师会问：“用什么材料做这个零件最好？”当然，他们会得到五十个不同的答案。每个答案单独看都正确，但整体上并不成立。

When you try to join all these parts made of dissimilar metals, there are many issues. You need better sealant to prevent galvanic corrosion. You’ve got to join some with rivets, some with spot welds, some with resin, or resin and spot welds. Then it looks like a Frankenstein situation all together.320

当你试图将由不同金属制成的零件连接起来时，会遇到很多问题。你需要更好的密封剂来防止电化腐蚀。你必须用铆钉连接一些，用点焊连接一些，用树脂或树脂加点焊连接另一些。整体看起来就像是个“弗兰肯斯坦”拼凑的局面。320

It’s way better to have a single piece, casted. Then you have no gaps, no sealant, no dissimilar metals. You can reduce the size of the body shop in the factory dramatically. Having the rear body cast for Model Y allowed us to reduce the body shop by 30 percent.321

采用单一铸件要好得多。这样就没有缝隙，没有密封剂，没有不同金属。你可以大幅缩小工厂的车身车间规模。Model Y采用后车身铸件，使车身车间缩小了30%。321

There’s roughly a thousand robots on the Model 3 body line. Which, by the way, is not a figure of merit. We got rid of three hundred robots by switching to rear body casting. When we change the front body to casting, we’ll get rid of another three hundred robots. You want fewer things, not more.322

Model 3车身生产线上大约有一千台机器人。顺便说一句，这并不是衡量优劣的标准。通过改用后车身铸件，我们淘汰了三百台机器人。等前车身也改用铸件时，我们还会淘汰另外三百台机器人。你想要的是更少的东西，而不是更多。322

It’s easy to say “simplify,” but it’s very difficult to do it.323

“说‘简化’很容易，但真正做到却非常难。”323

THE ALGORITHM算法

I became a broken record on The Algorithm. But it’s helpful to say it to an annoying degree.

我在谈论算法时简直成了破唱片机。但反复强调这一点确实很有帮助。

I have everyone at my companies rigorously implement a five-step process for engineering. I call it The Algorithm. I’ll list the steps, then explain. The order is very important.

我要求我公司的每个人严格执行一个五步工程流程。我称之为算法。我会先列出步骤，然后解释。顺序非常重要。

Q: Why is the order of the steps so important?

问：为什么步骤的顺序如此重要？

I’ve personally made the mistake of going backward—on all five steps—multiple times. Many things at Tesla were automated, accelerated, simplified, and then deleted.

我个人犯过多次倒着做这五个步骤的错误。特斯拉的许多事情都是先自动化、加速、简化，然后才被删除。

One example was fiberglass mats on top of the battery pack. At one point I was basically living on the battery pack production line, because it was choking the entire car’s production. The first mistake was trying to fix the automation to make the robot better, to make it move faster on a shorter path. We increased the rate by 20 percent, then 100 percent. Then, we tried to optimize the use of glue and the drying speed.

举个例子，电池组顶部的玻璃纤维垫。那时我几乎天天待在电池组生产线上，因为它拖慢了整车的生产。第一个错误是试图修正自动化，让机器人更好、更快地走更短的路径。我们把速度提高了20%，然后是100%。接着，我们尝试优化胶水的使用和干燥速度。

Automating was a mistake, then accelerating was a mistake, then optimizing was a mistake. Finally, I asked the battery safety team, “What the hell are these mats for? Fire protection?”

自动化是个错误，加速是个错误，优化也是错误。最后，我问电池安全团队：“这些垫子到底是干什么的？防火吗？”

They said, “No, they’re for noise and vibration.”

他们说：“不是，是用来减震降噪的。”

I said, “But…you’re the battery team.”

我说：“可是……你们是电池团队啊。”

So I asked the noise vibration analysis team, “What’s the mat for?”

于是我问噪声振动分析团队：“这些垫子是干什么用的？”

They said fire safety. It was like being in a Dilbert cartoon. I feel like I’m in a Dilbert cartoon quite frequently.

他们说是防火安全。这简直像在看《迪尔伯特》漫画。我觉得自己经常像在《迪尔伯特》漫画里。

So we ran a test: a car with and without the fiberglass mats and a microphone in both. No one could tell the difference. So, we deleted the part (the mats), which deleted a step that required two million dollars of robotics, because it was just a big pile of nonsense.324

于是我们做了个测试：一辆车装有玻璃纤维垫，另一辆没有，两车都装了麦克风。没人能听出区别。于是，我们删除了这部分（垫子），也删除了一个需要花费两百万美元机器人设备的工序，因为这完全是一堆废话。324

Q: Can you walk me through the steps?

问：你能给我详细讲讲这些步骤吗？

The first step is to question the requirements, and make your requirements less dumb. You have to start there, because otherwise you could get the perfect answer to the wrong question. This step makes the question the least wrong possible.

第一步是质疑需求，让你的需求不那么愚蠢。必须从这里开始，否则你可能会得到错误问题的完美答案。这一步让问题尽可能少错。

Your requirements are definitely dumb. It does not matter who gave them to you. Requirements from smart people are the most dangerous, because you’re less likely to question them. Always question requirements, even if it came from me. Everyone is wrong some of the time.

你的需求肯定有问题。不管是谁给你的，聪明人给的需求最危险，因为你不太可能质疑它们。无论是谁给的需求，都要质疑。每个人都有犯错的时候。

Whatever requirements or constraints you do have must come from a person, not a department. You can’t actually ask a department. You have to be able to ask a person, and the person putting forth the requirement must take responsibility for that requirement. Otherwise, you could have a requirement made up by an intern two years ago off the cuff, or someone who isn’t even at the company anymore. You must know the name of the real person who made every requirement.

你所有的需求或限制必须来自具体的人，而不是部门。你不能问一个部门，必须能问到具体的人，提出需求的人必须对该需求负责。否则，可能是两年前某个实习生随口说的，或者是已经离职的人。你必须知道每条需求背后真实负责人的名字。

Many times we’ve had dumb requirements, dug into them, and discovered no one currently working in that department agrees with the requirement. These things are often way more silly than you think. So step one is make your requirements less dumb.325

很多时候，我们遇到愚蠢的需求，深入挖掘后发现部门里没人同意这些需求。这些事情往往比你想象的更荒谬。所以第一步是让你的需求不那么愚蠢。325

Step two: Try very hard to delete the part or process. It sounds obvious, but people often forget to try deleting something entirely.

第二步：努力尝试删除这个部件或流程。听起来显而易见，但人们常常忘了尝试彻底删除某些东西。

If you’re not adding deleted things back in 10 percent of the time, you’re clearly not deleting enough. Somewhat illogically, people often feel they’ve succeeded if they are not forced to put anything back in. But actually they have failed in a different way, because they’ve been overly conservative and have left things in there that shouldn’t be.

如果你删除的东西中不到10%需要再加回来，那说明你删得还不够。虽然有点不合逻辑，人们常觉得只要不用把东西加回来就算成功了。其实那是另一种失败，因为他们太保守，留下了不该有的东西。

The bias tends to be very strong for adding this part or process step “just in case we need it.” But you can make “just in case” arguments for many, many, many things.

人们往往倾向于保留某个部件或流程，“以防万一”。但你可以为很多很多东西都找“以防万一”的理由。

People tend to remember, with sometimes a jarring level of pain, when they deleted something they later needed. They overcorrect, and put too much stuff in there, which overcomplicates things.

人们往往记得很痛苦地经历过删掉后来需要的东西，于是过度纠正，放进太多东西，导致过度复杂。

I tell them this in advance: “Look, we’re deliberately going to delete more than we should. Some of the things we delete, we’re going to put back in. At least one in ten things, we’re going to add back in.” People get a little shook by that. But if you’re so conservative in deleting that you never have to put anything back in, you obviously have a lot of stuff that isn’t needed. So, you’ve got to overcorrect for that tendency.

我提前告诉他们：“听着，我们会故意删得比应该的多。删掉的东西中至少有十分之一会加回来。”大家听了会有点震惊。但如果你删得太保守，永远不用加东西回来，说明你有很多不必要的东西。所以你必须对此倾向做出过度纠正。

At SpaceX, I would tell the team, “We are on a deletion rampage!! Nothing is sacred. We will delete any remotely questionable tubes, sensors, manifolds, etc. tonight. Please go ultrahardcore on deletion and simplification.” We put immense effort into reducing mass.

在SpaceX，我会告诉团队：“我们要大规模删除！！没有什么是神圣不可侵犯的。今晚我们会删除任何有疑问的管子、传感器、歧管等。请极端严格地进行删除和简化。”我们投入巨大努力减轻质量。

There’s a recursive factor to mass. If you add an extra ton of heat shielding, you also need more fuel to get it to orbit, and you need more fuel to deorbit, and you need more fuel to land it. Also the structure now has more load on it, because it’s carrying an extra ton of heat shield. This applies to any given ton.

质量有递归效应。如果你多加一吨隔热层，你还需要更多燃料送它入轨，更多燃料脱轨，更多燃料着陆。结构也承受更大负荷，因为它要承载额外的一吨隔热层。任何一吨都是如此。

Adding each ton is almost like adding two tons when it’s fully considered. We’ve calculated it to be around a 1.8 recursion factor, but we’re probably forgetting something that gets us closer to two. Every one ton of mass begets an extra ton. That is why it is very important to delete.326

每增加一吨，实际上相当于增加了近两吨。我们计算的递归因子约为1.8，但可能还有遗漏，接近2。每增加一吨质量，就会引发额外一吨。这就是为什么删除非常重要。326

Recapping so far: Step one, make your requirements less dumb. Step two, try hard to delete the part or process.327

到目前为止总结：第一步，让需求不那么愚蠢。第二步，努力删除部件或流程。327

Then, once you have deleted as much as you can, the third step is to simplify or optimize. The third step. The third step. Not the first step. Why?

然后，当你尽可能多地删除后，第三步是简化或优化。第三步。第三步。不是第一步。为什么？

The most common mistake of smart engineers is to optimize a thing that should not exist.

聪明工程师最常犯的错误是优化本不该存在的东西。

Everyone was trained in high school and college to answer the question in front of them. It’s convergent logic. You can’t tell the professor, “Your question is dumb.” You have to answer the question. Without knowing it, almost everyone has this mental straightjacket on. They’ll work on optimizing a thing that should simply not exist.

每个人在高中和大学都被训练去回答眼前的问题。这是收敛逻辑。你不能告诉教授，“你的问题很蠢”。你必须回答问题。几乎所有人都无意识地戴上了这副精神枷锁。他们会去优化本不该存在的东西。

Then, and only then, step four: accelerate cycle time. Once you’re moving in the right direction, and moving efficiently…you’re moving too slow. Go faster. You can always make things go faster.

然后，只有在那之后，第四步：加快周期时间。一旦你朝正确方向高效前进……你还是太慢了。加快速度。你总能让事情更快。

But, do not go faster until you have worked on the other three things first. I mistakenly spent a lot of time accelerating processes that I later realized should have been deleted. Speeding up something that shouldn’t exist is absurd. If you’re digging your grave, don’t dig it faster. Stop digging.

但在你先做完前三步之前，不要加速。我曾错误地花了很多时间加速后来发现应该删除的流程。加速不该存在的东西是荒谬的。如果你在挖坟墓，别挖快了，停下来。

Step five, the final step, is to automate. The big mistake I made in the Tesla factories in Nevada and Fremont was trying to automate every step too early. To fix that, we had to tear hundreds of expensive robots out of the production line. We put a hole in the side of the building just to remove all that equipment.328

第五步，最后一步，是自动化。我在内华达和弗里蒙特的特斯拉工厂犯的大错是太早尝试自动化每一步。为了解决这个问题，我们不得不拆除生产线上的数百台昂贵机器人。我们甚至在厂房侧面开了个洞来移除这些设备。328

Always wait until the end of designing a process—after you have questioned all the requirements and deleted unnecessary parts—before you introduce automation.329

设计流程时，务必等到最后——在质疑所有需求并删除不必要部分之后——才引入自动化。329

MANIACAL URGENCY狂热的紧迫感

A maniacal sense of urgency is our operating principle.330

狂热的紧迫感是我们的行动准则。330

DON’T WASTE TIME不要浪费时间

Excessive meetings are the blight of big companies and almost always get worse over time. Get rid of all large meetings, unless you’re certain they are providing value to the whole audience, in which case, keep them short.

过多的会议是大公司的祸害，而且几乎总是随着时间推移变得更糟。除非你确定会议对所有参与者都有价值，否则应取消所有大型会议；如果保留，也要保持简短。

Also get rid of frequent meetings, unless you are dealing with an extremely urgent matter. Meeting frequency should drop rapidly once the urgent matter is resolved.

除非你正在处理极其紧急的事项，否则也应取消频繁的会议。一旦紧急事项解决，会议频率应迅速减少。

Walk out of a meeting or drop off a call as soon as it is obvious you aren’t adding value. It is not rude to leave; it is rude to make someone stay and waste their time.

一旦明显你无法为会议或电话提供价值，就应离开会议或挂断电话。离开并不无礼，无礼的是让别人留下来浪费时间。

SPEED IS BOTH OFFENSE AND DEFENSE速度既是进攻也是防守

The only true currency is time.331

唯一真正的货币是时间。331

The best offense and defense is speed. The SR-71 Blackbird is a military plane with almost no defense except acceleration. It was never shot down. Not even once. Over three thousand missiles were shot at the SR-71 Blackbird and none hit. All it did was go faster. The power of speed is underappreciated as a competitive factor.332

最好的进攻和防守就是速度。SR-71“黑鸟”是一种几乎没有防御手段，除了加速的军用飞机。它从未被击落过，一次也没有。三千多枚导弹射向SR-71“黑鸟”，却没有一枚命中。它所做的只是飞得更快。速度的力量作为竞争因素被严重低估了。332

The real way you actually achieve intellectual property (IP) protection is by innovating fast. If your rate of innovation is high, then you don’t need to worry about protecting the IP because other companies will be copying something you did years ago. That’s fine. Just make sure your rate of innovation is fast. Speed of innovation is what matters.333

真正实现知识产权（IP）保护的方式是快速创新。如果你的创新速度很快，那么你不必担心保护知识产权，因为其他公司复制的都是你多年前做过的事情。没关系，只要确保你的创新速度够快。创新的速度才是关键。333

We obviously cannot compete with the big car companies in size, so we must do so with intelligence and agility.334 A factory moving at twice the speed of another factory is basically equivalent to two factories. The company will succeed if it can do with one factory what takes other companies two, three, or four factories. We try to think, “How can we make each factory produce what would normally require five or even ten factories?”335

我们显然无法在规模上与大型汽车公司竞争，所以必须依靠智慧和敏捷。334 一个工厂的运转速度是另一个工厂的两倍，基本上相当于两个工厂。如果一家公司能用一个工厂完成其他公司需要两、三或四个工厂才能完成的工作，那么这家公司就会成功。我们努力思考，“如何让每个工厂的产能相当于五个甚至十个工厂？”335

I have a running triage of what I do at each company, constantly thinking, “What is the most useful thing I could do?”336

我对自己在每家公司所做的事情有一个持续的优先排序，不断思考，“我现在能做的最有用的事情是什么？”336

In early SpaceX, I told the team everything we did was a function of our burn rate. We were burning through a hundred thousand dollars per day. In the same way, I expected the revenue in ten years to be ten million dollars a day. Every day we were slower to achieve our goals was a day of missing out on that revenue.337

在SpaceX早期，我告诉团队我们所做的一切都取决于我们的烧钱速度。我们每天烧掉十万美元。同样，我预计十年后每天的收入将达到一千万美元。每一天我们未能加快实现目标的速度，都是错失那笔收入的一天。337

You need to be a vector, not just a scalar. That means you need to go at high speed in the right direction. No company will always be going in the right direction all the time—so you have to do course corrections, like a guided missile.338

你需要成为一个向量，而不仅仅是一个标量。这意味着你需要以高速朝着正确的方向前进。没有任何公司能一直始终朝着正确方向前进——所以你必须像制导导弹一样进行航向修正。338

I try to work as much as possible—to the edge of sanity. Every good hour or even minute of thinking about Tesla and SpaceX has such a big effect on the company. It makes sleeping difficult.

我尽可能多地工作——达到理智的边缘。每一个关于特斯拉和SpaceX的高质量思考时刻，无论是一小时还是一分钟，对公司都有巨大影响。这让睡觉变得困难。

Tesla is getting to the point where every high-quality minute of thinking has a million-dollar impact. That is insane. If Tesla is doing $2 billion a week in revenue, that’s about $300 million a day, seven days a week. There are many instances where a half-hour meeting improved the company’s financial outcome by $100 million.339

特斯拉正达到这样一个阶段：每一分钟的高质量思考都能带来百万美元的影响。这简直疯狂。如果特斯拉每周收入达20亿美元，约合每天3亿美元，且一周七天营业。有很多情况下，半小时的会议就能让公司的财务结果改善1亿美元。339

DO THINGS IN PARALLEL并行处理事务

Everything is measured in terms of time. What is the time risk associated with something?

一切都以时间来衡量。某件事的时间风险是多少？

The one thing you cannot replace is time.340

你无法替代的唯一东西就是时间。340

For PayPal, we needed to establish a lot of back-office relationships to attach various data sources. The credit card system for processing credit cards, the Federal Reserve System for doing electronic funds transfers, the various fraud databases to run fraud checks. There was a lot we had to interface with, and it took a while to set all that up.

对于PayPal，我们需要建立许多后台关系来连接各种数据源。信用卡系统用于处理信用卡，联邦储备系统用于电子资金转账，各种欺诈数据库用于进行欺诈检查。我们必须对接很多系统，这花费了不少时间来搭建。

But by doing them all in parallel, it all came together simultaneously. Developing the software and having it ready for the general public coincided with us concluding deals with the outside vendors. All that took about a year.341

但通过并行处理，这一切同时完成。软件开发并准备好面向公众发布的时间，恰好与我们与外部供应商达成协议的时间相吻合。整个过程大约花费了一年。341

Avoid serialized dependencies. A lot of things have a “gestation period” and there is nothing you can do to accelerate it. If you can have all those things gestating in parallel, that will substantially accelerate your overall timeline. People tend to serialize too much. Put as many gestating elements in parallel as possible.342

避免串行依赖。很多事情都有“孕育期”，你无法加速它。如果你能让所有这些事情并行孕育，将大大加快整体进度。人们往往过度串行化。尽可能让更多孕育中的元素并行进行。342

If a timeline is long, it’s wrong.343

如果时间线很长，那就是错的。343

BREAK DOWN THE “IMPOSSIBLE”拆解“不可能”

The general principles of first-principle thinking apply to software, hardware, anything really. Often, we were told something was impossible, but once we broke it down into its constituent elements, we could solve those.344

第一性原理思维的通用原则适用于软件、硬件，实际上适用于任何事物。我们经常被告知某件事是不可能的，但一旦我们将其拆解成组成部分，就能解决这些问题。344

For xAI in 2024, we were trying to build a training supercluster (a giant network of computers to train an AI model). We went to the various suppliers and said we needed one hundred thousand H100s (superpowerful computer chips) to be able to train coherently. Their estimates for how long it would take to complete that were eighteen to twenty-four months. Well, we needed to get that done in six months or we wouldn’t be competitive.

对于2024年的xAI，我们试图构建一个训练超级集群（一个用于训练AI模型的庞大计算机网络）。我们去找了各个供应商，说我们需要十万块H100（超级强大的计算机芯片）来实现连贯训练。他们估计完成这项工作需要十八到二十四个月。可是，我们必须在六个月内完成，否则就没有竞争力。

So, we broke that down. We need a building; we need power; we need cooling.

所以，我们把问题拆解开来。我们需要一栋建筑；我们需要电力；我们需要冷却。

We didn’t have enough time to build a building from scratch. So, we found a factory in Memphis that was no longer in use. The input power was 15 megawatts and we needed 150 megawatts. So, we rented generators and put them along one side of the building.

我们没有足够的时间从零开始建造一栋建筑。所以，我们找到了孟菲斯一座已停用的工厂。输入电力是15兆瓦，而我们需要150兆瓦。于是，我们租用了发电机，放在建筑的一侧。

Then, we needed cooling. So, we rented about a quarter of the mobile cooling capacity of the US and put the chillers on the other side of the building.

接着，我们需要冷却。于是，我们租用了美国约四分之一的移动冷却能力，把冷却设备放在建筑的另一侧。

That didn’t fully solve the problem because the power variations during training are very big. Power can drop by 50 percent in 100 milliseconds, which the generators can’t keep up with. So then we added Tesla megapacks and modified the software in the megapacks to be able to smooth out the power variation during the training run.

这并没有完全解决问题，因为训练期间的电力波动非常大。电力可能在100毫秒内下降50%，发电机无法跟上。于是我们增加了特斯拉超级电池，并修改了超级电池的软件，以便在训练过程中平滑电力波动。

Then there were a bunch of networking challenges. Networking cables for one hundred thousand GPUs (graphics processing units) are very, very difficult. We ran the networking team to do the cabling in four shifts, 24/7. I was sleeping in the data center and doing cabling myself.345

然后还有一系列网络挑战。为十万块GPU（图形处理单元）布线非常非常困难。我们让网络团队分四班倒全天候布线。我自己也在数据中心睡觉并亲自布线。345

Now called Colossus, it is currently the largest AI training platform in the world. It was constructed in 122 days. Ninety-two days later, it had been doubled to two hundred thousand GPUs.346

现在称为“巨像”，它是目前世界上最大的AI训练平台。它在122天内建成。九十二天后，GPU数量翻倍达到二十万块。346

SET AGGRESSIVE TIMELINES设定激进的时间表

I often tell the Tesla team: “It’s okay to scrap equipment or money. It’s not okay to scrap time.”347

我经常对特斯拉团队说：“设备或资金可以浪费，但时间绝不能浪费。”347

When I was interviewing for a vice president of machining, I explained I was paying for everything at SpaceX out of pocket. I asked him, “What’s the cheapest you would work for?” He haggled, and eventually we agreed. Ten minutes later, I gave him a contract. This was a Saturday at 5:00 p.m. He started working that night.348

当我面试机械副总裁时，我解释说我在SpaceX的一切开销都是自掏腰包。我问他：“你愿意接受的最低工资是多少？”他还价，最终我们达成了协议。十分钟后，我给了他合同。那是一个星期六下午五点，他当晚就开始工作了。348

Q: Do you deliberately make aggressive prediction timelines to drive people to be ambitious?

问：你是否故意制定激进的预测时间表以激励大家雄心勃勃？

For internal timelines, we set the most aggressive timelines we can. I do this because there’s a kind of “law of gaseous expansion” for schedules. Whatever time you set, it’s not going to be less than that. It’s rare that something will ever get done faster than the schedule.349

对于内部时间表，我们设定尽可能激进的时间。我这么做是因为时间表有一种“气体膨胀定律”。无论你设定多短的时间，实际完成时间都不会少于这个时间。很少有事情能比计划更快完成。349

When I cite a schedule, it is actually the schedule I think is true. It’s not some fake schedule I made up. It may be delusional—that is entirely possible, and that has happened from time to time. But it’s never some knowingly fake deadline, ever.350

当我引用时间表时，那实际上是我认为真实的时间表。不是我编造的假时间表。它可能是幻想——这完全有可能，而且确实偶尔发生过。但绝不是故意编造的假截止日期。350

I want to emphasize that some dates are not dates that will actually be met. For example, the initial release date for the Model 3 at Tesla was an impossible date because there are seven thousand unique components in the Model 3, and our deadline assumed all of them would arrive on time. That won’t happen. But, it was a date for us to hold ourselves (internally) and our suppliers to.351

我想强调，有些日期实际上是无法实现的。例如，特斯拉Model 3的最初发布日期是不可能实现的，因为Model 3有七千个独特零件，而我们的截止日期假设所有零件都会准时到达。这不可能。但这个日期是我们（内部）和供应商必须遵守的目标。351

I do have a habit of being optimistic with schedules.352

我确实有对时间表持乐观态度的习惯。352

When I give estimates about our production, it’s guesswork. Especially guesses about exponential curves. In exponential growth, the difference a year or two makes to an outcome is enormous. We got a lot of criticism for the number of cars we delivered in 2017. The area under the curve of production in 2017 was quite small because it was the beginning of an exponential ramp, but once that growth got going, the area under the curve was enormous. That’s why people were so shocked. I kept trying to say this, but people don’t understand what exponential means.353

当我对我们的产量做出估计时，这其实是猜测。尤其是对指数曲线的猜测。在指数增长中，提前一两年对结果的影响巨大。我们因2017年交付的汽车数量受到很多批评。2017年产量曲线下面积很小，因为那是指数增长的起点，但一旦增长开始，曲线下面积就非常大。这就是人们感到震惊的原因。我一直试图解释，但人们不理解指数增长的含义。353

In 2018, we doubled our global fleet, cumulatively. We made and delivered about as many cars as we had in our entire history. Most people thought it wasn’t possible. If you predicted linear growth, it wouldn’t seem possible.

2018年，我们的全球车队规模累计翻了一番。我们制造并交付的汽车数量几乎等于我们历史上的总和。大多数人认为这是不可能的。如果你预测线性增长，这确实看起来不可能。

But when making estimates involving exponential growth, small changes in the calendar breakpoint have enormous percentage differences in outcome. The time difference is small, but the percentage difference in the outcome is enormous.354

但在涉及指数增长的估计中，时间节点的微小变化会导致结果的百分比差异巨大。时间差异很小，但结果的百分比差异非常大。354

As far as our predictions are concerned, the media tends to report all the wrong ones and ignore all the correct ones. I’ve had a long career in multiple industries. If you list just my sins, I sound like the worst person on Earth. But if you put those in the context of what I’ve done right, it makes much more sense.

就我们的预测而言，媒体往往报道所有错误的预测，忽略所有正确的。我在多个行业有长期职业生涯。如果只列举我的错误，听起来我像地球上最糟糕的人。但如果把这些放在我做对的事情的背景下，就更合理了。

I don’t want to blow your mind, but I’m not always right.355

我不想让你震惊，但我并不总是正确的。355

The longer you do anything, the more mistakes you will make, cumulatively. If you sum up just the mistakes, it sounds like I’m the worst predictor ever, which is not the case.

你做任何事情时间越长，累计犯的错误就越多。如果只把错误加起来，听起来我像是最糟糕的预测者，但事实并非如此。

Some of them happen sooner or later, but they do tend to come true. It’s rare they do not come true eventually. For radical technology predictions, the point is not that it was a few years late, but that it happened at all. That’s the more important part.356

有些错误迟早会发生，但它们往往会成真。很少有预测最终不成真的。对于激进的技术预测，关键不在于晚了几年，而在于它最终实现了。这才是更重要的部分。356

I may be a little optimistic, but I always deliver.357

我可能有点乐观，但我总是能兑现承诺。357

WE MUST MAKE STUFF我们必须制造东西

Manufacturing is underrated. It’s hard.358

制造业被低估了。这很难。358

THE REAL WORK真正的工作

Some people have an absurd view of the economy as a magic thing that just produces stuff.

有些人对经济抱有荒谬的看法，认为它是一个神奇的东西，能够自动生产物品。

They think goods and services magically come from somewhere, and if somebody has more stuff than somebody else, it’s because they took more from this magic source of stuff.359

他们认为商品和服务是神奇地从某处产生的，如果有人拥有比别人更多的东西，那是因为他们从这个神奇的物质源头那里拿得更多。359

Now, let me break it to the fools out there. If we don’t make stuff, there’s no stuff. If we don’t grow the food, process the food, and transport the food…there’s no food. Medical treatments, getting your teeth fixed, everything. There’s no stuff if we don’t make stuff.

现在，让我告诉那些愚蠢的人。如果我们不制造东西，就没有东西。如果我们不种植食物、加工食物和运输食物……就没有食物。医疗治疗、修牙，所有一切。如果我们不制造东西，就没有东西。

Some people have become detached from reality. This notion that the government can just send checks out to everybody and everything will be fine is not true—obviously. You can’t just legislate money to solve things. If you don’t make stuff, there is no stuff. The whole machine could grind to a halt.360

有些人已经脱离了现实。政府可以给每个人发支票，一切都会好起来的这种想法显然不正确。你不能仅靠立法印钱来解决问题。如果你不制造东西，就没有东西。整个机器可能会停摆。360

Technological progress is not inevitable. It’s not some kind of abstract concept. Humans make technology. If we don’t do it, it will not happen.361

技术进步不是必然的。这不是某种抽象的概念。技术是人类创造的。如果我们不去做，它就不会发生。361

Somebody has to do the real work.

必须有人做真正的工作。

There’s an overallocation of talent in finance and law, especially in the United States. Too many smart people go into finance and law. This is both a compliment and a criticism. We should have fewer people doing law and finance and more people making stuff.

在金融和法律领域，尤其是在美国，人才过度集中。太多聪明人进入了金融和法律行业。这既是赞美也是批评。我们应该减少从事法律和金融的人数，增加制造业的人数。

Manufacturing used to be highly valued in the United States. These days it hasn’t been as much, which I think is wrong. Making cars is an honest day’s living, that’s for sure. Making anything or providing a valuable service like good entertainment, good information—these are valuable things to do.362

制造业曾经在美国备受重视。如今情况不那么明显了，我认为这是错误的。制造汽车是一份诚实的工作，这一点毋庸置疑。制造任何东西或提供有价值的服务，比如优质娱乐、优质信息——这些都是有价值的事情。362

I’ve got mad respect for the makers of things.363

我非常尊重那些制造东西的人。363

THE FACTORY IS THE PRODUCT工厂即产品

The biggest epiphany I had building Tesla is what really matters is the machine that builds the machines—the factory.364

我在打造特斯拉过程中最大的顿悟是，真正重要的是制造机器的机器——工厂本身。364

To accelerate a sustainable future, Tesla had to scale up production volume as quickly as possible. That is why Tesla engineering transitioned to focus heavily on designing the machine that makes the machines—turning the factory itself into the product.

为了加速可持续未来，特斯拉必须尽快扩大生产规模。这就是为什么特斯拉工程团队转而重点设计制造机器的机器——将工厂本身变成产品。

A first-principles physics analysis of automotive production suggests that somewhere between a fivefold to tenfold improvement is achievable by version three on a roughly two-year iteration cycle.365

基于第一性原理的汽车生产物理分析表明，通过大约两年的迭代周期，第三代工厂有望实现五倍到十倍的改进。365

Tesla believes strongly in making things. Apple and Google do not. It’s a philosophical difference. We believe that manufacturing technology is itself subject to a tremendous amount of innovation. In fact, we believe there’s more potential for innovation in manufacturing than in the design of a car—by a long shot. This is just a philosophical difference. Perhaps we are wrong. But we believe in manufacturing, and a company that values manufacturing as highly as we do will attract the best minds in manufacturing.366

特斯拉坚信制造业的重要性，而苹果和谷歌则不然。这是哲学上的差异。我们相信制造技术本身蕴含着巨大的创新潜力。事实上，我们认为制造业的创新潜力远远超过汽车设计——这是显而易见的哲学差异。也许我们错了，但我们相信制造业，且一家高度重视制造的公司将吸引制造领域的顶尖人才。366

Our success or failure will not be because of competition. It will be our capability to make a high-quality product at a price people can afford.367

我们的成功或失败不会因为竞争，而是取决于我们是否有能力以人们负担得起的价格制造高质量产品。367

You’ve got this giant factory, a cybernetic collective with ten thousand things going wrong and you’ve got to solve them all. Fast. If you don’t solve problems fast enough, the factory doesn’t go. A big factory burns a huge amount of money every minute you aren’t making a product.368

你拥有一个庞大的工厂，这个网络系统中有一万个问题同时出现，你必须全部快速解决。如果你解决问题不够快，工厂就无法运转。一个大型工厂每一分钟不生产产品就会烧掉大量资金。368

ATTACK THE CONSTRAINT攻克瓶颈

Designing a rocket is trivial.

设计火箭很简单。

There are tons of books you can read, and if you can understand equations, you can design a rocket. Real easy. But making even one of these things and getting it to orbit is very hard.369

你可以读很多书，如果你懂方程式，你就能设计火箭。真的很简单。但制造出哪怕一个火箭并让它进入轨道却非常难。369

It’s the same thing for cars. It’s easy to make a car prototype; it’s hard to do car production.370

汽车也是一样。做出汽车原型很容易，批量生产汽车却很难。370

This is underappreciated. People think there is a “eureka” moment where you come up with an idea and that’s it. They believe design is the hard part and production is just making copies. That’s completely false.371

这一点被严重低估了。人们认为有一个“顿悟”时刻，想出一个点子就完事了。他们以为设计是难点，生产只是复制。这完全错误。371

At Tesla, we learned a valuable lesson. The production line will move as fast as the slowest and least lucky part of the entire production line. Let’s say there are ten thousand things that have to go right for production to work. If you have 9,999 things working and one that isn’t, that sets the production rate.372

在特斯拉，我们学到了宝贵的一课。生产线的速度取决于整条生产线中最慢、最不走运的环节。假设生产要顺利进行必须有一万件事都正确，如果有9999件事正常，只有一件不行，那生产速度就由这件事决定。372

Things move as fast as the least lucky or least competent supplier. Any natural disaster you care to name has happened to our suppliers. A supplier had a factory burn down. An earthquake. A tsunami. Massive hail. A tornado. A ship sank. A shoot-out at the Mexican border. I’m not kidding. That delayed trunk carpet.373

速度取决于最不走运或最不称职的供应商。你能想到的任何自然灾害，我们的供应商都遇到过。供应商工厂着火了。地震。海啸。巨大冰雹。龙卷风。船只沉没。墨西哥边境枪战。我不是开玩笑。这导致后备箱地毯延迟。373

So when scaling SpaceX, we spent ten to one hundred times more effort on designing the manufacturing system than on designing the Raptor engine.374 We built the rockets first and the factory later, because building the production system is the harder thing.375

所以在扩大SpaceX规模时，我们在制造系统设计上花费的精力是设计猛禽发动机的十倍到一百倍。374 我们先造火箭，后建工厂，因为建立生产系统更难。375

Design is overrated, and manufacturing is underrated. There is 1,000 percent, maybe 10,000 percent more work that goes into the production system than the product itself.376 Especially for a product with new technology. The difficulty of manufacturing is proportionate to the amount of new technology in the product.377

设计被高估了，制造被低估了。投入到生产系统的工作量比产品本身多1000%，甚至可能多10000%。376 尤其是新技术产品。制造难度与产品中新技术的含量成正比。377

MANUFACTURING IS THE MOAT制造是护城河

Two things define manufacturing competitiveness: economies of scale and technology. If you maximize your level of technology and maximize your level of scale, this is obviously going to be the most competitive situation. That’s why plants are so freaking giant.378

制造竞争力由两方面决定：规模经济和技术。如果你最大化技术水平和规模水平，这显然是最具竞争力的状态。这就是为什么工厂如此巨大。378

Our plant in Texas starts from actual rail cars of raw materials coming in. We form the battery cell and the battery pack, build the motor, and cast the parts. We also have introduced a major innovation, which is to cast the entire front third and rear third of the car as a single piece. I got this idea from toy cars.

我们在德克萨斯的工厂从实际的铁路原材料车厢开始。我们组装电池单元和电池包，制造电机，并铸造零件。我们还引入了一项重大创新，将汽车的前1/3和后1/3作为一个整体铸造。我这个想法来自玩具车。

I wondered, “Toys are cheap! How do they make toys?”

我想，“玩具很便宜！他们是怎么制造玩具的？”

“They just cast them.”

“他们就是直接铸造。”

I was like, “Well, can you build a casting machine big enough for a car?”

我说，“那么，你能制造一台足够大的铸造机来铸造汽车吗？”

They said, “No one ever has.”

他们说，“没人做过。”

“Are we breaking any laws of physics?”

“我们是不是违反了物理定律？”

“No…”

“没有……”

“Well, let’s just ask them.”

“那我们就去问问他们吧。”

There were six major casting machine suppliers in the world at the time. Five of them said no and the sixth said maybe. I said, “I’ll take that as a yes.”379

当时全球有六家主要的铸造机供应商。五家说不，第六家说可能。我说，“那我就当作是肯定了。”379

Prototypes are easy and fun. Reaching volume production with a reliable product at an affordable price is excruciatingly difficult.380

原型制作简单且有趣。实现可靠产品的批量生产且价格合理则极其艰难。380

PART III第三部分

Part III: Building Companies

第三部分：打造公司

Q: What fuels your creativity?

问：是什么激发了你的创造力？

Pressure. Necessity.381

压力。必要性。381

BECOMING A FOUNDER成为创始人

You want to embark on something where success is certain to be one of the possible outcomes.382

你想要开始一件成功可能性确定存在的事情。382

Q: What was the path to starting your first company?

问：你是如何开始你的第一家公司？

Growing up in South Africa, it seemed like a lot of the advanced technology was being produced in America and Silicon Valley, especially. I wanted to go where I could be involved in the creation of new technology. That’s what took me first to Canada (because I could get citizenship in Canada through my mom) and then ultimately to the US.383

我在南非长大，感觉很多先进技术都是在美国，尤其是硅谷生产的。我想去一个能参与新技术创造的地方。这让我先去了加拿大（因为我可以通过我妈妈获得加拿大公民身份），最终去了美国。383

When I was seventeen, I got my Canadian passport. Three weeks later I was in Canada.384 I left South Africa and landed in Montreal. I arrived with a backpack, a suitcase of books, and $2,000.

我十七岁时拿到了加拿大护照。三周后我就到了加拿大。384我离开南非，抵达蒙特利尔。我带着背包、一箱书和两千美元到达。

I stayed in a youth hostel for a few days, then bought a bus ticket for one hundred bucks to go across the country, making stops along the way.385

我在青年旅舍住了几天，然后花一百块买了张跨国巴士票，中途多次停靠。385

I managed to get to Swift Current in Saskatchewan. My cousin’s son had a wheat farm where I worked for about six weeks, as I turned eighteen. We did a barn raising, cleared out the wheat bins and grain silos, and worked the vegetable patch.386

我设法到了萨斯喀彻温省的斯威夫特卡伦特。我表弟的儿子有个小麦农场，我在那里工作了大约六周，正值我十八岁生日。我们搭建谷仓，清理小麦仓和粮仓，还种植蔬菜。386

Then I got back on the bus and went to Vancouver. I had a half-uncle there in the lumber industry. I ended up working at a lumber mill chainsawing logs and cleaning out the pulp boiler. That might be the hardest job I’ve had. You had to crawl through this little tunnel in a hazmat suit and then shovel this steaming sand and mush out of the boilers to clean them out. There’s only one entrance or exit, a little tunnel. If you’re claustrophobic, it would be real bad. It did not seem safe. But it was the highest-paying job at the employment office. Other jobs were under eight dollars an hour, and this one was eighteen dollars an hour.387

然后我又坐巴士去了温哥华。我有个半叔叔在木材行业。我最终在一家锯木厂工作，锯木头和清理纸浆锅炉。那可能是我做过最辛苦的工作。你得穿着防护服爬进一个小隧道，然后铲出锅炉里冒着热气的沙子和泥浆来清理。只有一个进出口，是个小隧道。如果你有幽闭恐惧症，那会非常糟糕。看起来也不安全。但这是就业办公室工资最高的工作。其他工作时薪不到八美元，这个是十八美元。387

I worked there as a lumberjack doing odd jobs for a few months. Then I applied for college, and went to Queen’s University in Kingston. I was there for a couple years before applying to UPenn. I didn’t think I’d be able to go because I was paying my own way through university. That’s not too hard in Canada because tuition is highly subsidized, but in the US college is more expensive.388

我在那里做了几个月的伐木工和杂活。然后我申请了大学，去了金斯顿的皇后大学。在那里待了几年后申请了宾夕法尼亚大学。我原以为自己负担不起，因为我自费上大学。在加拿大这不难，因为学费有很大补贴，但美国大学更贵。388

I paid my own way through college and dropped out of Stanford grad school with $110K in college debt.389

我自费上大学，后来在斯坦福研究生院辍学，背负了11万美元的学生债务。389

Physics and computer science were always my two best subjects, because I wanted to figure out the nature of the universe. I thought I might do physics at a particle accelerator, banging particles together to see what happens in a research facility. Then, the supercollider got canceled in the US, and I realized I could study for years to work at a collider, and then the government could arbitrarily cancel it. Decision made: I could not do that.390

物理和计算机科学一直是我最擅长的两门学科，因为我想弄清宇宙的本质。我曾想在粒子加速器做物理研究，在研究设施里撞击粒子看看会发生什么。后来，美国取消了超级对撞机项目，我意识到我可能要花多年时间研究对撞机，结果政府可能随时取消。决定已定：我不能做那条路。390

Q: How did you decide to leave your PhD program and start your first company?

问：你是如何决定离开博士项目并创办第一家公司的？

Because of my interest in electric cars as a college student, I took an internship at a company that made high-energy-density capacitors. My intent was to get a PhD in energy storage solutions for electric vehicles. I was going to Stanford to study material science and the physics of high-energy-density capacitors for use in electric vehicles.391

因为大学时对电动汽车的兴趣，我在一家制造高能量密度电容器的公司实习。我的目标是获得电动汽车能量存储解决方案的博士学位。我打算去斯坦福学习材料科学和高能量密度电容器的物理，用于电动汽车。391

But, I wasn’t sure if my work during that PhD would actually be useful. I was concerned it could be academically useful but not practically useful.392

但我不确定博士期间的工作是否真的有用。我担心它可能在学术上有用，但在实际应用上没用。392

Success on an academic level would have been quite likely. I could publish a paper, but most papers are pretty useless.393 Once in a while you get something spectacular but it’s pretty rare.394 How many PhD papers are actually used by someone, ever? Percentagewise, not many. You add a leaf to the tree of knowledge, but that leaf could be saying, Nope, not possible. This is not good enough technology to be used in an electric vehicle. There goes seven years of my life.395

学术上的成功很可能实现。我可以发表论文，但大多数论文其实没什么用。393偶尔会有非常出色的成果，但很罕见。394有多少博士论文被真正使用过？比例不高。你为知识树添了一片叶子，但那片叶子可能是说“不行，不可能”，这技术不够好，不能用在电动汽车上。那就白白浪费了七年时间。395

Not that I cared about the PhD, actually. I just needed a lab. I could spend years working in a lab and maybe the technology would work…or maybe it wouldn’t.396

其实我并不在乎博士学位。我只是需要一个实验室。我可以花几年时间在实验室里工作，也许技术会成功……也许不会。396

I was not sure success was even a possibility. I thought maybe it was, but I wasn’t sure.397

我不确定成功是否可能。我觉得可能，但不确定。397

Then the internet started taking off. It was clear to me the internet was happening in 1995, although most people weren’t aware of it then.398 I was pretty sure success was one of the possible outcomes for an internet company. I knew watching the internet get built while doing a PhD would be frustrating.399 I decided to put graduate school on hold and start an internet company.400

后来互联网开始兴起。1995年我就清楚互联网正在发生，尽管当时大多数人还没意识到。398我很确定互联网公司成功是可能的。我知道一边做博士一边看互联网发展会很沮丧。399我决定暂停研究生学业，创办一家互联网公司。400

I thought I’d be able to come back to electric vehicles. I figured electric vehicle technology and energy storage technology would have a natural progression, and that ended up happening.

我以为自己还能回头做电动汽车。我觉得电动汽车技术和能量存储技术会有自然的进展，事实也确实如此。

In 1995, it was not obvious you could make any money on the internet. Until Netscape went public in late 1995, nobody thought you could make a valuable internet company. Now it seems obvious. But back then, it was not at all.

1995年，没人觉得互联网能赚钱。直到1995年底Netscape上市，没人认为互联网公司能有价值。现在看起来很明显，但当时完全不是这样。

Humanity was effectively becoming a superorganism, qualitatively different from what it had been before. I wanted to be part of that. I wanted to help build humanity’s nervous system.401

人类实际上正在成为一个超级有机体，质的变化。我想成为其中一员，想帮助构建人类的神经系统。401

The internet turned out to be a good idea for my first company because software is a low-capital endeavor. I didn’t have any money, and had a bunch of student debt. You don’t need a lot of tooling and equipment to get started. Software you can just write by yourself. It’s not capital intensive. Starting something software-related as your first company is much, much easier.402
