互联网成为我第一家公司的好选择，因为软件是低资本投入的。我没钱，还背着学生债务。开始做软件不需要很多工具和设备。软件可以自己写，不需要大量资本。作为第一家公司做软件相关的事情要容易得多。402

STARTING ZIP2开始Zip2

When we first started Zip2, our ambitions were quite low: make enough money to pay rent.403

“当我们刚开始Zip2时，我们的目标很低：挣够钱付房租。”403

Q: When did you start your first company?

问：你什么时候创办了第一家公司？

Starting a company was not actually the first thing I tried. I tried to get a job at Netscape, but they didn’t reply to my emails. So I went to hang out in the lobby at Netscape. I didn’t know who to talk to, and I was too shy to talk to anyone. I figured, “If I can’t get a job, I’ll just try writing my own software.”404

实际上，创办公司并不是我尝试的第一件事。我试图去Netscape找工作，但他们没有回复我的邮件。于是我去Netscape的大堂逛了一圈。我不知道该跟谁说话，也太害羞不敢跟任何人说。我想，“如果我找不到工作，那我就试着自己写软件。”404

It wasn’t actually from the desire to start a company. I just wanted to be part of building the internet in some way. Since I couldn’t get a job at an internet company, I had to start one myself.405

其实这并不是出于想创业的愿望。我只是想以某种方式参与建设互联网。既然我找不到互联网公司的工作，那我就得自己创办一家。405

I wanted to try to build something useful, but I didn’t think I would build anything particularly great. Probabilistically, greatness seemed unlikely, but I wanted to at least try.406

我想尝试做点有用的东西，但我并不认为自己会做出特别伟大的东西。从概率上看，伟大似乎不太可能，但我至少想试一试。406

We started off building maps, directions, and classified ads. It was, to the best of my knowledge, the first map and directions on the internet. There may still be some patents—or maybe they lapsed at this point. (Note from Eric: there is, patent #5944769.407) The whole initial code base I wrote myself because there wasn’t anyone else. It was just me.408

我们一开始做的是地图、路线和分类广告。据我所知，这是互联网上的第一个地图和路线服务。可能还有一些专利——或者现在已经过期了。（埃里克注：有，专利号5944769。407）最初的全部代码都是我自己写的，因为当时没有别人，只有我一个人。408

I was twenty-four at the time and only had a few thousand dollars saved. I convinced my brother to join, and he brought about five thousand dollars, which was a lot for us. For the first few months, we only had one computer. When the website wasn’t working, it was because I was compiling code. The website was up during the day; I was coding at night, seven days a week, all the time. We more or less squatted in the office because the landlord was always out of the country, and nobody was using it.409

那时我24岁，存款只有几千美元。我说服了我弟弟加入，他带来了大约五千美元，对我们来说已经很多了。最初几个月，我们只有一台电脑。网站不工作的时候，是因为我在编译代码。白天网站在线，晚上我编程，一周七天，几乎全天候。我们差不多是蹭着办公室住，因为房东总是在国外，没人用那个办公室。409

Soon, there were six of us. Me, my brother, a friend of my mom’s, and three salespeople we hired on contingency by putting an ad in the newspaper.410

很快，我们有六个人了。我、我弟弟、我妈妈的一个朋友，还有三个我们通过报纸广告按业绩付费雇来的销售人员。410

Things were pretty tough in the early going. I didn’t have any money. In fact, I had negative money because of a huge student debt. I couldn’t afford an apartment and an office, so I rented an office because that was cheaper. I slept on the futon at the office and showered at the YMCA. I briefly had a girlfriend in that period and to stay with me, she had to sleep in the office. I was in the best shape—every day a workout, shower, and you’re good to go!411

刚开始的时候非常艰难。我没钱，实际上因为巨额学生贷款，我的钱是负数。我负担不起公寓和办公室，只能租办公室，因为那更便宜。我睡在办公室的沙发床上，在YMCA洗澡。那段时间我有个女朋友，她要跟我在一起，就得睡办公室。我那时状态最好——每天锻炼、洗澡，然后就准备好了！411

There was a small internet service provider on the floor below us, so we drilled a hole through the floor and connected a modem cable. That got us our internet connection for one hundred bucks a month. We had a tiny revenue stream, but an absurdly tiny burn rate. We actually had more revenue than we had expenses. When we talked to VCs (venture capitalists), we could say we were profitable.412

我们楼下有一家小型互联网服务提供商，于是我们钻了个洞穿过地板，接了调制解调器线。这样我们每月花一百美元就有了互联网连接。收入很少，但开销更少。实际上我们的收入比支出还多。跟风险投资家谈时，我们可以说我们是盈利的。412

Most venture capitalists wouldn’t take a meeting with us, though.

“不过大多数风险投资家都不愿意跟我们见面。”

They said, “Who’s made money on the internet? No one. Okay, pass.”413

“他们说，‘谁在互联网上赚钱了？没人。好吧，算了。’”413

There was a lot more interest in the internet following the Netscape IPO (initial public offering). Our software was more impressive by then, too. Mohr Davidow Ventures (a VC firm) invested $3 million for 60 percent of the company, which we thought was crazy. They’re going to give us money for nothing? They must be mad. It seemed insane to give us so much money for a company that consisted of six people at the time. But it worked out well for them.414

Netscape上市后，互联网的关注度大增。那时我们的软件也更令人印象深刻了。Mohr Davidow Ventures（一家风险投资公司）投资了300万美元，获得了公司60%的股份，我们觉得这太疯狂了。他们要无偿给我们钱？他们一定疯了。给一家只有六个人的公司这么多钱，听起来很疯狂。但对他们来说结果很好。414

Q: How did the original idea for Zip2 come about?

问：Zip2的最初想法是怎么来的？

We needed to build something that would earn money quickly. We thought the media industry would need help converting their content from print to electronic, and they clearly had money. If we could help them move to the internet, maybe we could generate revenue. There was no advertising revenue on the internet at the time. That was the basis of Zip2.415

我们需要快速做出能赚钱的东西。我们认为媒体行业需要帮助把内容从印刷转到电子，而且他们显然有钱。如果我们能帮他们上网，也许能产生收入。当时互联网没有广告收入。这就是Zip2的基础。415

We hired a lot more people and wrote software to bring newspapers online. Knight Ridder, New York Times Company, and Hearst all became investors and customers. And at one point, Zip2 was responsible for a significant section of the New York Times Company website. I got to know the media industry well.416

我们雇了更多人，写软件帮助报纸上线。Knight Ridder、纽约时报公司和赫斯特都成了投资者和客户。Zip2一度负责纽约时报公司网站的一个重要部分。我对媒体行业非常了解。416

The challenge was too much control by the existing media companies. They had too many board seats and too much voting control over Zip2. They kept trying to push the company in directions that made no sense. We actually had good software—comparable to Yahoo! or Excite at the time. It was all being forced through media companies, who would not fully use it. We built the best technology, but it wasn’t being deployed properly.417

挑战在于现有媒体公司控制过多。他们占据太多董事会席位，对Zip2有过多投票权。他们不断试图把公司推向毫无意义的方向。我们其实有很好的软件——当时可与Yahoo!或Excite媲美。但都被媒体公司强行控制，他们并未充分利用。我们打造了最好的技术，但没有得到正确部署。417

It’s a bit like building F-22 fighter jets and selling them to people who roll them down the hill at each other. Not the way to use the technology!418

“这有点像造F-22战斗机，却卖给那些把它们滚下山坡互相撞击的人。不是技术的正确用法！”418

That’s when I realized you want to sell your products straight to the end consumer. If you’ve got great technology, go directly to the end consumer. Don’t sell it to some bonehead legacy company that doesn’t understand how to use it.419

那时我意识到，你想把产品直接卖给最终消费者。如果你有很棒的技术，就直接面向最终消费者。别卖给那些不懂怎么用它的老旧公司。419

Fortunately, Compaq came along.420 We had the opportunity to sell Zip2 to Compaq in early 1999 and accepted that offer. It was a little over $300 million in cash. Cash is a currency I highly recommend.421

幸运的是，Compaq出现了。420 1999年初，我们有机会把Zip2卖给Compaq，并接受了报价。现金稍微超过3亿美元。现金是我极力推荐的货币形式。421

To this day, that moment astonishes me. At the time I was living in a house with four housemates. Literally, a check came in my mailbox. I was like, “This is insane. What if somebody…? I mean, I guess they’d have trouble cashing it?” Seems like a weird way to send money. My bank account went from $5,000 to $21,005,000.422

直到今天，那一刻仍让我震惊。那时我和四个室友住一屋。真的，一张支票寄到了我的邮箱。我当时想，“这太疯狂了。如果有人……？我猜他们兑现会有麻烦？”用这种方式寄钱真奇怪。我的银行账户从5000美元变成了2100.5万美元。422

Immediately after the sale, I didn’t take any time off. I still had to pay taxes, then I ended up putting almost all of that into X.com (which became PayPal). This was early 1999, and there were a lot of opportunities remaining on the internet.423

卖掉后我没休息，仍得交税，然后几乎把所有钱都投到了X.com（后来成为PayPal）。那是1999年初，互联网还有很多机会。423

I’ve always wanted to push my chips back on the table or play the next level of the game. I’m not good at sitting back.424

“我一直想把筹码推回桌面，或者玩下一关。我不擅长坐视不管。”424

GOING ALL IN, AGAIN (ZIP2 EARNINGS INTO PAYPAL)再次全力以赴（将Zip2收益投入PayPal）

It was frustrating.

这令人沮丧。

We built incredible technology at Zip2, and it had not been used. I wanted to build another internet company to show technology can be extremely effective when used properly.425

我们在Zip2开发了令人难以置信的技术，但它并未被充分利用。我想建立另一家互联网公司，来证明技术在正确使用时可以极其有效。425

I felt like we had our wings clipped somewhat with Zip2. I wanted to avoid being constrained by our customers like that, and go direct-to-consumer. That’s what motivated me to start PayPal.426

我觉得我们在Zip2有些被束缚了。我想避免被客户那样限制，直接面向消费者。这就是促使我创办PayPal的动力。426

Q: How did you come up with the idea for PayPal?

问：你是如何想到PayPal这个点子的？

I thought, What was essentially digital? What exists in the form of information, but is not high bandwidth? In 1999, most people still had modems, so video was not really feasible. Money is low bandwidth and mostly digital. What can we do to make money work better?427

我想，什么本质上是数字化的？什么以信息形式存在，但带宽不高？1999年，大多数人还在用调制解调器，所以视频并不现实。钱是低带宽且主要是数字化的。我们能做些什么让钱运作得更好？427

Money is like a database for guiding people to decide what they should do. You can think of banks as a set of databases.428

钱就像一个指导人们决策的数据库。你可以把银行看作是一组数据库。428

The 1990s financial infrastructure was a bunch of ancient mainframes, running ancient code, doing batch processing with poor security, and a series of heterogeneous databases. A herky-jerky frickin’ monstrosity.

90年代的金融基础设施是一堆古老的大型机，运行着古老的代码，进行批处理，安全性差，还有一系列异构数据库。简直是一个断断续续的混乱怪物。

From an information theory standpoint, finance would be much better if it could be real-time, secure, and fast. Essentially just one real-time database. Let’s try to build that.

从信息理论角度看，如果金融能做到实时、安全且快速，那会好得多。基本上就是一个实时数据库。让我们试着构建它。

Q: Can you explain the PayPal idea from an information theory perspective?

问：你能从信息理论的角度解释PayPal的理念吗？

Think of money as information. People often think money has power in and of itself. It does not. Money is just information. Money is a database for resource allocation across time and space.

把钱看作信息。人们常认为钱本身就有力量，其实没有。钱只是信息。钱是一个跨时间和空间进行资源分配的数据库。

Let’s try thinking in the limit, as I explained before. If you are stranded on a tropical island with a trillion dollars, it’s useless. On the island there are no resources to allocate, except yourself, so money doesn’t help. If you’re stranded with no food, all the Bitcoin in the world will not stop you from starving.

让我们尝试极限思考，正如我之前解释的。如果你被困在一个热带岛屿上，带着一万亿美元，那毫无用处。岛上没有资源可分配，只有你自己，所以钱无济于事。如果你被困且没有食物，世界上所有的比特币也救不了你免于饥饿。

You need something to create ratios of value between products and services. In an economy, you have a massive number of products and services. You can’t trade for everything. That would be extremely unwieldy. You need something to create the ratio of exchange between goods and services. You also need something that allows us to shift obligations across time, like debt and equity.429

你需要某种东西来创建产品和服务之间的价值比率。在经济中，有大量的产品和服务。你不可能用物物交换来交易所有东西，那会极其笨拙。你需要某种东西来创建商品和服务之间的交换比率。你还需要某种东西让我们能够跨时间转移义务，比如债务和股权。429

The quality of money as a system is a function of different variables. Just like an internet connection, you want high bandwidth, low latency, and few errors.430

钱作为一个系统的质量是不同变量的函数。就像互联网连接一样，你希望有高带宽、低延迟和少错误。430

What PayPal really did was help improve the bandwidth—the speed at which money could move. Instead of mailing checks back and forth, you could do real-time exchange of money online. Sellers could then ship items immediately instead of waiting for a check and then for the bank to clear it.431

PayPal真正做的是帮助提高带宽——钱流动的速度。你不再需要邮寄支票，可以在线实时交换资金。卖家可以立即发货，而不必等待支票和银行清算。431

We should look at currencies from an information theory standpoint.

我们应该从信息理论的角度看待货币。

Whichever has least error and latency will win.

哪个误差和延迟最小，哪个就会胜出。

That’s what X.com originally was. I thought we should try to do all the financial things as well—not just payments. I still think that’s what PayPal should have done, but whatever. It’s water under the bridge at this point.432

这就是X.com最初的理念。我认为我们应该尝试做所有金融相关的事情，而不仅仅是支付。我仍然认为PayPal本应该这么做，但无所谓了，毕竟已经成了过去。432

I rolled most of my Zip2 money into X.com, investing $12.5 million.433 In 1999, Sequoia invested in X.com, buying $5 million in shares, and Mike Moritz joined the company’s board. When Moritz invested, he said we should hire a CEO. I said, “Great, I don’t want to be CEO.” I had no desire to be a CEO. It’s a lot of chores…being CEO sucks. He also told me, “Dude, you should not invest basically everything except your house and car in your startup. ”434

我将大部分Zip2的钱投入了X.com，投资了1250万美元。433 1999年，红杉资本投资了X.com，买入了500万美元的股份，迈克·莫里茨加入了公司董事会。当莫里茨投资时，他说我们应该聘请一位CEO。我说，“太好了，我不想当CEO。”我一点也不想当CEO，做CEO有很多琐事……当CEO很糟糕。他还告诉我，“伙计，你不应该把除了房子和车之外的几乎所有东西都投资到你的创业公司里。”434

I kept the chips on the table.435

我把筹码都摊开在桌面上了。435

LISTEN WELL, CORRECT FAST善于倾听，快速纠正

The initial thought with X.com was to create a conglomeration of financial services, so you have one place where all your financial service needs could be seamlessly integrated and work smoothly.

X.com最初的想法是创建一个金融服务集团，这样你就有一个地方可以无缝整合并顺畅运作你所有的金融服务需求。

We had a little feature: payments through email. When we showed the system to someone, we’d show the hard part first: the conglomeration of financial services. Nobody was interested.

我们有一个小功能：通过电子邮件支付。当我们向别人展示系统时，我们先展示最难的部分：金融服务的整合。没人感兴趣。

Then we showed people email payments, which were relatively easy to build, and everybody was interested. So, we focused on email payments. That’s what really got PayPal to take off.

然后我们向人们展示电子邮件支付，这相对容易构建，大家都感兴趣。所以，我们专注于电子邮件支付。这才真正让PayPal起飞。

It’s important to take feedback from your environment. If we hadn’t responded to what people said, we probably would not have been successful. It’s important to look for things like that, focus on them, and correct your prior assumptions. You want to close those loops as quickly and clearly as possible.436

从环境中获取反馈非常重要。如果我们没有回应人们的意见，可能就不会成功。重要的是寻找这样的东西，专注于它们，纠正之前的假设。你要尽快且清晰地关闭这些循环。436

I’m trying to create an accurate mental model of reality. If I have a wrong view on something, or if there’s a nuanced improvement that can be made, I say, “I used to think this, which turned out to be wrong—thank goodness I don’t have that wrong belief anymore.”437

我试图建立一个准确的现实心理模型。如果我对某事有错误的看法，或者有细微的改进可以做，我会说：“我以前是这么想的，结果证明是错的——谢天谢地我不再有那个错误的信念了。”437

I’m a huge believer in taking feedback.

我非常相信接受反馈。

We took a similar approach with PayPal that worked at Zip2, which was to have a small group of very talented people, and keep it small. PayPal had maybe thirty engineers for a system which I would say is more sophisticated than the Federal Reserve clearing system.

我们对PayPal采取了与Zip2类似的做法，即保持一小群非常有才华的人，并保持团队规模小。PayPal大约有三十名工程师，而我认为其系统比联邦储备清算系统还要复杂。

Both Zip2 and PayPal operated like Silicon Valley startups. Pretty flat hierarchy, everybody had a similar desk, and anyone could talk to anyone.

Zip2和PayPal都像硅谷初创公司一样运作。层级结构相当扁平，每个人的办公桌差不多，任何人都可以和任何人交流。

We had a philosophy of “best idea wins” as opposed to the person proposing the idea winning because of who they are. Even though there were times when I thought that should have been the way to go. Everyone was an equity stakeholder.438

我们的理念是“最佳创意获胜”，而不是因为提出创意的人是谁而获胜。尽管有时我认为应该按身份决定。每个人都是股权持有者。438

If there were two options, and one wasn’t obviously better than the other, rather than spend time trying to pick which one was slightly better, we would just pick one and go. Sometimes we were wrong and picked the suboptimal path, but at least we moved fast.439

如果有两个选项，且没有明显更好的一方，我们不会花时间去挑哪个稍好，而是直接选一个然后行动。有时我们错了，选了次优路径，但至少我们行动迅速。439

Better to pick a path and keep moving than just vacillate endlessly on a decision.

选择一条路并持续前进，总比在决策上犹豫不决要好。

We were focused on building the best product we possibly could. Both Zip2 and PayPal were product-focused companies. We didn’t worry too much about intellectual property paperwork. We were incredibly obsessive about building the best possible customer experience. That was a far more effective selling tool than having a giant sales force or marketing gimmicks.440

我们专注于打造尽可能最好的产品。Zip2和PayPal都是以产品为中心的公司。我们不太担心知识产权文件。我们极度执着于打造最佳客户体验。这比拥有庞大的销售团队或营销噱头更有效的销售工具。440

Pay close attention to negative feedback, and solicit it, particularly from friends. It’s incredibly helpful.

密切关注负面反馈，并主动征求，尤其是来自朋友的反馈。这非常有帮助。

This may sound like simple advice, but hardly anyone does it.441

这听起来像是简单的建议，但几乎没人这么做。441

UNITE AND CONQUER团结并征服

What matters to me is winning, and not in a small way.442

对我来说，重要的是赢，而且不是小打小闹的赢。442

Around the same time I was starting X, another company formed called Confinity, which was Peter Thiel, Max Levchin, Luke Nosek, David Sacks, Ken Howery, etc. At X.com, we had Jeremy Stoppelman, who created Yelp, and Roelof Botha, who went on to run Sequoia. Both companies had a crazy amount of talent.

大约在我创办X的同时，另一家公司Confinity成立了，成员包括彼得·蒂尔、马克斯·列夫钦、卢克·诺塞克、大卫·萨克斯、肯·豪瑞等。在X.com，我们有杰里米·斯托普尔曼，他后来创建了Yelp，还有罗洛夫·博萨，他后来管理红杉资本。两家公司都拥有大量顶尖人才。

Confinity started as a PalmPilot cryptography company, back when you could communicate through the infrared port of a PalmPilot. Then they evolved in the direction of payments as well. We were both in Palo Alto, a block away from each other—at one point, even in the same building. We were competing with each other like maniacs.

Confinity最初是一家PalmPilot加密公司，那时你可以通过PalmPilot的红外端口进行通信。后来他们也转向了支付领域。我们两家公司都位于帕洛阿尔托，彼此相距一个街区——有一段时间甚至在同一栋楼里。我们像疯子一样互相竞争。

Finally, we had coffee on University Avenue and said, “Hey, why don’t we just combine our efforts? Otherwise we’re gonna bludgeon each other to death.” We knew we had to get that deal done fast or we were both gonna die.

最终，我们在大学大道上喝咖啡时说：“嘿，为什么不合并我们的努力呢？否则我们会互相残杀。”我们知道必须尽快达成这笔交易，否则我们都会死。

So we merged Confinity with X.com and raised $100 million in three weeks during March of 2000.443 About a year later, we changed the company name to the product name, PayPal.444

于是我们将Confinity与X.com合并，并在2000年3月的三周内筹集了1亿美元。443大约一年后，我们将公司名称改为产品名称PayPal。444

We went from starting a company to fourteen months later merging and having a valuation of $500 million in March of 2000. It felt so sudden, like, “This is completely ridiculous.”445

我们从创办公司到十四个月后合并，并在2000年3月估值达到5亿美元。感觉非常突然，就像，“这简直荒谬。”445

In April 2000, the market went into freefall.446 The challenge was keeping the company alive for the next two years.447

2000年4月，市场开始暴跌。446挑战是让公司在接下来的两年内存活下来。447

There was a lot of drama; it was a turbulent period. I didn’t expect PayPal’s growth rate to be what it was, and that created major problems. After the first month of the website being active, we had one hundred thousand customers. It was nutty.448 We definitely did not anticipate that.449

那段时间充满了戏剧性，非常动荡。我没预料到PayPal的增长速度会如此之快，这带来了重大问题。网站上线第一个月，我们就拥有了十万客户。那简直疯狂。448我们绝对没预料到这一点。449

Q: What made PayPal’s growth rate so dramatic?

问：是什么让PayPal的增长速度如此惊人？

PayPal was a perfect case for viral marketing. Like Hotmail, one customer would be like a salesperson for you, bringing other customers. Customers would send money to a friend and bring that friend into the network. We had this exponential growth, where the more customers we had, the faster it grew. It was like bacteria in a petri dish, following an S-curve.450

PayPal是病毒式营销的完美案例。就像Hotmail一样，一个客户就像你的推销员，带来其他客户。客户会向朋友汇款，并将朋友带入网络。我们实现了指数增长，客户越多，增长越快。这就像培养皿中的细菌，遵循S型曲线。450

We started off first by offering people twenty dollars if they opened an account and twenty dollars if they referred anyone. Then we dropped it to ten dollars. Then we dropped it to five dollars. As the network got bigger and bigger, the value of the network itself exceeded any sort of carrot that we could offer.451 We probably spent $60 or $70 million in incentives to build that network. That seems like a lot, but it built a very valuable network. The relative cost depends on your scale. That’s a peanut to Google.452

我们最初提供开户奖励20美元，推荐奖励20美元。后来降到10美元，再降到5美元。随着网络越来越大，网络本身的价值超过了我们能提供的任何奖励。451我们大约花了6000万到7000万美元做激励。这看起来很多，但它建立了一个非常有价值的网络。相对成本取决于规模。对谷歌来说，这只是九牛一毛。452

Q: Leading through that kind of growth seems very challenging. How did you manage it all?

问：在如此高速增长中领导似乎非常具有挑战性。你是如何管理的？

I ran PayPal for about the first two years of its existence. We launched after year one, and by the end of year two we had a million customers. It gives you a sense for how fast things can grow. We didn’t have a sales force, VP of sales, or VP of marketing and we didn’t spend any money on advertising.453

我管理PayPal大约头两年。我们在第一年后上线，到第二年末拥有一百万客户。这让你感受到增长的速度。我们没有销售团队，没有销售副总裁，也没有市场副总裁，也没有花钱做广告。453

It wasn’t easy, because we still had some bugs in the software. Even if the bug only occurs one in one thousand times, that’s still a thousand angry customers shouting, “Where is my money!?”

这并不容易，因为软件仍有一些漏洞。即使漏洞发生率是千分之一，也意味着有一千个愤怒的客户在喊：“我的钱呢！？”

We had a customer service office on University Avenue in Palo Alto with five people. When something went wrong, customer service phones would explode. We had many challenges. Various financial regulatory agencies were trying to shut us down. The FTC was trying to shut us down. Visa, MasterCard, and eBay were all trying to have us shut down. There were a lot of battles.454

我们在帕洛阿尔托大学大道有一个五人客服办公室。出现问题时，客服电话会爆炸。我们面临许多挑战。各种金融监管机构试图关闭我们。联邦贸易委员会（FTC）试图关闭我们。Visa、MasterCard和eBay都试图让我们关闭。战斗不断。454

It was a close call. We came close to dying in 2000 and 2001.455

那真是千钧一发。我们在2000年和2001年差点死掉。455

FROM EXILE TO EXIT从流放到退出

I was away for two weeks and the management team decided I wasn’t the right guy to run the company.

我离开了两周，管理团队决定我不是合适的人选来管理公司。

Instead of fighting during a critical time, I thought it was best to concede.456

在关键时期选择争斗不如选择让步，我认为最好是让步。456

It’s not a good idea to leave the office when there are a lot of major things underway that are causing people a great deal of stress. I left for a trip, a combination of needing to raise money and I had gotten married earlier that year and had not had a vacation or honeymoon. It was kind of a combined financing trip and honeymoon. Also not a great idea.

当有许多重大事务正在进行，给大家带来极大压力时，离开办公室并不是一个好主意。我当时去旅行，既是为了筹集资金，也是因为那年早些时候我结了婚，还没有休过假或蜜月。这次旅行兼具融资和蜜月的性质，也不是个好主意。

I got removed as CEO of PayPal because I wanted to do a bunch of things that seemed extremely risky. I think they would have worked out, but at a time when companies were dropping like flies, I was proposing we do all these things that sound risky—it was much too scary for the rest of the team.457

我被免去了PayPal CEO的职务，因为我想做一堆看起来极其冒险的事情。我认为这些事情本来会成功，但当时公司纷纷倒闭，我提出的这些听起来很冒险的计划，对团队其他成员来说太吓人了。457

I didn’t agree with their conclusion, but I understood why they took the action they did. Peter, Max, David, and the other guys are smart people with the right motivations. They did what they thought was right, for the right reasons. The reasons weren’t valid in my opinion but it’s hard to argue with the ultimate outcome, which was positive.

我不同意他们的结论，但我理解他们为何采取那样的行动。Peter、Max、David和其他人都是聪明且动机正当的人。他们做了他们认为正确的事，出于正确的理由。虽然我认为这些理由并不成立，但最终结果是积极的，这一点很难反驳。

It would be easy to be bitter and hate them forever, but the better course of action is to turn the other cheek. I put a lot of effort into making things good between us, and they became good. I invested in some of Peter’s companies, including Founders Fund, which is made up primarily of former PayPal people. Peter, who replaced me as CEO, later invested in SpaceX.458

心怀怨恨、永远憎恨他们很容易，但更好的做法是以德报怨。我付出了很多努力让我们之间的关系变好，最终确实变好了。我投资了Peter的一些公司，包括主要由前PayPal成员组成的Founders Fund。后来接替我成为CEO的Peter也投资了SpaceX。458

Life is too short for long-term grudges.459

人生苦短，不值得怀恨终身。459

Q: What was the exit like?

问：退出过程是怎样的？

In about February of 2002, PayPal went public. I think we were the only internet company to go public in the first part of 2002. It went reasonably well, although I can imagine we set a record on SEC rewrites. This was right around the time of Enron and other corporate scandals, so they put us through the wringer.460

大约在2002年2月，PayPal上市了。我想我们是2002年初唯一上市的互联网公司。上市过程还算顺利，尽管我想我们在美国证券交易委员会的文件修改上创了纪录。那时正值安然和其他企业丑闻爆发，所以他们对我们进行了严苛审查。460

It was a tough, long-running battle between PayPal and eBay’s payment system, and it was certainly challenging. There were times it felt like trying to win a land war in Asia. They got to set the ground rules. It was like trying to beat Microsoft inside their own operating system. It took a lot of effort to beat eBay in their own system. One of the long-term risks for PayPal was eBay would one day prevail. One way to retire that risk was to sell to eBay.461

PayPal与eBay支付系统之间的斗争漫长而艰难，确实充满挑战。有时感觉就像在亚洲打陆地战争。他们制定了游戏规则，就像试图在微软自己的操作系统内击败微软一样。要在他们自己的系统中击败eBay，需要付出巨大努力。PayPal面临的长期风险之一是eBay最终会胜出。消除这一风险的一个方法就是将公司卖给eBay。461

In October 2002, we struck a deal to sell PayPal to eBay for about $4.5 billion.462

2002年10月，我们达成协议，以约45亿美元将PayPal出售给eBay。462

I could go and buy one of the islands of the Bahamas and turn it into my personal fiefdom, but I am much more interested in trying to build and create a new company.

我可以去买巴哈马的一个岛屿，把它变成我的私人领地，但我更感兴趣的是尝试建立和创造一家新公司。

I haven’t spent my winnings.

我还没有花掉我的收益。

I’m going to put almost all of it back into a new game.463

我打算把几乎所有的钱都投入到一个新项目中。463

BUILDING TESLA打造特斯拉

Accelerating the removal of hydrocarbons from the crust and placing them in the atmosphere is unwise. This is one of the biggest problems of the twenty-first century.

加速将碳氢化合物从地壳中释放到大气中是不明智的。这是二十一世纪最大的难题之一。

Hopefully this transition to sustainable energy occurs before it’s too late.464

希望向可持续能源的转变能在为时未晚之前实现。464

THE MISSION TO PROTECT THE PLANET保护地球的使命

There’s no way we can conserve our way to a good future. We have to make energy sustainable.465

我们不可能靠节约来实现美好的未来。我们必须让能源变得可持续。465

Q: Why is the mission of Tesla so important to you?

问：为什么特斯拉的使命对你如此重要？

The overarching purpose of Tesla is to help expedite the move from a mine-and-burn hydrocarbon economy toward a solar-electric economy, which I believe to be the primary, but not exclusive, sustainable solution.466

特斯拉的总体目标是加速从采矿燃烧的碳氢化合物经济向太阳能电力经济的转变，我相信这是主要的，但不是唯一的可持续解决方案。466

Energy output is the foundation of the economy, just like sunlight is the foundation of the ecosystem. To a first approximation, a country’s goods and services production will be proportionate to its energy output.467

能源产出是经济的基础，就像阳光是生态系统的基础。大致来说，一个国家的商品和服务产出将与其能源产出成正比。467

By definition, we must at some point achieve a sustainable energy economy or we will run out of fossil fuels to burn and civilization will collapse. Given that we must get off fossil fuels anyway, and that virtually all scientists agree dramatically increasing atmospheric and oceanic carbon levels is insane…the faster we achieve sustainability, the better.468

从定义上讲，我们必须在某个时刻实现可持续能源经济，否则我们将耗尽可燃的化石燃料，文明将崩溃。既然我们无论如何都必须摆脱化石燃料，而且几乎所有科学家都同意大幅增加大气和海洋中的碳含量是疯狂的……我们越快实现可持续发展越好。468

I don’t have any fundamental dislike of hydrocarbons. I simply look at the future and say, “What is the thing that will actually work?” Using a nonrenewable resource obviously will not work.469

我并不根本反感碳氢化合物。我只是看未来，问：“什么才是真正可行的？”显然，使用不可再生资源是行不通的。469

There are time extensions on the game, but the game is going to end eventually. That should be obvious. If we rely on nonrenewables, it’s like we’re stuck in a room where the oxygen is gradually depleting. We want to get out of that room. The sooner we get out of that room, the better.470

游戏有延长时间，但终究会结束。这应该很明显。如果我们依赖不可再生资源，就像被困在一个氧气逐渐耗尽的房间里。我们想要离开那个房间。越早离开越好。470

Q: How do you respond to people who don’t see the problem with extraction?

问：你如何回应那些看不到开采问题的人？

It is dangerous to be extracting vast quantities of hydrocarbons from deep within the earth and putting them in the atmosphere. Sooner or later, something bad will happen. There are a lot of people, particularly in the US, who are vehemently against electric cars and sustainable energy. It’s quite difficult to reason with them.

从地球深处开采大量碳氢化合物并将其排放到大气中是危险的。迟早会发生不好的事情。特别是在美国，有很多人强烈反对电动汽车和可持续能源。与他们讲道理相当困难。

They’ll say, “Well, some scientists don’t think climate change is a problem.”471

他们会说，“有些科学家不认为气候变化是个问题。”471

You can find some small number of people that will disagree with anything.

你总能找到少数人反对任何事情。

This reminds me of the tobacco industry. For the longest time you’d see ads where they claimed tobacco was healthy for you. It’s hard to believe these days, but it’s true.

这让我想起了烟草行业。很长一段时间里，你会看到他们声称烟草对健康有益的广告。现在很难相信，但这是真的。

There were reports where there seemed to be a correlation between lung cancer and smoking, but tobacco companies would say, “Our scientists have conducted experiments and they show no relation at all!” It’s complete nonsense.472

有报告显示肺癌与吸烟似乎有关联，但烟草公司会说，“我们的科学家做了实验，显示两者完全无关！”这完全是胡说八道。472

Almost any reasonable scientist would say, “Yes, of course smoking causes lung cancer and all sorts of other bad things. Not definitively but it’s extremely likely.” Yet the tobacco industry would still say, “Scientists disagree!” because 1 or 2 percent of the scientific community didn’t feel that way.

几乎所有理性的科学家都会说，“是的，吸烟当然会导致肺癌和各种其他疾病。虽然不是绝对确定，但极有可能。”然而烟草行业仍会说，“科学家们意见不一！”因为有1%或2%的科学家不这么认为。

The public just hears “Scientists disagree!” rather than “99 percent of scientists think the other 1 percent are stupid.”473

公众只听到“科学家们意见不一！”而不是“99%的科学家认为那1%的人是愚蠢的。”473

The question isn’t, “Can you prove we’re making the planet warmer?” but, “Can you prove we’re not?” And you can’t.474

问题不是“你能证明我们让地球变暖了吗？”，而是“你能证明我们没有吗？”而你不能。474

It makes me mad when smart, ethical scientists are accused of publishing climate papers for “grant money.” They earn peanuts compared to their other opportunities and give that up to help the world. But their accusers earn billions by slowing down the growth of clean energy. Who is more credible?475

当聪明且有道德的科学家被指责为“为了拿资助金”而发表气候论文时，我很生气。他们的收入与其他机会相比微不足道，却放弃那些机会来帮助世界。但他们的指责者通过阻碍清洁能源的发展赚取数十亿美元。谁更可信？475

People often believe things inversely proportional to the evidence. It’s so strange.

人们往往相信与证据成反比的事情。这真奇怪。

Given a set of possible explanations, why pick the extremely unlikely one?476

面对一系列可能的解释，为什么要选择极不可能的那个？476

Q: So it’s not too late to turn things around?

问：那么现在还不晚，可以扭转局面吗？

Humanity will solve sustainable energy if we continue to push hard. The future is bright for cheap, abundant energy. It will be possible to use that energy to pull carbon out of the atmosphere. It takes a lot of energy to do that carbon capture, because putting it into the atmosphere releases energy. To pull it out, you need to use a lot of energy.

只要我们继续努力，人类将解决可持续能源问题。未来的能源将廉价且充足。我们将能够利用这些能源从大气中捕获碳。碳捕获需要大量能源，因为将碳释放到大气中会释放能量，而要将其从大气中移除，则需要消耗大量能量。

But if we’ve got a lot of sustainable energy from wind and solar, we can sequester carbon. We can reverse the CO2 parts per million of the atmosphere and oceans and restore the climate.477

但如果我们拥有大量来自风能和太阳能的可持续能源，我们就能封存碳。我们可以逆转大气和海洋中的二氧化碳浓度，恢复气候。477

I donated $100 million to a prize for the best carbon capture technology.

我捐赠了一亿美元设立最佳碳捕获技术奖。

We can create as much fresh water as we want. Earth is mostly water. We should call Earth “Water.” It’s 70 percent water by surface area. We just happen to be on the small bit that’s land. With energy, we can turn ocean water into fresh water or irrigation water at low cost.478

我们可以制造任意多的淡水。地球大部分是水。我们应该称地球为“水星”。地球表面70%是水。我们恰好处在那小块陆地上。有了能源，我们可以低成本地将海水转化为淡水或灌溉用水。478

When you burn fossil fuels, there’s all these side reactions and toxic gasses of various kinds. A lot of little particulates that are bad for your lungs. All sorts of bad things are happening, which will go away with sustainable energy. The sky will be cleaner and quieter. The future is going to be good.479

燃烧化石燃料会产生各种副反应和有毒气体，还有许多对肺部有害的微小颗粒。所有这些坏事都会随着可持续能源的使用而消失。天空将更清洁、更安静。未来会更美好。479

The purpose of Tesla was, and remains, accelerating the use of sustainable energy, so we can imagine far into the future and life is still good. That’s what “sustainable” means. It’s not some silly hippie thing—it matters for everyone.480

特斯拉的目的曾经是，现在仍然是，加速可持续能源的使用，这样我们才能畅想遥远的未来，生活依然美好。这就是“可持续”的意义。它不是某种愚蠢的嬉皮士理念——它关系到每个人。480

GOING ALL IN, AGAIN (PAYPAL EARNINGS INTO TESLA)再次全力以赴（将PayPal收益投入特斯拉）

When you have a big technology change, it tends to come from new companies.481

当出现重大技术变革时，往往来自新兴公司。481

A lot of entrepreneurial talent and financing goes to the internet. Other sectors like automotive, solar, and space don’t see new entrants. Not a lot of entrepreneurs go into those areas, and not a lot of capital goes into those startups.482

大量的创业人才和资金流向互联网。其他行业如汽车、太阳能和航天领域则鲜有新进入者。很少有创业者进入这些领域，也很少有资本投入这些初创企业。482

This is a problem because innovation tends to come from new entrants to an industry. This is Schumpeter’s idea of creative destruction. In an oligopoly, no one is forced to innovate.483 New entrants drive innovation more than anything, which is why I have devoted my efforts to building new companies in those industries.484

这是一个问题，因为创新往往来自行业的新进入者。这就是熊彼特所说的“创造性破坏”。在寡头垄断中，没有人被迫去创新。483新进入者比任何因素都更能推动创新，这也是我为何将精力投入到这些行业的新公司建设中。484

These industries require quite a bit of capital to get going. My proceeds from PayPal after tax were about $180 million. I thought I would allocate half to SpaceX, Tesla, and SolarCity. I thought that should be fine. I’d have $90 million left, and that’s…a lot, you know?485

这些行业启动需要相当多的资金。我从PayPal税后获得的收益约为1.8亿美元。我原本打算将一半资金分配给SpaceX、特斯拉和SolarCity。我觉得这样应该没问题。剩下9000万美元，这已经很多了，你知道的？485

But things cost more and took longer than I thought.486 Each one ended up costing double what I expected. I thought SpaceX would need $50 million. Tesla, I thought, would need $25 million, maybe $30 million. SolarCity…actually went really well.487

但事情的花费比我预想的更多，时间也更长。486每个项目的花费最终都是我预期的两倍。我原以为SpaceX需要5000万美元，特斯拉大概需要2500万到3000万美元。SolarCity……实际上进展得非常顺利。487

I ended up putting $10 million into Solar City, $70 million into Tesla, and $100 million into SpaceX. I literally had to borrow money for rent. It was a close call.488

我最终投入了1000万美元到SolarCity，7000万美元到特斯拉，1亿美元到SpaceX。我甚至不得不借钱来付房租。真是千钧一发。488

BUILDING THE FIRST PROTOTYPE打造第一个原型车

I first realized all vehicles would be electric in the early nineties, way before Tesla started. I was studying physics in my sophomore year in college; it was obvious to me even then.489

我最早是在九十年代初意识到所有车辆都会是电动车，那时远早于特斯拉成立。我大学二年级时在学物理；即使那时我也觉得这很明显。489

Getting the timing right was important. Lithium-ion batteries were the critical breakthrough needed for compelling electric cars. I knew it became possible to start Tesla because we went from the energy density of lead acid batteries to lithium-ion batteries—about a 4x energy density improvement. If you had a sixty-mile range with a lead-acid battery, you would have about one-hundred-forty-mile range with lithium-ion for the same weight.

把握时机非常重要。锂离子电池是制造有吸引力的电动车所需的关键突破。我知道特斯拉之所以能启动，是因为我们从铅酸电池的能量密度跃升到了锂离子电池——能量密度提高了大约4倍。如果用铅酸电池能跑六十英里，那么用锂离子电池同样重量下能跑大约一百四十英里。

A company called AC Propulsion had built a prototype, which had similar specs to what we eventually brought to market as the first Tesla Roadster. It was cool to see it in action, working. I tried hard to convince those guys to commercialize their car but they just did not want to do it.

一家叫AC Propulsion的公司曾制造过一个原型车，其规格与我们最终推出的第一款特斯拉Roadster相似。看到它运行起来很酷。我努力说服他们将车商业化，但他们就是不愿意。

The car they wanted to make was like an electric Scion. I told them, “You guys, nobody’s gonna pay $70,000 for an electric Scion. This is not gonna work. You will sell like fourteen of these things.”

他们想做的车类似一款电动Scion。我告诉他们，“你们这帮人，没人会花七万美元买一辆电动Scion。这行不通。你们大概只能卖出十四辆。”

Though I also said, “Even though I think this is the dumbest idea ever, I will fund one tenth of it if you can find nine other people.”490

不过我也说，“虽然我觉得这是最愚蠢的主意，但如果你们能找到另外九个人，我愿意出资十分之一。”490

I thought it would fail, but at least it was something. They didn’t actually get it off the ground. Eventually I said, “If you guys are not going to commercialize this, do you mind if I do?”491

我觉得它会失败，但至少是个开始。他们实际上没能启动项目。最终我说，“如果你们不打算商业化，我能不能来做？”491

We made so many mistakes in the beginning of Tesla. Almost every decision we made was wrong.492

“我们在特斯拉初期犯了太多错误。几乎每个决定都是错的。”492

The founding principles of Tesla were basically completely wrong. The premise was, “It’s not gonna be that hard! We’ll take the Lotus Elise, a nice lightweight car, and we’ll take AC Propulsion’s drive unit technology, put ’er together, and we’ll have an electric car! It’ll be great!”

特斯拉的创立原则基本上完全错误。前提是，“这不难！我们拿莲花Elise，一款轻量级好车，再用AC Propulsion的驱动单元技术，组合起来就有电动车了！这会很棒！”

Except the AC Propulsion technology could not be industrialized. It was handcrafted electronics. In hot or cold weather, it would respond differently or not at all. It was impossible to scale this technology. You could only have finicky, individually made, superexpensive prototypes.

但AC Propulsion的技术无法工业化。它是手工制作的电子设备。无论是炎热还是寒冷天气，表现都不同甚至完全失效。无法将这项技术规模化。只能做出挑剔、单独制造、超级昂贵的原型车。

I remember in the early days giving a test ride to Larry Page and Sergey Brin, whom I’ve known for a long time. There was some bug in the system and, damn it, the car would only go ten miles an hour. I was in the passenger seat saying, “Guys, I swear, it goes a lot faster than this.”

我记得早期给拉里·佩奇和谢尔盖·布林试驾时，我认识他们很久了。系统出了点故障，车速只能达到每小时十英里。我坐在副驾驶座上说，“伙计们，我发誓它能跑得更快。”

They were kind enough to put a little investment into the company, despite the world’s worst demo.493

尽管演示是世界上最糟糕的，他们还是好心地对公司进行了少量投资。493

It was just a flat-out burning dumpster fire of stupidity. One example: The chassis had to be redesigned to fit the battery pack and became 40 percent heavier. This invalidated the crash testing Lotus had done.494

这简直是彻头彻尾的愚蠢灾难。举个例子：为了安装电池组，底盘不得不重新设计，重量增加了40%。这使得莲花之前做的碰撞测试失效了。494

We ended up using none of the AC Propulsion technology. Something that looks cool and works as an individual prototype does not necessarily scale. Eventually maybe 7 percent of the parts of the original Tesla Roadster were common with the Lotus Elise. It would have been much smarter to start with a clean-sheet design and not try to modify something else.495

我们最终没有使用任何AC Propulsion的技术。看起来酷且能作为单个原型车工作的东西，不一定能规模化。最终，原始特斯拉Roadster的零件中只有大约7%与莲花Elise通用。要是从零设计而不是改装别的车，会聪明得多。495

I was going to start an electric vehicle (EV) company with JB Straubel based on the AC Propulsion prototype. When I asked AC Propulsion if it was okay to do that, they said, “There are some other people who want to create an EV company; would you like to join forces with them?” I said, “OK.” That was a huge mistake. JB and I should have just started the company ourselves. My default inclination is to start things from scratch, even more so after this experience.496

我本打算和JB Straubel基于AC Propulsion的原型车一起创办电动车公司。当我问AC Propulsion是否可以时，他们说，“还有其他人想创办电动车公司，你愿意和他们合伙吗？”我说，“好吧。”这是个大错误。JB和我本该自己单独创办公司。我的默认倾向是从零开始，经历这次后更是如此。496

The most important thing is to start somewhere, be prepared to question your assumptions, fix what you did wrong, and adapt to reality.497

“最重要的是从某处开始，准备质疑你的假设，修正错误，适应现实。”497

BECOMING TESLA’S CEO成为特斯拉的首席执行官

The actual error was me trying to have my cake and eat it too: I just wanted to work on the technology and the product. I thought someone else could be the CEO and run the business, because I just like working on technology, product, and design. I was also doing SpaceX at the time, and our rockets were exploding.

真正的错误是我想既吃蛋糕又保留蛋糕：我只想专注于技术和产品。我以为可以让别人当CEO来经营公司，因为我只喜欢做技术、产品和设计。那时我还在做SpaceX，我们的火箭一直在爆炸。

I had always wanted to build an electric car company. I thought this is how I could have my cake and eat it too. That was a huge mistake and fundamentally a moral error. When I joined, there were no employees. There was no intellectual property. There was no prototype, no nothing.498

我一直想创办一家电动汽车公司。我以为这样我就能既吃蛋糕又保留蛋糕。这是一个巨大的错误，根本上也是道德上的错误。刚加入时，公司没有员工，没有知识产权，没有原型，什么都没有。498

I had provided about 95 percent of the money for Tesla, so I could become CEO anytime I wanted. And in the end, I had to frickin’ become CEO of Tesla. I didn’t want to be, but it was either that or the company was going to die.499

我为特斯拉提供了大约95%的资金，所以我随时都可以成为CEO。最终，我不得不他妈的成为特斯拉的CEO。我并不想当，但要么这样，要么公司就会死掉。499

I never wanted to be a CEO, but I learned you could not truly be the chief technology or product officer unless you were the CEO.500

“我从未想过要当CEO，但我学到了，除非你是CEO，否则你无法真正成为首席技术官或首席产品官。500”

Being CEO of two startups at the same time was not appealing. It shouldn’t be appealing, by the way, for anyone thinking it was a good idea. It was a terrible idea.501

同时担任两家初创公司的CEO并不吸引人。顺便说一句，任何认为这是个好主意的人都不应该觉得吸引人。这是个糟糕的主意。501

If you’re a CEO of a company, the chore level is high, and if you don’t do your chores then the company goes to hell. Frankly, I hate doing chores—who doesn’t? There’s a whole bunch of personnel and legal issues: things I don’t find enjoyable to work on, but if I don’t work on them, the company suffers. The sheer volume of work is insane.502

如果你是公司的CEO，琐事很多，如果你不处理这些琐事，公司就会陷入困境。坦白说，我讨厌做琐事——谁不讨厌呢？有很多人事和法律问题：这些我不喜欢处理，但如果我不处理，公司就会受影响。工作量之大简直疯狂。502

The perception of me as a businessperson is fine, but my time is spent almost entirely with the engineering team. I’m a physics guy, an engineer. I’d prefer to be working on products. I do the business stuff because you have to do the business stuff. If you don’t do the business stuff, somebody else could do it for you and then you could be in trouble.503

别人对我作为商人的看法还可以，但我几乎所有时间都花在工程团队上。我是个物理学家，工程师。我更愿意做产品。我做商业事务是因为必须做。如果你不做，别人会替你做，那你可能会有麻烦。503

I have a habit of biting off more than I can chew and just sitting there with chipmunk cheeks.504

“我有咬得比自己能嚼的还多，然后就像花栗鼠一样鼓着脸颊坐着的习惯。504”

SEQUENCED STRATEGY OF TESLA特斯拉的分阶段战略

Tesla is focused on making electric cars more affordable, which is tough. You need high volume to make cars affordable. When we started, other car companies made a lot more cars, so they got much better economies of scale. As we build more cars in higher volume, we make electric cars available to a wider range of people at lower cost.505

特斯拉专注于让电动汽车更实惠，这非常困难。你需要大规模生产才能降低汽车价格。刚开始时，其他汽车公司生产的汽车数量远远超过我们，因此他们拥有更好的规模经济。随着我们生产更多汽车，产量提高，我们能够以更低的成本让更多人买得起电动汽车。505

The first version of a product has both a new-technology problem and a low-volume problem. You want to make your mistakes at a small scale, work the bugs out of the system, then reach for scale.506

产品的第一个版本既面临新技术问题，也面临低产量问题。你希望在小规模上犯错，解决系统中的漏洞，然后再追求规模化。506

With a new product, the first thing engineers try to do is make it work. After you make it work, then you optimize and optimize and optimize.507

对于新产品，工程师首先要做的是让它能正常工作。让它正常工作后，再不断优化、优化、优化。507

The master plan was:

总体规划是：

(While doing above, also provide zero-emission electric power generation options.)508

（在上述过程中，同时提供零排放的电力发电方案。）508

We had to start off with step one, because it was all I could afford with what I made from PayPal. I thought our chances of success were so low, I didn’t want to risk anyone’s funds but my own in the beginning.509

我们必须从第一步开始，因为那是我用PayPal赚的钱所能承担的全部。我认为成功的机会非常低，起初我不想冒用任何人的资金风险，只愿意用自己的钱。509

The list of successful car startups is short. The number of American car companies that haven’t gone bankrupt is a grand total of two: Ford and Tesla. Starting a car company is idiotic, and an electric car company is idiocy squared.510

成功的汽车创业公司屈指可数。美国未破产的汽车公司总共只有两家：福特和特斯拉。创办汽车公司是愚蠢的，而创办电动汽车公司则是愚蠢的平方。510

The idea is to drive to mass market as rapidly as possible, at the pace technology matures.511

“我们的理念是以技术成熟的速度，尽快进入大众市场。”511

As a little startup building a car, there was no way we could afford a giant plant to make hundreds of thousands of cars a year. That’s the kind of volume you need to make cheap cars, and that plant would cost a billion dollars we didn’t have.

作为一家小型创业公司，我们根本负担不起建造一个年产数十万辆汽车的巨大工厂。要生产廉价汽车需要这样的产量，而那样的工厂造价高达十亿美元，我们根本没有那笔钱。

Our first car was a sports car, not because we think the world lacks a sports car, but because it was the right entry point for the market. If you have a new technology, the right place to enter is high unit-cost, low unit-volume.512

我们的第一款车是一款跑车，并不是因为我们认为世界缺少跑车，而是因为这是进入市场的正确切入点。如果你有一项新技术，正确的切入点是高单价、低产量的市场。512

A low-volume car means a much smaller, simpler factory with most things done by hand. Without economies of scale, anything we built would be expensive whether it was an economy sedan or a sports car. Some people would be prepared to pay a high price for a sports car. No one would pay $100K for an electric Honda Civic, no matter how cool it looked.513

低产量的汽车意味着工厂规模更小、更简单，大部分工作由手工完成。没有规模经济，无论是经济型轿车还是跑车，我们制造的任何东西都会很贵。有些人愿意为跑车支付高价，但没人会为一辆电动本田思域支付10万美元，不管它看起来多酷。513

When a new cell phone or a new laptop comes out, they tend to be expensive at first, because they’re figuring out the issues and it takes time to optimize. Over time, with scale, that new technology becomes cheaper and cheaper.514

当一款新手机或新笔记本电脑推出时，起初通常价格较高，因为他们还在解决问题并需要时间优化。随着时间推移和规模扩大，这项新技术会变得越来越便宜。514

That is the unique and important thing Tesla accomplished. It is not the design of an electric vehicle, an electric vehicle prototype, or even low-volume production of a car. The hard part is not creating a prototype or going into limited production. There have been hundreds of car startups over the years that got that far.515

这是特斯拉独特且重要的成就。这不仅仅是设计一辆电动车、电动车原型，甚至不是低产量生产汽车。难点不在于制造原型或进行有限生产。多年来已有数百家汽车创业公司做到这一步。515

The difficult thing—which has not been accomplished by an American car company in one hundred years—is reaching volume production without going bankrupt. That is the actual hard thing. The last American car company to reach volume production without going bankrupt was Chrysler. That was in the 1920s.516

真正困难的——一百年来没有一家美国汽车公司做到的——是实现大规模生产而不破产。这才是真正的难点。上一次美国汽车公司实现大规模生产且未破产还是克莱斯勒，那是在1920年代。516

KEEPING TESLA ALIVE让特斯拉活下去

Tesla is alive by the skin of its teeth. So is SpaceX. If things had gone a little bit the other way, both companies would be dead.

特斯拉靠着极其微弱的优势活了下来。SpaceX也是如此。如果情况稍有不同，这两家公司都会倒闭。

Man, this was grim. I thought this was going to turn into a tale of warning for hubris.517

天哪，情况非常严峻。我原以为这会变成一个关于傲慢的警示故事。517

In 2008, SolarCity made a deal with Morgan Stanley, and Morgan Stanley had to renege on the deal because they themselves were running out of money. For a while it looked like all three companies were going to die. I was also going through a divorce. In addition to all that, I was getting dumped on, massively, in the press. That was definitely a low point.518

2008年，SolarCity与摩根士丹利达成了一笔交易，但摩根士丹利不得不违约，因为他们自己也快没钱了。有一段时间，看起来这三家公司都会倒闭。那时我还在经历离婚。除此之外，媒体对我进行了大规模的抨击。那绝对是低谷时期。518

In the midst of this, I faced one of the most difficult choices of my whole life. Tesla and SpaceX were both on the brink of bankruptcy.

在这期间，我面临了人生中最艰难的选择之一。特斯拉和SpaceX都濒临破产。

I had maybe $30 million or $40 million left. I had two choices: I could put it all into one company, and the other would definitely die. I could split it between Tesla and SpaceX—but if I split it between the two, they both might die.

我大概只剩下三千万到四千万美元。我有两个选择：可以把所有钱投入一家，另一家肯定会死；也可以把钱分给特斯拉和SpaceX——但如果分开投，两家可能都会死。

When you put your blood, sweat, and tears into creating something, building something important—it’s like a child.

当你倾注血汗心血去创造、建设重要的东西时——它就像一个孩子。

Which one will I let starve to death?

我会让哪一个饿死呢？

I couldn’t bring myself to do it.

我无法下定决心去做出选择。

So, I split the money between the two.519

所以，我把钱分给了两家公司。519

Trying to raise money for a startup electric car company in 2008 while GM was going bankrupt was difficult, to say the least. Eventually a subset of the previous investors came in, which included Antonio Gracias, Steve Jurvetson, and Aaron Price, all whom I hold a debt of gratitude to. They said they would invest as much as I put in, so I put in everything. All the money I had left. Literally everything. I didn’t have a house. I was staying in Jeff Skoll’s spare bedroom.

2008年，在通用汽车破产的背景下，为一家初创电动汽车公司筹集资金，至少可以说是非常困难。最终，一部分之前的投资者加入了进来，包括Antonio Gracias、Steve Jurvetson和Aaron Price，我对他们心存感激。他们说会投入与我相同数额的资金，于是我投入了所有剩余的钱。字面意思上的全部。我没有房子，住在Jeff Skoll的备用卧室里。

(Note from Eric: At this point, Steve Jurvetson might interject with this context about entrepreneurial heroism.

（Eric注：此时，Steve Jurvetson可能会插入关于创业英雄主义的背景说明。）

“Elon wrote a check for the entire remainder of his personal wealth to save the company, covering payroll during Christmas when no one else would. The economy was in a bad situation. Goldman just failed a private offering. It was the middle of the financial crisis.

“Elon开出了一张支票，投入了他个人财富的全部剩余，用以拯救公司，支付圣诞节期间的工资，当时没人愿意这样做。经济形势很糟糕。高盛刚刚失败了一次私募发行。正值金融危机中期。

Tesla did not look appealing, either. There was no DOE loan. There was no Daimler deal. The car had a negative gross margin. Oh, and the largest shareholder was pissed off, going AWOL. It was ugly.”)520

特斯拉看起来也毫无吸引力。没有能源部贷款，没有戴姆勒的交易。汽车毛利为负。哦，最大股东还愤怒地失联了。情况非常糟糕。”)520

We closed that round at 6:00 p.m. on Christmas Eve 2008. It was the last hour of the last day possible. We would have bounced payroll two days after Christmas.

我们在2008年圣诞节前夕下午6点完成了那轮融资。那是最后一天的最后一小时。圣诞节后两天，我们就无法支付工资了。

Eventually, Daimler executives came to visit. They showed up expecting a PowerPoint. While they were on their way over, we dropped a Roadster motor and battery into a Smart Car and let them drive it. They were shocked when it hit 60 mph in four seconds. Daimler ended up investing $50 million. If Daimler had not invested in Tesla at that time, we would have died.521

最终，戴姆勒的高管们来访。他们本期待看到一份PPT。在他们来的路上，我们把Roadster的电机和电池装进了一辆Smart Car，让他们试驾。当车速四秒内达到60英里时，他们震惊了。戴姆勒最终投资了5000万美元。如果当时戴姆勒没有投资特斯拉，我们就会死掉。521

It’s definitely stressful when death is inches from your face, trying to eat your face off—like right there and the foam is spattering on you.522

当死亡近在咫尺，试图撕咬你的脸——就在你面前，泡沫四溅时，压力绝对巨大。522

For a while all I could think was, “We need to live. How do we live?”523

有一段时间，我脑海里只有一句话：“我们必须活下去。我们怎么活？”523

At both SpaceX and Tesla in 2008, if we’d paid our suppliers on time we would have gone bankrupt immediately.524

2008年，在SpaceX和特斯拉，如果我们按时支付供应商款项，我们会立刻破产。524

THE EDGE OF SANITY理智的边缘

Q: How do you prioritize with so many things going on at once?

问：在同时处理这么多事情的情况下，你如何确定优先顺序？

Prioritizing has usually been out of desperation, not selection. It’s not, “Oh, let’s sit back and leisurely decide how we shall spend these resources.” It’s, “This isn’t working, if we don’t make it work, we’re gonna go bankrupt, so we better make it work.”

确定优先顺序通常是出于绝望，而非选择。不是“哦，让我们坐下来悠闲地决定如何分配这些资源”，而是“这行不通，如果我们不让它行得通，我们就会破产，所以我们必须让它行得通。”

We messed up almost every aspect of the Model 3 production line. There were so many mistakes, the entire company had to be devoted to fixing them. We took everyone off every other project—we all started working on the Model 3. We had to make it work or there wouldn’t be any more Tesla.525

我们几乎把Model 3生产线的每个方面都搞砸了。错误太多了，整个公司都必须投入到修复中。我们让所有人暂停其他项目——大家都开始专注于Model 3。我们必须让它成功，否则就没有特斯拉了。525

There was no choice. We had to get to high-volume production. It was a chicken-and-egg situation. You can’t make the car at an affordable price unless you have high volume. Without high volume, you can’t sell at an affordable price. So what do you do?526

别无选择。我们必须实现大规模生产。这是一个先有鸡还是先有蛋的局面。没有大规模生产，你无法以负担得起的价格制造汽车。没有负担得起的价格，你也卖不出去。那么怎么办呢？526

You take a giant flying leap at high volume and hope you can grab a cliff ledge with your fingertips.527

你必须奋力一跃，冲向大规模生产，希望能用指尖抓住悬崖边缘。527

I felt like Indiana Jones running through the temple. There’s a huge boulder chasing you and you need to jump across a giant pit in the ground. If you slow down, the boulder will crush you, and if you don’t make the leap, you’ll die in the pit. That’s prioritizing.528

我感觉自己像印第安纳·琼斯在穿越神庙。有一块巨石在追赶你，你需要跳过一个巨大的深坑。如果你放慢脚步，巨石会压死你；如果你跳不过去，你会掉进坑里死去。这就是确定优先顺序的感觉。528

Q: How did you motivate the team to do whatever it takes?

问：你是如何激励团队不惜一切代价完成任务的？

I told them we had to go ultrahardcore. They had to prepare for a level of intensity greater than anything they had experienced before.529

我告诉他们我们必须变得极端强硬。他们必须为比以往任何时候都更高强度的工作做好准备。529

I lived in the Fremont and Nevada factories for three years fixing that production line, running around like a maniac through every part of that factory, living with the team. I slept on the floor so the team going through a hard time could see me on the floor and know I was not in some ivory tower. Whatever pain they experienced, I had more.530

我在弗里蒙特和内华达工厂住了三年，修复那条生产线，像疯子一样跑遍工厂的每个角落，与团队同甘共苦。我睡在地板上，让经历困难的团队成员看到我就在他们身边，而不是高高在上的象牙塔里。他们经历的痛苦，我经历得更多。530

I worked to the edge of sanity. There wasn’t any other way to make it work but three years of hell. From 2017 to 2019, I experienced the longest period of excruciating pain in my life. There wasn’t any other way, and we still barely made it. We were on the ragged edge of bankruptcy the entire time. Three years of pain, but it had to be done or Tesla would be dead.531

我工作到了理智的边缘。除了经历三年的地狱般生活，没有别的办法让它成功。从2017年到2019年，我经历了人生中最长时间的极度痛苦。别无选择，我们几乎撑了过来。那段时间我们一直处于破产的边缘。三年的痛苦，但必须完成，否则特斯拉就完了。531

At this point, I think I know more about manufacturing than anyone currently alive on Earth.

到现在为止，我想我比地球上任何活着的人都更了解制造业。

I can tell you how every damn part in that car is made. That’s what happens when you live in the factory for three years.532

我可以告诉你那辆车上每一个零件是如何制造的。这就是在工厂里住了三年的结果。532

A WHOLE NEW KIND OF CAR COMPANY一种全新的汽车公司

Ultimately, what we induce other companies to do will have a greater impact than the cars we make ourselves.533

最终，我们促使其他公司采取的行动，其影响将远大于我们自己制造的汽车。533

Q: A Tesla is a very different product, but how different is Tesla as a company from a traditional auto company?

问：特斯拉是一种非常不同的产品，但作为一家公司，特斯拉与传统汽车公司有多大不同？

A typical car company manages a supply chain, assembles vehicles, and sends them to dealers. They might make the engine, but most of the actual technology development and parts manufacturing is done by suppliers. Most of the vehicle software is done by suppliers too. The amount of “real work” done by car companies like GM or Ford is not as much as you think. They don’t do sales or service either; dealerships do.534

典型的汽车公司管理供应链，组装车辆，并将其发送给经销商。他们可能制造发动机，但大部分实际的技术开发和零部件制造都是由供应商完成的。大多数车辆软件也是由供应商完成的。像通用汽车或福特这样的汽车公司所做的“真正工作”并不像你想象的那么多。他们也不负责销售或服务；这些由经销商完成。534

At Tesla, we do our own sales and service. We don’t have dealerships.535 I have made it a principle within Tesla that we should never attempt to make service a profit center. It does not seem right to me when companies make a profit off customers when their product breaks.536 The best way to experience service is, of course, to not need service.537

在特斯拉，我们自己负责销售和服务。我们没有经销商。535 我在特斯拉内部确立了一个原则，那就是我们绝不应将服务作为盈利中心。当公司的产品出现故障时，从客户身上赚钱对我来说是不对的。536 体验服务的最好方式当然是不需要服务。537

If we charge for something, it is not because we want to make things more expensive; it’s because we can’t figure out how to make it less expensive.538

如果我们收费，并不是因为我们想让东西变得更贵；而是因为我们还没找到让它更便宜的方法。538

There’s a lot of vertical integration at Tesla. We make the battery pack, the power electronics, and the drivetrain ourselves. We’re vertically integrated because the pace we needed to move was much faster than the supply chain could move. To the degree you rely on the legacy supply chain, you inherit the legacy constraints—including their speed, costs, and technology.

特斯拉有大量的垂直整合。我们自己制造电池组、电力电子和驱动系统。我们之所以垂直整合，是因为我们需要的推进速度远远快于供应链的速度。你越依赖传统供应链，就越会继承其限制——包括速度、成本和技术。

We do car insurance now, too. Car insurance is a bigger deal than it may seem. A lot of people are paying 30–40 percent as much as their car lease payment for insurance. The car insurance industry is incredibly inefficient because they’ve got all these middlemen, from the insurance agent all the way to the final reinsurer. There are a half dozen companies each taking a cut.539

我们现在也做汽车保险。汽车保险比看上去更重要。很多人支付的保险费相当于他们汽车租赁费的30%到40%。汽车保险行业效率极低，因为中间环节太多，从保险代理人到最终的再保险公司，有半打公司各自抽取一部分利润。539

Insurance is driven by statistics, so even if you’re a good driver at twenty years old, it’s extremely expensive. Tesla allows for real-time insurance based on how you actually drive the car. If you drive the car in a safer way, you pay lower insurance. Our insurance is based on how you drive, not how people who fit your demographic have driven historically.540

保险是基于统计数据的，所以即使你20岁开车技术很好，保险费也非常昂贵。特斯拉允许基于你实际驾驶方式的实时保险。如果你开车更安全，保险费就更低。我们的保险基于你的驾驶行为，而不是基于与你人口统计特征相符的人过去的驾驶记录。540

Tesla is as much a software company as a hardware company. The software in a Tesla operates the car, the screen, the charging…all developed by us. Then, Tesla built an autopilot AI team from scratch, the best real-world AI team on Earth. We also built a chip team too, because the hardware we could buy wasn’t capable of running our AI software.541

特斯拉既是软件公司，也是硬件公司。特斯拉的软件控制汽车、屏幕、充电……所有这些都是我们自己开发的。随后，特斯拉从零开始组建了自动驾驶AI团队，这是地球上最优秀的现实世界AI团队。我们还组建了芯片团队，因为市面上的硬件无法运行我们的AI软件。541

Q: How important is the look and design of the cars?

问：汽车的外观和设计有多重要？

The value of beauty and inspiration is underrated, no question.542

美感和灵感的价值被低估了，毫无疑问。542

If you want to make something beautiful, you must trigger whatever fundamental aesthetic algorithms there are. Our brains have some intrinsic elements that represent beauty, which trigger the emotion of appreciation of beauty in our mind.

如果你想制造美丽的东西，必须触发某些基本的审美算法。我们的大脑有一些内在元素代表美，这些元素会激发我们内心对美的欣赏情感。

I think these are relatively consistent among people. Not completely. Not everyone likes exactly the same thing, but there’s a lot of commonality. It is important to combine aesthetic design with functionality.

我认为这些在不同人之间相对一致。不是完全一致。不是每个人都喜欢完全相同的东西，但有很多共性。将美学设计与功能性结合起来非常重要。

What was hard about the Model S and Model X was to combine aesthetics and utility, to balance the two. You can make a car look good by giving it certain proportions—making it low and slim. But if you do that, the utility is significantly affected. The big challenge is trying to figure out how to get five adults plus two kids in a seven-seater with high utility and keep it looking good. To make a sports car look good is relatively easy. But to make a sedan or an SUV look good is quite difficult.543

Model S和Model X的难点在于将美学和实用性结合，平衡两者。你可以通过赋予汽车某些比例——让它低矮且修长——来让它看起来好看。但如果这样做，实用性会大大受影响。最大的挑战是如何让五个成年人加两个孩子在一辆七座车里既有高实用性，又保持外观美观。让跑车好看相对容易，但让轿车或SUV好看则相当困难。543

Another incredibly important design principle is to have it feel bigger on the inside than it looks on the outside. That’s also a hard thing to do.544

另一个极其重要的设计原则是让车内空间感觉比外观看起来更大。这也是一件难事。544

Most people don’t consciously notice the small details, but they do subconsciously. Your mind takes in an overall impression. You know if something is appealing or not, even though you may not be able to point out exactly why. That sense is a summation of many details. Most of us experience this as “that’s ugly,” or “that’s beautiful,” or “wow, that’s elegant,” but can’t break down why.545

大多数人不会有意识地注意到细节，但潜意识会注意。你的大脑会形成整体印象。你知道某物是否吸引人，尽管你可能说不出具体原因。这种感觉是许多细节的综合。我们大多数人会用“丑”或“美”或“哇，真优雅”来形容，但无法拆解原因。545

You can train yourself. You can make yourself pay attention to “why.” You can learn to bring subconscious awareness into conscious awareness. Look closely and carefully. Look at each object’s geometry.546

你可以训练自己。你可以让自己关注“为什么”。你可以学会将潜意识的感知转化为意识。仔细认真地观察。观察每个物体的几何形状。546

Pay attention to the little details. Train yourself to notice them. Notice the nuances of design, shape, form, function, and the way it looks in different lights. Anyone can do this, although it is a double-edged sword, because then you always notice all the little things. Now when something’s off—even a little thing—it drives me bananas.

注意小细节。训练自己去发现它们。注意设计、形状、形式、功能以及在不同光线下的外观细微差别。任何人都可以做到，尽管这是一把双刃剑，因为你会一直注意到所有小细节。现在只要有一点点不对劲——哪怕是小事——都会让我抓狂。

If you’re trying to make a perfect product, attention to detail is essential.547

如果你想做出完美的产品，关注细节是必不可少的。547

GIVE PEOPLE MORE FOR LESS以更低的成本为人们提供更多

Revolutionizing industries is not for the faint of heart.548

颠覆行业不是胆小者能做的事。548

Q: How do you think about iterating through products to reach a mass market?

问：你如何看待通过产品迭代来实现大众市场？

The nature of new technology adoption tends to follow an S-curve. People underpredict it in the beginning, because they tend to extrapolate trends in a straight line. Then they’ll overpredict it at the midpoint during massive growth. It will take longer than people think at the midpoint, but much shorter than people think at the beginning.549

新技术的采用通常遵循S型曲线。人们一开始往往低估它，因为他们倾向于将趋势直线外推。然后在大规模增长的中期会高估它。中期所需时间比人们想象的要长，但比起开始阶段要短得多。549

One way to look at technology is like rendering an image in successive levels of detail. The first layer of the image is very blurry and things are out of place. Then with the next pass, it gets a bit more defined and things start to shift into place. And you do another pass and another pass, and eventually it’s refined and actually works.550 It generally takes three major iterations of any major new technology to have it work really, really well.551

看待技术的一种方式就像逐层渲染图像。第一层图像非常模糊，很多东西都不在位。下一遍渲染时，图像会更清晰，事物开始归位。再一遍又一遍，最终图像被细化并且真正可用。550任何重大新技术通常需要三次主要迭代才能真正运行良好。551

Progress comes from design and technology improvements as well as scale. Look at the earliest cell phones. In the original Wall Street movie in 1987, the guy’s walking down the beach with a giant phone, carrying a briefcase to power it. It had like thirty minutes of battery life. Without technology improvements, no amount of money or scale could have made that phone affordable. It took a lot of engineering and design iterations.552

进步来自设计和技术改进以及规模。看看最早的手机。1987年电影《华尔街》中，主角拿着一个巨大的手机，提着一个公文包为它供电。电池续航只有大约三十分钟。没有技术进步，再多的钱和规模也无法让那部手机变得负担得起。它经历了大量的工程和设计迭代。552

In the early days of cell phones, laptops, and gasoline cars, they were considered toys for rich people. You need to go through this phase of having an expensive car available to few in order to build the low-cost car available to many. The first version is simply about making the new technology work. Then, you work to optimize.553

在手机、笔记本电脑和汽油车的早期，它们被认为是富人的玩具。你必须经历这个阶段——只有少数人能买得起昂贵的汽车，才能制造出低成本、普及的汽车。第一版只是让新技术能用起来。然后，你开始优化。553

We’re probably on the thirtieth version of a cell phone, and with each successive design iteration we add more capability. We integrate more parts and figure out better ways to produce it so it gets both better and cheaper. Progress in any new technology takes multiple versions and a large production volume to make it affordable.554

我们现在可能已经到了手机的第三十个版本，每次设计迭代都会增加更多功能。我们整合更多零件，找到更好的生产方式，使产品既更好又更便宜。任何新技术的进步都需要多个版本和大规模生产才能实现负担得起。554

Air travel used to be accessible to only a few people. It was insanely expensive and dangerous. Now it is common to fly. The first TVs were rare and expensive. Then big, flat-screen plasma TVs used to be extremely expensive. Now, you can buy an amazing flat-screen plasma for two hundred bucks. It’s amazing.555

航空旅行曾经只有少数人能享受，既昂贵又危险。现在飞行已很普遍。最初的电视机稀有且昂贵。大尺寸平板等离子电视曾极其昂贵。现在，你可以用两百美元买到一台出色的平板等离子电视，令人惊叹。555

This is also true for electric cars. The strategy for Tesla was to enter at the high end of the market with the Roadster, where customers are prepared to pay a premium. Then move as fast as possible to higher volume and lower prices with each successive model.556

电动汽车也是如此。特斯拉的策略是先以高端市场的Roadster切入，客户愿意支付溢价。然后尽快通过每一代车型实现更高销量和更低价格。556

The Model S was a sporty four-door family car at roughly half the price point of the Roadster. Then the Model 3 was even more affordable. All free cash flow was plowed back into R&D to drive down costs and bring the next products to market as fast as possible. When someone bought the Tesla Roadster, they were helping pay for development of the low-cost family car.557

Model S是一款运动型四门家用车，价格大约是Roadster的一半。随后Model 3更为实惠。所有自由现金流都投入研发，以降低成本并尽快推出下一款产品。当有人购买特斯拉Roadster时，他们实际上是在帮助开发低成本家用车。557

Q: Any other unique aspects about Tesla’s product philosophy?

问：特斯拉的产品理念还有哪些独特之处？

Focus on signal over noise. A lot of companies get confused. They spend a lot of money on things that don’t actually make the product better. At Tesla, we put all the money into research and development, manufacturing, and design to try and make the cars as good as possible. For any company, ask, “Are the efforts we’re expending resulting in a better product or service?” If they’re not, stop those efforts.558

专注于信号而非噪音。很多公司容易迷失方向，花大量资金在并未真正提升产品的事情上。在特斯拉，我们把所有资金投入研发、制造和设计，力求让汽车尽善尽美。任何公司都应问：“我们付出的努力是否带来了更好的产品或服务？”如果没有，就停止这些努力。558

Also, go for extreme levels of precision. One of the examples we use at Tesla is LEGO blocks. LEGO is super precise. The press-fit comes down to a quarter millimeter or less, and each one is exactly the same. LEGO doesn’t work if the press-fit is too soft or too hard. If it’s too soft, the press-and-click won’t stick; if it’s too hard, you can’t get it on. They can make something that is a tiny fraction of a millimeter accurate and it’s a low-cost plastic toy. If LEGO can be that precise, so can a car.559

此外，要追求极致的精度。特斯拉举的一个例子是乐高积木。乐高非常精确，卡合度误差在四分之一毫米以内，每个积木都完全相同。乐高如果卡合度太松或太紧就无法正常使用。太松时无法卡住，太紧时无法装上。它们能做到毫米级别的精度，而且是低成本的塑料玩具。如果乐高能做到如此精确，汽车也能做到。559

THE BATTLE OF PUBLIC PERCEPTION公众认知之战

It should puzzle you that a Tesla crash resulting in a broken ankle is front-page news, yet the many thousands of people who die in US auto accidents each year get almost no coverage.560

一辆特斯拉车祸导致脚踝骨折却成为头条新闻，而美国每年因车祸死亡的数千人几乎没有报道，这应该让你感到困惑。560

Q: How do you stay positive when faced with public attacks and misrepresentation, without becoming resentful or cynical?561

问：面对公众的攻击和误解时，你如何保持积极，而不变得怨恨或愤世嫉俗？561

It does get me down at times. It makes me sad. But at some point you realize these attacks are by people who don’t know you and their goal is to generate clicks. If you can detach yourself emotionally (which is not easy) and say, “This person does not know me. They’re just writing to get clicks” then it doesn’t hurt as much.562

有时确实会让我感到沮丧，也会让我难过。但在某个时刻，你会意识到这些攻击来自不了解你的人，他们的目的只是为了吸引点击。如果你能情感上抽离（这并不容易），并告诉自己，“这个人不了解我，他们只是为了点击而写”，那么伤害就会小很多。562

It also helps to stay focused on our mission. Tesla’s motivation remains to make electric transport as affordable as possible. That informs all our actions.563

专注于我们的使命也很有帮助。特斯拉的动力始终是让电动交通尽可能负担得起。这指导着我们所有的行动。563

We put all our money and effort into trying to make the product as compelling as possible. The way to sell any product is through word of mouth. The key is to have a product people love. People will talk about the things they love.564

我们将所有资金和努力都投入到尽可能让产品具有吸引力上。销售任何产品的方式都是口碑。关键是拥有让人喜爱的产品。人们会谈论他们喜欢的东西。564

That generates real word of mouth, and that’s how our sales have grown. We’re not spending money on advertising, public relations, or endorsements. Anyone who buys our car bought it because they like the car, not their impression of the car.565

这产生了真正的口碑，这就是我们销售增长的方式。我们不花钱做广告、公关或代言。任何购买我们汽车的人都是因为喜欢这辆车，而不是他们对这辆车的印象。565

Tesla does not advertise or pay for endorsements. Instead, we use that money to make the product great.566

“特斯拉不做广告，也不付费请代言。相反，我们用这笔钱来打造卓越的产品。”566

There are over a million internal combustion engine car fires per year resulting in thousands of deaths, but one Tesla car fire with no injuries gets the biggest headlines. Why the double standard?567

每年有超过一百万起内燃机汽车起火事故，导致数千人死亡，但一辆特斯拉起火且无人受伤却成为最大头条。为何存在双重标准？567

The problem is journalists are under constant pressure to get maximum clicks. They either earn advertising dollars or get fired. It’s a tricky situation, since Tesla doesn’t advertise and fossil fuel companies and legacy car companies are among the world’s biggest advertisers.568

问题是记者们面临着获取最大点击量的持续压力。他们要么赚取广告费，要么被解雇。这是个棘手的局面，因为特斯拉不做广告，而化石燃料公司和传统汽车公司是世界上最大的广告主之一。568

Regulators tend to pay a disproportionate amount of attention to whatever is in the press. This is an objective fact. And Tesla generates a lot of press. In the United States, there are about forty thousand automotive deaths per year. But if there are four in a Tesla, they’ll probably receive a thousand times more press than any other incident.569

监管机构往往对媒体报道的内容给予过度关注。这是客观事实。特斯拉产生了大量媒体关注。在美国，每年约有四万人死于汽车事故。但如果特斯拉发生四起事故，其报道量可能是其他事故的一千倍。569

The reality is a Tesla, like most electric cars, is over 500 percent less likely to catch fire than gas combustion engine cars, which carry massive amounts of highly flammable fuel. Why is this never mentioned?570

事实上，特斯拉和大多数电动车相比，起火概率比燃油车低超过500%，而燃油车携带大量高度易燃燃料。为何这一点从未被提及？570

FOUNDING SOLARCITY创立SolarCity

Q: How did SolarCity fit into the vision for Tesla?

问：SolarCity如何融入特斯拉的愿景？

The earth is almost entirely solar powered today. The only reason we’re not a frozen ice ball at three degrees Kelvin is the sun. The sun is responsible for precipitation. It’s responsible for the vast majority of the ecosystem. We could say the entire earth is solar powered. That may not be super obvious to people. Also, the energy we consume to power civilization today is actually a tiny amount of the total energy the sun sends in our direction.571

地球如今几乎完全依靠太阳能。我们之所以没有变成零下三开尔文的冰球，唯一的原因就是太阳。太阳负责降水，负责绝大多数生态系统。我们可以说整个地球都是太阳能驱动的。虽然这对很多人来说可能并不十分明显。此外，我们今天用来支撑文明的能源，其实只是太阳向我们方向发送的总能量中的极小一部分。571

I wouldn’t say there was a particular epiphany to start SolarCity. I was at Burning Man with my cousins. They were thinking about what company to start after their first startup, called Everdream, sold to Dell. They built software that enabled companies to manage tens of thousands of computers.572

我不会说创立SolarCity是某个特别的顿悟。我当时和表兄弟们一起去Burning Man。他们在考虑在他们的第一家创业公司Everdream被戴尔收购后，下一步要做什么。Everdream开发的软件让公司能够管理成千上万台计算机。572

I was trying to convince them to do solar, because I thought it was an area that needed really good entrepreneurs. I was already somewhat overcommitted at the time. I said, “If you guys do a solar company, I’ll fund you and give whatever guidance I can provide.”573

我当时试图说服他们做太阳能，因为我认为这是一个需要优秀创业者的领域。那时我已经有些超负荷了。我说，“如果你们做太阳能公司，我会资助你们，并尽我所能提供指导。”573

I thought it was really important for great entrepreneurs to build in solar because it wasn’t doing well as an industry. People were not focusing on the right problem. The industry acted like the solar panel production was the main problem. It’s a problem, but it’s not the most important problem. The panel is somewhat commoditized at this point. Making standard-efficiency solar panels is about as hard as making drywall. It’s easy. In fact, making drywall is probably harder.574

我觉得让优秀的创业者进入太阳能领域非常重要，因为这个行业发展不佳。人们没有关注正确的问题。行业内普遍认为太阳能电池板的生产是主要问题。确实是个问题，但不是最重要的问题。电池板现在已经有些商品化了。制造标准效率的太阳能电池板和制造石膏板差不多难度，挺简单的。事实上，制造石膏板可能更难。574

The thorny problem is figuring out how to get solar on tens of thousands, eventually hundreds of thousands, of rooftops. You’ve got to reroof millions of buildings, figure out how the grid interconnects work, and then manage all those systems.

棘手的问题是如何让太阳能装到成千上万，最终是数十万屋顶上。你得给数百万建筑重新铺设屋顶，弄清楚电网的互联方式，然后管理所有这些系统。

With hundreds of thousands of distributed systems, managing them all is tough. It is a really complex distributed energy company. This played to their strengths: creating scalable software for managing computers in a distributed fashion.

面对数十万分布式系统，管理它们非常困难。这是一个非常复杂的分布式能源公司。这正好发挥了他们的优势：创建可扩展的软件来分布式管理计算机。

The SolarCity team did an awesome job. I would show up to the board meetings asking, “What’s the good news this time?” They did an amazing job with almost no help from me.575

SolarCity团队表现非常出色。我去董事会会议时都会问，“这次有什么好消息？”他们几乎没怎么依赖我，就做得很棒。575

We want to create a well-integrated beautiful solar-roof-with-battery product. This empowers individuals to be their own utility, then scale that throughout the world. One ordering experience, one installation, one service contact, one phone app.

我们想打造一个集成度高、美观的太阳能屋顶加电池产品。这让个人能够成为自己的电力供应商，然后将这种模式推广到全世界。一次订购体验，一次安装，一次服务联系，一个手机应用。

We can’t do this well if Tesla and SolarCity are different companies. We needed to break down the barriers of being separate companies. That they were separate at all is largely an accident of history. They have similar origins and pursue the same overarching goal of sustainable energy. When Tesla was ready to scale Powerwall (our home battery for energy storage) and SolarCity was ready to provide a differentiated solar system for energy generation, the time had come to bring them together.576

如果特斯拉和SolarCity是两家公司，我们无法做好这件事。我们需要打破作为独立公司的壁垒。它们之所以分开，主要是历史偶然。他们有相似的起源，追求同一个可持续能源的宏大目标。当特斯拉准备好大规模生产Powerwall（我们的家用储能电池），而SolarCity准备好提供差异化的太阳能发电系统时，融合的时机就到了。576

BUILDING SPACEX打造SpaceX

Building mass-market electric cars was inevitable.

打造大众市场电动汽车是不可避免的。

It would have happened without me.

即使没有我，这也会发生。

But becoming a space-faring civilization is not inevitable.577

但成为一个太空航行文明却不是必然的。577

THE ONLY ONE CRAZY ENOUGH FOR SPACE唯一一个疯狂到敢去太空的人

I’ve always been optimistic. If I wasn’t optimistic, I wouldn’t be attempting all these crazy things. I must be pathologically optimistic, I suppose.578

我一直很乐观。如果我不乐观，就不会尝试所有这些疯狂的事情。我想我一定是病态乐观主义者。578

The origin of SpaceX was trying to figure out why we had not sent anyone to Mars. The obvious next step after Apollo was to send people to Mars.579

SpaceX的起源是试图弄清楚为什么我们还没有派人去火星。阿波罗计划之后，显而易见的下一步就是派人去火星。579

Every year I’d look at the NASA website, and there didn’t seem to be a date for it. We were able to go to the moon in 1969. Our last mission to the moon was in 1972. Now here we are half a century later and we still have not gone back to the moon. It would be extremely tragic if Apollo was the high-water mark for humanity, if the moon was as far as we got.580

每年我都会查看NASA的网站，但似乎没有确定的日期。我们1969年能够登月，最后一次登月任务是在1972年。现在，半个世纪过去了，我们仍然没有重返月球。如果阿波罗是人类的最高成就，月球就是我们达到的最远点，那将是极其悲哀的。580

The space shuttle could only take people to low earth orbit. Then the space shuttle retired, and the United States could take no one to orbit. The trend was dwindling down to nothing. Does this mean we’ve peaked as a civilization?581

航天飞机只能将人送入近地轨道。航天飞机退役后，美国就无法将任何人送入轨道。趋势正在逐渐消失。这是否意味着我们作为文明已经达到顶峰？581

If you had asked people in 1969 what the world would look like in fifty years, they would have expected a base on the moon and some people visiting Mars. Maybe even a base on Mars. They would expect orbiting space hotels and other awesome stuff.582

如果你在1969年问人们五十年后的世界会是什么样子，他们会期待月球基地和一些人访问火星。甚至可能有火星基地。他们会期待轨道上的太空酒店和其他令人惊叹的东西。582

If you told them, “Well, we have a device smaller than a deck of cards with access to all the world’s information, and you can talk to anyone instantly on planet Earth. But the United States will not be able to send anyone to orbit,” they would have called bullshit. “You have all of that, and nothing is happening in space?!” After we sold PayPal in 2001, I was trying to figure out why.583

如果你告诉他们，“我们有一个比一副扑克牌还小的设备，可以访问全世界的信息，你可以瞬间与地球上的任何人通话。但美国却无法将任何人送入轨道，”他们会觉得这简直荒谬。“你拥有这一切，但太空领域却毫无进展？！”在我们2001年卖掉PayPal后，我一直在思考原因。583

The original idea for SpaceX wasn’t to create a company. It was to figure out why we hadn’t sent people to Mars. I thought maybe we’d lost the will to explore. I thought we had to create the will to explore. But that was wrong.

SpaceX最初的想法并不是创建一家公司，而是弄清楚为什么我们没有派人去火星。我曾以为我们失去了探索的意志。我认为我们必须创造探索的意志。但那是错误的。

We have not lost the will to explore; people just did not think there was a way forward. If people don’t think there’s a way, then they won’t continuously bash their head against the wall for progress.584

我们并没有失去探索的意志；只是人们认为没有前进的道路。如果人们认为没有路可走，他们就不会不断地撞墙以求进步。584

There must be things to inspire us—that make you proud to be a member of humanity.585

必须有一些能激励我们的东西——让你为成为人类的一员而感到自豪。585

The Apollo moon landing was an example of that. Only a handful of people went to the moon—and yet actually we all went to the moon. We went with them vicariously. We shared in that adventure. No one would say that was a bad idea—that Apollo wasn’t great. We need more of those.586

阿波罗登月就是这样的例子。只有少数人登上了月球——但实际上我们都跟他们一起去了月球。我们通过他们间接参与了那场冒险。没人会说那是个坏主意——阿波罗计划不伟大。我们需要更多这样的事情。586

The United States is a distillation of the human spirit of exploration. It’s fundamental to the psyche.

美国是人类探索精神的结晶。这是心理的根本所在。

Once people realized, “There is a way to do this,” we got a lot of support.587

一旦人们意识到，“有办法做到这一点，”我们就得到了很多支持。587

I EXPECTED TO LOSE EVERYTHING我预料会失去一切

When something is important enough, you do it even if the odds are not in your favor.588

当某件事足够重要时，即使成功的几率不大，你也会去做。588

When I started them, I guessed both SpaceX and Tesla each had a probability of less than 10 percent to succeed.

当我开始创办SpaceX和特斯拉时，我估计它们各自成功的概率都不到10%。

I don’t look at ideas and ask, “What is the rank-ordered list of best business opportunities from a financial standpoint?” I look for problems that are important to fix for people now and for the future to be good.

我不会看一个想法然后问，“从财务角度看，最佳商业机会的排名是什么？”我寻找的是现在和未来对人们来说重要且必须解决的问题。

If one were to do a risk-adjusted rate of return estimate on opportunities, building rockets and building cars would be pretty close to the bottom of the list. They would be the dumbest things you could possibly do.

如果对机会做风险调整后的收益率估算，造火箭和造汽车几乎会排在最底层。那可能是你能做的最愚蠢的事情。

Company stock value is not a metric by which I judge my own achievements.589

公司股票价值不是我评判自己成就的标准。589

I had a lot of friends try to talk me out of starting a rocket company, because they thought it was crazy. Everyone thought it was a crazy idea. Some people had actually tried to start rocket companies before and failed. They tried to talk me out of it too.590

我有很多朋友试图劝我不要创办火箭公司，因为他们觉得这很疯狂。每个人都觉得这是个疯狂的想法。有些人之前也尝试过创办火箭公司但失败了，他们也劝我放弃。590

One good friend compiled a bunch of footage of rocket failures and forced me to watch it. I said, “I’ve seen them all already.”591

一位好朋友收集了很多火箭失败的视频，强迫我观看。我说，“我都看过了。”591

I think these people misunderstood my premise. When I started SpaceX, it was not with the expectation of success. I thought the most likely outcome was failure.

我认为这些人误解了我的前提。当我创办SpaceX时，并不期望成功。我认为最可能的结果是失败。

Their premise for talking me out of it was, “You’re going to lose the money you invest.” I replied, “Well, that was my expectation anyway, so I don’t really mind!”

他们劝我放弃的理由是，“你会亏掉投资的钱。”我回答说，“那本来就是我的预期，所以我并不介意！”

I mean, I mind losing money, but it’s not like I was trying to figure out the rank-ordered best way to invest my money and on that basis chose space. I didn’t think, “I could do real estate, or invest in shoemaking, and—whoa! Space has the highest ROI!” That was not my premise.592

我的意思是，我确实不喜欢亏钱，但我并不是在试图找出投资钱的最佳排序方式，然后基于此选择太空。我没想过，“我可以做房地产，或者投资制鞋业，哇！太空的投资回报率最高！”那不是我的前提。592

There have been many times where I expected to lose everything. Who starts a car company and a rocket company expecting them to succeed? Certainly not me. I thought they both had a low chance of success, less than 10 percent. Maybe 1 percent, I don’t know. Frankly, I wasn’t wrong.593

我曾多次预料会失去一切。谁会创办汽车公司和火箭公司还期望它们成功？肯定不是我。我认为它们成功的概率都很低，不到10%。也许只有1%，我不知道。坦白说，我没错。593

We must be optimistic. There’s no point in being pessimistic. It just doesn’t help. My theory is you’d rather be optimistic and wrong about the future than pessimistic and right. If you’re pessimistic, you’re going to be miserable. Might as well enjoy the journey.594

我们必须保持乐观。悲观毫无意义，没什么帮助。我的理论是，你宁愿对未来乐观而错，也不要悲观而对。如果你悲观，你会很痛苦。不如享受这段旅程。594

Q: How did you go about creating belief and support for the Mars mission?

问：你是如何建立对火星任务的信念和支持的？

If you’re trying to convince the public to do something, you have to think about what will excite people.

如果你想说服公众去做某事，你必须考虑什么能激发人们的兴趣。

What message are we going to try to convey? What will people respond to? What would I respond to if I was an objective member of the public?

我们要传达什么信息？人们会有什么反应？如果我是一个客观的公众成员，我会有什么反应？

I thought we could send a small greenhouse to the surface of Mars, with seeds and a nutrient gel to hydrate the seeds upon landing.595

我想我们可以送一个小型温室到火星表面，里面带有种子和营养凝胶，着陆后为种子提供水分。595

The greenhouse mission would be the first life on another planet, as far as we knew. The farthest life had ever traveled. We would have a great picture of green plants on a red background. That’s the money shot. People tend to respond to precedents and superlatives.

温室任务将是我们所知的第一个在另一颗星球上的生命。生命旅行的最远距离。我们会有一张绿色植物映衬红色背景的精彩照片。这是关键画面。人们往往对先例和极致事物有反应。

I thought getting a greenhouse on Mars would get people excited about sending humans there. My original goal was to get the public excited, to get NASA’s budget increased to get a mission to Mars funded.596

我认为把温室送上火星会让人们对送人类去火星感到兴奋。我的初衷是激发公众热情，增加NASA预算，资助火星任务。596

I was willing to spend half the money I got from PayPal, so $90 million, on this mission with no expectation of return. This was just something important to accomplish. If I spent $90 million to get NASA a bigger budget, which resulted in us going to Mars, that would be a good outcome.597

我愿意花掉从PayPal获得的一半资金，也就是9000万美元，来做这个没有回报预期的任务。这只是件重要的事。如果我花9000万美元让NASA预算增加，最终促成我们去火星，那就是好结果。597

Also, I was trying to figure out if I could afford to build a spacecraft. I wanted to budget for two missions because if we only did one and it failed, it might actually deter people from trying in the future.598

此外，我还想弄清楚自己是否能负担得起造飞船。我想预算两个任务，因为如果只做一个失败了，可能会阻碍未来的尝试。598

I was able to get the cost of the spacecraft, communications, and the little greenhouse down. But the one thing I couldn’t compress was the cost to launch. There were only a few options, and the US options were way too expensive.
