I ended up going to Russia three times to try to buy the biggest intercontinental ballistic missile (ICBM) in the Russian nuclear fleet. That didn’t work out.599

我成功压缩了飞船、通信和小温室的成本。但唯一无法压缩的是发射成本。选择不多，美国的选项太贵了。我最终去了俄罗斯三次，试图买下俄罗斯核武库中最大的洲际弹道导弹（ICBM）。但没成功。599

In doing this, I realized what we really need to do is improve the technology of space transportation.600 I wanted to hold out hope that humans could be a space-faring civilization, out there among the stars. There was no chance of that unless a new company was started to create revolutionary rockets.601

通过这件事，我意识到我们真正需要做的是改进太空运输技术。600我想抱有希望，人类能成为一个星际文明，遨游星辰。除非有新公司创立，制造革命性火箭，否则不可能实现。601

If a startup didn’t do something to advance rocket technology, it wouldn’t happen. Either it’s coming from a startup or it’s not happening at all. A small chance of success is better than no chance of success. So I started SpaceX in mid-2002, expecting to fail.602

如果没有初创公司推动火箭技术进步，这事就不会发生。要么由初创公司来做，要么根本不会发生。有一点成功的机会总比没有好。所以我在2002年中创办了SpaceX，预期会失败。602

Q: Why did you decide to fund SpaceX yourself?

问：你为什么决定自己资助SpaceX？

After PayPal was sold, I started debating between working on solar, electric cars, or space. I figured space would be the least likely to succeed, so least likely to attract other entrepreneurs. Nobody else was crazy enough to do space. I thought I’d better work on space first.

PayPal卖掉后，我在太阳能、电动汽车和太空之间犹豫。我觉得太空成功可能性最低，也最难吸引其他创业者。没人疯狂到做太空。我想我得先做太空。

My first idea, the greenhouse mission, would have a 100 percent chance of losing all the money associated with it. If anything, starting a rocket company had less than a 100 percent chance of losing all the money associated with it. From my perspective, it was actually less risky than the first idea, which was just paying to send the greenhouse to Mars.603

我最初的想法——温室任务，100%会亏掉所有相关资金。相比之下，创办火箭公司亏光所有资金的概率还低一点。从我的角度看，这其实比第一个想法风险还小，第一个只是花钱把温室送到火星。603

The likeliest outcome is I will lose all my money.

最可能的结果是我会亏光所有钱。

But what’s the alternative? No progress in space exploration?

但还有什么选择呢？太空探索停滞不前吗？

We’ve got to give this a shot, or we’re stuck on Earth forever.604

我们必须尝试，否则我们永远被困在地球上。604

I would not recommend a space company to first-time entrepreneurs. Space is advanced entrepreneuring. You’re better off starting something that requires little capital first. Space is definitely a high-capital effort.605

我不推荐初次创业者做太空公司。太空是高级创业。你最好先做资本需求少的项目。太空绝对是高资本投入。605

I’m a big believer in this: Don’t ask investors to invest their money if you’re not prepared to invest your money. It doesn’t seem right to me to ask other people to invest if you don’t also invest. I’d rather lose my money than any of my friends’ money or investors’ money.606

我非常相信这一点：如果你不准备投资自己的钱，就不要让投资者投钱。我觉得让别人投资而自己不投不太对。我宁愿亏自己的钱，也不愿亏朋友或投资者的钱。606

I didn’t even seek investor funding for the first three rounds of SpaceX because the first thing investors want to ask you is, “Tell us about prior successes in this field. What can we compare this to?” When you have about zero in the success category and a cemetery full of failures, they’re not too keen. Rockets are pretty far out of the comfort zone of most venture capitalists.

SpaceX前三轮融资我甚至没找投资者，因为投资者第一件事就是问：“告诉我们你在这个领域的成功经验，有什么可比的吗？”当你几乎没有成功案例，满是失败，他们就不感兴趣。火箭技术远超大多数风险投资者的舒适区。

We were able to get venture funding after we demonstrated we were almost able to get to orbit. Credit goes to Founders Fund, my compatriots from PayPal: Peter Thiel, Luke Nosek, Ken Howery, and the guys. They invested before we got to orbit, so credit to them.

我们在快接近进入轨道时才获得风险投资。功劳归于Founders Fund，我PayPal的战友：Peter Thiel、Luke Nosek、Ken Howery等。他们在我们进入轨道前就投资了，功劳归他们。

Rockets are hard. I had never made physical stuff before SpaceX, let alone rockets. I had to show I could actually make stuff.607

火箭很难造。SpaceX之前我从没做过实体产品，更别说火箭了。我必须证明我能造东西。607

Once we started building a space launch company, I predicated the strategic plan on a known market, something I know for a fact exists: the need to put small- to medium-sized satellites into orbit. We served that need initially. With that as a base of revenue, we could eventually move into the human transportation market.608

当我们开始建造航天发射公司时，我的战略计划基于一个已知市场——需要将中小型卫星送入轨道。我们最初满足了这个需求。以此为收入基础，我们最终可以进入载人运输市场。608

First, we built an orbital launch vehicle. A high mass-efficiency launch vehicle targeted to solving the satellite delivery market. Our approach was to make this a solid, sound business from the beginning.609

首先，我们造了一个轨道发射载具。一个高质量效率的发射载具，目标是解决卫星发射市场。我们的策略是从一开始就做一个稳健的生意。609

The long-term aims of the company were always human transportation. But I think the smart strategy was to first go for cargo delivery, putting satellites into orbit. Our eventual upgrade path was to build Starship, the successor to Saturn V rocket, a superheavy-lift vehicle that could be used for setting up a moon base or doing a Mars mission. That is our holy grail objective.610

公司的长期目标一直是载人运输。但我认为明智的策略是先做货物运输，把卫星送入轨道。我们的最终升级路线是建造星舰，土星五号的继任者，一种超重型运载火箭，可用于建立月球基地或执行火星任务。这是我们的圣杯目标。610

ROCKETS FROM FIRST PRINCIPLES从第一性原理看火箭

I was trying to figure out if there was something fundamentally superexpensive about rockets.611

我当时想弄明白火箭到底是不是天生就超级贵。611

Q: How did you determine whether your vision for SpaceX had the potential to succeed?

问：你是如何判断SpaceX的愿景是否有成功潜力的？

How could the Russians build low-cost rockets? It’s not like we drive Russian cars, fly Russian planes, or have Russian kitchen appliances. The US is a pretty competitive place, and we should be able to build a cost-efficient launch vehicle.612

俄罗斯人怎么能造出低成本火箭？我们可没开俄罗斯车、坐俄罗斯飞机或用俄罗斯厨房电器。美国竞争激烈，我们应该能造出成本高效的运载火箭。612

I started reading quite a bit about rockets, trying to understand why they’re so friggin’ expensive. It used to be $60 million to build the Delta II. Now, a Delta II costs $100 million to make. Crazy number. Delta II is a relatively small rocket! The bigger rockets are anywhere from $200 to $400 million.

我开始大量阅读火箭相关资料，试图理解它们为什么这么他妈贵。以前造一枚三角洲二号火箭要6000万美元，现在造一枚要1亿美元。数字疯狂。三角洲二号算是相对小的火箭！更大的火箭造价在2亿到4亿美元之间。

I was pretty mad, and when I get mad I try to reframe the problem.613

我当时挺生气的，生气时我会试着重新定义问题。613

I looked at the suppliers NASA had been relying on. With suppliers like Boeing and Lockheed, you’re screwed.614

我调查了NASA一直依赖的供应商。像波音和洛克希德这样的供应商，你就完了。614

One problem with those big aerospace firms is incredible aversion to risk. Even if better technology is available, they still use legacy components, often ones that were developed in the 1960s. Everyone is trying to optimize their ass-covering.615

这些大型航天公司有个问题，就是极度厌恶风险。即使有更好的技术，他们仍用老旧部件，很多还是上世纪60年代开发的。大家都在努力保护自己屁股。615

Second, there’s a tendency in big aerospace companies to outsource everything. That’s been trendy in many industries, but aerospace has done it to a ridiculous degree. They outsource to subcontractors, and then the subcontractors outsource to sub-subcontractors, and so on. You have to go four or five layers down to find somebody actually doing real work—cutting metal, shaping atoms. Every level above that tacks on cost—it’s overhead to the fifth power. I began to understand why things were so expensive.616

其次，大型航天公司有个倾向，就是把所有事情外包。这在很多行业都流行，但航天业做得过分。他们外包给分包商，分包商又外包给次级分包商，如此层层递进。你得往下找四五层，才能找到真正干活的——切割金属、塑造原子。每一层都加价——这就是五次方的管理费用。我开始明白为什么成本这么高。616

Boeing and Lockheed just want their cost-plus gravy trains. When you’ve had success for too long, you lose the desire to take risks. We can’t get to Mars with that system. They have an incentive to never finish the job.617

波音和洛克希德只想要他们的成本加成肥缺。成功太久了，就失去了冒险的欲望。用那套体系，我们去不了火星。他们有动力永远不把工作做完。617

There wasn’t really a good reason for rockets to be so expensive. Rockets could be a lot cheaper even if they were still expendable. But, if one could make them reusable, like airplanes, then the cost of rocketry and space travel would both drop dramatically.618

火箭贵其实没什么合理理由。即使是一次性火箭，也能造得便宜得多。但如果能像飞机一样实现可重复使用，火箭和太空旅行的成本都会大幅下降。618

I put together a feasibility study with a team of engineers who were involved in all major launch vehicle developments over the last thirty years. We met over a number of Saturdays in early 2001 to find the smartest way to approach launch cost and reliability, and we came up with a default design.619

我和一群过去三十年参与过所有主要运载火箭开发的工程师组建了一个可行性研究团队。我们在2001年初的几个周六开会，找出降低发射成本和提升可靠性的最聪明方法，最终设计出了一个默认方案。619

It was fortunate timing. The feasibility study finished around the time we agreed to sell PayPal to eBay. So coincidently with that sale, I moved down to Los Angeles, the biggest concentration of aerospace talent in the world.620

时机很巧。可行性研究完成时，我们刚同意把PayPal卖给eBay。正好那笔交易完成后，我搬到了洛杉矶——全球航天人才最集中的地方。620

Q: Did people give you a hard time for this? It seems like a lot of people were saying “Elon is a software guy. Why is he working on hardware?”

问：有人因此对你刁难吗？很多人说“埃隆是个软件人，干嘛做硬件？”

One hundred percent. A lot of the press from that time is still online. They kept calling me an “internet guy” attempting to build a rocket company. We got ridiculed quite a bit.

百分之百有。当时很多媒体报道还在网上。他们一直叫我“互联网人”试图造火箭公司。我们被嘲笑了不少。

It does sound absurd. “Internet guy starts rocket company” doesn’t sound like a recipe for success, frankly, so I don’t hold it against them. It does sound improbable, and I agreed. It was improbable.621

这确实听起来荒谬。“互联网人开火箭公司”说实话听起来不像成功秘诀，所以我不怪他们。确实不大可能，我也同意。这确实不大可能。621

I didn’t study rocket science but have picked it up along the way.622

我没学过火箭科学，但一路学了不少。622

I didn’t really know how to start a rocket company. The first three launches failed. I did not hit the bull’s-eye.623

我其实不知道怎么开火箭公司。前三次发射都失败了。我没打中靶心。623

KEEPING SPACEX ALIVE让SpaceX继续生存

SpaceX is in this for the long haul and, come hell or high water, we are going to make this work.624

SpaceX是为了长远打算，不管遇到多大困难，我们都要让这件事成功。624

I thought if we couldn’t get to orbit within three failures, we deserved to die. That was my going-in proposition.625

我认为如果我们在三次失败内不能进入轨道，我们就该死。这是我一开始的设定。625

In 2006, our first rocket landed a couple hundred yards away from the launch site in tiny fragments. The second attempt failed too. But, we got further each time.626 In 2008, we had the third failure in a row of the Falcon rocket. I had only budgeted for three attempts.627

2006年，我们的第一枚火箭以碎片形式坠落在发射场几百码外。第二次尝试也失败了。但每次我们都走得更远。626 2008年，猎鹰火箭连续第三次失败。我只预算了三次尝试。627

That’s when I split all the money I had left between Tesla and SpaceX. It was enough to afford a fourth launch at SpaceX, if we moved super fast.628

那时我把剩下的钱平均分给了特斯拉和SpaceX。如果我们动作快，足够支持SpaceX第四次发射。628

I collected everyone in the conference room and said, “We have one last rocket. Get your shit together, go back to the island, and launch it. You have six weeks.”629

我召集所有人到会议室，说：“我们只有最后一枚火箭了。振作起来，回到岛上发射它。你们有六周时间。”629

Here was my email to the team: “There should be absolutely zero question that SpaceX will prevail in reaching orbit and demonstrating reliable space transport. For my part, I will never give up and I mean never. Thank you for your hard work. Now, on to flight four.”630

这是我给团队的邮件：“毫无疑问，SpaceX将成功进入轨道并展示可靠的太空运输。就我而言，我永不放弃，绝不。感谢你们的辛勤工作。现在，准备第四次飞行。”630

If we didn’t succeed, we would be pointed to as a reason people shouldn’t even try these things. We must do whatever is necessary to keep going.631

如果我们失败了，人们会用我们作为不该尝试这些事情的理由。我们必须做一切必要的事继续前进。631

I don’t ever give up. I’d have to be dead or completely incapacitated.632

我从不放弃。除非我死了或完全丧失能力。632

It was an interesting exercise in karma. After I got ousted by the PayPal coup leaders, I could have said “You guys suck,” but I didn’t. If I’d done that, Founders Fund wouldn’t have come through with an investment in SpaceX in 2008 and the company would be dead. Karma may be real.633

这是一种有趣的因果报应。被PayPal政变领导者赶出后，我本可以说“你们很糟”，但我没说。如果我那样做，Founders Fund就不会在2008年投资SpaceX，公司也会死掉。因果报应或许是真的。633

If the fourth launch hadn’t worked, that would have been curtains. I had no money left. It was a pretty close thing. We would have joined the graveyard of prior rocket startups.634

如果第四次发射失败，那就完蛋了。我没钱了。情况非常危急。我们本会加入之前火箭创业公司的坟墓。634

I thought the odds of the fourth launch working were better than 50 percent. There was just a little change in the thrust transient of the first-stage engine we couldn’t see on the ground. That made the difference. Fortunately, the fourth SpaceX launch worked.635

我认为第四次发射成功的概率超过50%。只是一级发动机推力瞬态有点变化，地面看不出来。那就是区别。幸运的是，第四次发射成功了。635

We almost went down as the company that made it to orbit, then died.

我们差点成为那个进入轨道后却死掉的公司。

When it worked, my cortisol levels were clinically high. I couldn’t feel celebratory. There was no jubilation. I was too stressed. Getting to orbit meant, “Okay, we’re not going to die today.” We’ll live a little bit longer. I just felt relief.636

成功时，我的皮质醇水平临床上偏高。我没法感到庆祝，没有欢腾。我太紧张了。进入轨道意味着，“好吧，今天我们不会死。”我们还能多活一会儿。我只是感到松了一口气。636

But it was short-lived. We were still in a tough position. It was not like we had customers lined up. Even the fourth launch working wasn’t enough to succeed. We also needed a big contract to keep us alive.637

但这种感觉很短暂。我们仍处境艰难。并不是有客户排队等着。即使第四次发射成功，也不足以保证成功。我们还需要一份大合同来维持生存。637

LANDING NASA CONTRACTS赢得NASA合同

After the successful launch, as I was scrambling to fundraise for Tesla in 2008, NASA called out of the blue to tell me SpaceX had won a contract. I couldn’t believe it. I screamed, “I LOVE NASA. YOU GUYS ROCK,” then hung up. I called our president, Gwynne Shotwell, and told her to immediately sign whatever deal NASA offered.638

成功发射后，2008年我正忙着为特斯拉筹集资金，NASA突然打电话告诉我SpaceX赢得了一份合同。我简直不敢相信。我大喊：“我爱NASA，你们太棒了！”然后挂断电话。我给我们的总裁格温·肖特韦尔打电话，告诉她立刻签下NASA提供的任何合同。638

It felt like I had been blindfolded and taken out to the firing squad. Then they yelled, “Fire” and the guns just went “click.” No bullets. Then they let me free. Sure, I was glad to be alive. But I was still pretty fucking nervous.639

感觉就像被蒙上眼睛带到行刑队面前。然后他们喊“开火”，枪声却只是“咔嗒”一声，没有子弹。随后他们放了我。当然，我很庆幸自己活着，但我依然非常紧张。639

My estimate of success was not far off.

我对成功的估计并不算偏差太大。

We just made it by the skin of our teeth.

我们几乎是侥幸成功的。

We had developed the Dragon spacecraft somewhat opportunistically. NASA announced they were going to retire the space shuttle, and they didn’t have the budget to develop a new vehicle with cargo transport capability to the space station.

我们有些机会主义地开发了“龙”飞船。NASA宣布将退役航天飞机，但没有预算开发一款具备向空间站运送货物能力的新飞行器。

They put it out to bid for the first time in NASA history. It was quite a big step, and we were lucky enough to win one of those contracts. Then, the other company wasn’t able to execute, and SpaceX ended up being the primary means of transporting cargo to and from the space station.640

他们首次在NASA历史上公开招标。这是一个很大的突破，我们很幸运赢得了其中一份合同。后来，另一家公司未能执行合同，SpaceX最终成为往返空间站货物运输的主要方式。640

Our rocket ended up costing around $6 million, super low compared to other rockets in that class, which were about $25 million. We’re about a quarter of the price of Boeing or Lockheed. Once it was reusable, payload delivery could be two orders of magnitude cheaper.641

我们的火箭成本约为600万美元，远低于同类火箭约2500万美元的价格。我们的价格大约是波音或洛克希德的四分之一。一旦实现可重复使用，货物运送成本将降低两个数量级。641

After we did the first two space station resupply missions, which thankfully both worked, NASA said, “What about astronaut transport?” They put out a big competition and awarded two contracts for astronaut transport, one to Boeing and one to SpaceX. Now, we transport astronauts to and from the space station.642

完成了前两次空间站补给任务后，幸运的是两次都成功了，NASA问：“那宇航员运输呢？”他们举办了大型竞标，授予了两份宇航员运输合同，一份给波音，一份给SpaceX。现在，我们负责往返空间站的宇航员运输。642

YOU HAVE TO BLOW THINGS UP你必须把东西炸掉

The first goal is to make the damn thing work—we’ll optimize it later.

首要目标是让该死的东西能工作——我们以后再优化。

We want to push the envelope. If you don’t push the envelope, you cannot achieve the goal of a fully and rapidly reusable rocket with high payload. It’s not possible. You have to go close to the edge on margins.643

我们想要突破极限。如果你不突破极限，就无法实现高有效载荷的完全快速可重复使用火箭的目标。这是不可能的。你必须在边际上接近极限。643

We intentionally iterate the design of the Starship at SpaceX rapidly. This is a fundamentally different optimization for Starship versus a polar extreme like our Dragon capsule. Since Dragon now carries crew, there can be no failures, ever. Everything’s going to be tested. There can never be a failure, ever, for any reason whatsoever. With human crew in a developed vehicle, we’re in extreme conservatism mode.

我们在SpaceX故意快速迭代星舰的设计。这是星舰与极端安全要求的龙飞船之间根本不同的优化。由于龙飞船现在载人，绝不能有任何失败。所有东西都必须经过测试。无论任何原因，绝不能失败。载人飞行器处于极端保守模式。

Falcon is a little less conservative. It is possible for us to have, say, a failure with a booster on landing. That’s not the end of the world. Early Starship models were the polar opposite of Dragon. We were iterating rapidly to learn.644

猎鹰火箭则稍微不那么保守。比如助推器着陆失败对我们来说是可能的，但这并不是世界末日。早期的星舰模型与龙飞船完全相反。我们快速迭代以学习。644

Q: Is that different from other space programs?

问：这和其他航天项目有何不同？

NASA’s space shuttle had almost no iteration because there were people on board. You can’t be blowing up shuttles. That lack of iteration was a problem. There were a lot of issues they were aware of, but people were too afraid to make changes to a design that had already worked. There was risk-reward asymmetry. If you make a change and something goes wrong, big punishment. If you make a change and it goes right, small reward.

NASA的航天飞机几乎没有迭代，因为机上有人。你不能炸毁航天飞机。缺乏迭代是个问题。他们知道有很多问题，但人们害怕对已成功的设计做改动。风险与回报不对称。改动出错，惩罚很大；改动成功，回报很小。

They had seen the issues with the O-ring and the insulation coming off and hitting the wing before, but there had not been a catastrophe. They figured it was good enough because it worked before. But that’s like Russian roulette: “Look, I’ve pulled the trigger and I’m fine.”

他们曾见过O形环问题和绝缘层脱落撞击机翼，但没有发生灾难。他们认为既然以前能用就够好了。但这就像俄罗斯轮盘赌：“看，我扣动扳机了，我没事。”

It’s hard to iterate when people are on board every mission. Starship does not have anyone on board during early trials so we can blow things up, learn, and iterate. That’s really helpful. To improve safety, you need to fly a lot and have a lot of redundancy. So if you lose an engine on the booster, it doesn’t matter. Even losing multiple engines shouldn’t matter.645

每次飞行都有乘员时很难迭代。星舰早期试验无人乘坐，所以我们可以炸毁它，学习并迭代。这非常有帮助。要提高安全性，你需要大量飞行和冗余设计。所以即使助推器失去一个发动机也没关系。即使失去多个发动机也不该有问题。645

We don’t want to design to eliminate every risk. Otherwise, we will never get anywhere.646

“我们不想设计成消除所有风险。否则，我们永远无法前进。646”

Before every Starship launch, we go through the list of risks we predict, which we call the “risk list.” If you look at various reasons why we blew up, none of the reasons they blew up were on our “risk list.” There’s a crazy amount of new technology, all evolving simultaneously. We need time and trials to iron out the unknown unknowns.647

每次星舰发射前，我们都会检查预测的风险清单，称为“风险列表”。如果你看我们爆炸的各种原因，没有一个是在我们的“风险列表”上的。这里有大量新技术同时发展。我们需要时间和试验来解决未知的未知问题。647

Q: What is the big-picture vision SpaceX is iterating toward?

问：SpaceX正在迭代的宏观愿景是什么？

The overarching optimization is: “What is the fastest time to a city on Mars?”

总体优化目标是：“最快达到火星城市的时间是多少？”

Then subset → fastest time to a fully usable rocket.

然后细分为→最快达到完全可用火箭的时间。

And subset → fastest time to orbit.648

再细分为→最快进入轨道的时间。648

Initial production was simply a learning exercise. None of the initial designs will be long term. We’re just trying to learn in the shortest period of time. The early Starship assembly yard looked like a garage shop, to be frank. It’s weird—we have superadvanced technology being built in a tent in a parking lot.

最初的生产只是学习练习。初期设计都不会是长期方案。我们只是想在最短时间内学习。早期星舰组装场坦率说看起来像个车库作坊。很奇怪——我们用超级先进技术在停车场的帐篷里建造。

Early versions of Starship didn’t even have doors. We didn’t need doors. We did need to be superfocused on getting to orbit. Then superfocused on getting the ship back. Doors were just unnecessary complexity. The first ten ships (or more) we won’t get back from orbit. We probably won’t be flying them again. Maybe once or twice—if we’re lucky.649

早期星舰甚至没有门。我们不需要门。我们需要专注于进入轨道，然后专注于让飞船回来。门只是多余的复杂性。前十艘（或更多）我们不会从轨道回收。我们可能不会再飞它们了。也许一两次——如果幸运的话。649

Eliminate what isn’t necessary to solve the key problem.650

“消除不必要的，专注解决关键问题。650”

BUILDING THE JUST BARELY POSSIBLE打造勉强可能的火箭

I was told many times rocket reusability was impossible.651

我被多次告知火箭可重复使用是不可能的。651

One of the hardest engineering problems known to man is making a reusable orbital rocket.

人类已知最难的工程难题之一就是制造可重复使用的轨道火箭。

We have reusability in bicycles, cars, and airplanes. It’s bizarre to not have reusability in another form of transport. It would be insane to chuck a boat away after every trip. Getting one trip every four days would not cut it in a car. But this is how rockets have worked thus far.652

自行车、汽车和飞机都实现了可重复使用。另一种交通工具却没有可重复使用，这很奇怪。每次航行后丢弃一艘船简直疯狂。汽车每四天跑一次也不行。但火箭迄今为止就是这样运作的。652

The design goal is immediate reflight. Refill propellants and go again, just like any other mode of transport. This is gigantic.653

设计目标是立即复飞。加注推进剂后再次起飞，就像其他交通工具一样。这意义重大。653

Getting to orbit was solved in the 1950s. The math clearly demonstrates there is no point in another expendable rocket; you have to achieve reusability.654

“1950年代就解决了进入轨道的问题。数学清楚表明，再造一次性火箭毫无意义；必须实现可重复使用。”654

It’s not like other rocket scientists were huge idiots who wanted to throw their rockets away all the time. It’s hard to make something like this. Nobody has ever succeeded, and for a good reason. Earth’s gravity is heavy. On Mars this would be no problem. Moon, piece of cake. On Earth, fucking hard. Just barely possible.

并不是其他火箭科学家都是大傻瓜，想一直扔掉火箭。制造这样的东西很难。没人成功过，这是有原因的。地球引力很大。在火星上这没问题。月球上，轻而易举。在地球上，真他妈难。勉强可能。

A fully reusable orbital system would be one of the biggest breakthroughs in the history of humanity. That’s why it hurts my brain. We’re just a bunch of monkeys. How did we even get this far? It beats me. We were swinging through the trees eating bananas not long ago.655

完全可重复使用的轨道系统将是人类历史上最大的突破之一。这让我头脑发麻。我们不过是一群猴子。我们怎么走到这一步的？我也不明白。不久前我们还在树上荡着吃香蕉呢。655

Can you imagine if human civilization continued at the current pace of technological advancement for another million years? Where would we be?

“你能想象如果人类文明以当前的技术进步速度持续一百万年？我们会到达哪里？”

I told my team, “Imagine there was a pallet of cash plummeting through the atmosphere and it was going to burn up and smash into tiny pieces. Would you try to save it? You probably would.”656

我告诉我的团队，“想象有一托盘现金正坠入大气层，马上会燃烧殆尽并碎成碎片。你会试着救它吗？你很可能会。”656

When people tried to make a reusable system before, they would conclude success was not one of the possible outcomes. In government programs, of course, the program would still continue for quite some time. It’s funny but it’s true.657

以前有人尝试制造可重复使用系统时，结论是成功不在可能结果之列。当然，在政府项目中，项目仍会持续相当长时间。这很有趣，但是真的。657

The space shuttle attempted some level of reusability, but it ended up costing more per flight than an expendable vehicle of equivalent capability. For a long time, people pointed to the space shuttle as an example of why attempting reusability was dumb. But you can’t take a single example and make an entire theory out of it.658

航天飞机尝试了一定程度的可重复使用，但每次飞行成本比同等能力的一次性飞行器还高。长期以来，人们以航天飞机为例，说明尝试可重复使用是愚蠢的。但不能凭一个例子就建立整个理论。658

I wasn’t sure if it was possible when we started SpaceX, but after a few years of work I became convinced. Full and rapid reuse is possible. It is possible to make this work, and that gave me hope. Of course, just because something is possible does not mean it will occur.659

我们创立SpaceX时，我不确定是否可能，但几年努力后我确信了。完全且快速的重复使用是可能的。能让它工作，这给了我希望。当然，可能不等于一定会发生。659

The first step is to establish that something is possible, then the probability it will occur.660

“第一步是确定某事可能，然后才是它发生的概率。”660

In no prior design was full reusability one of the possible outcomes.661

“之前的设计中，没有一个把完全可重复使用作为可能结果。”661

SpaceX’s Falcon 9 was the first rocket with any reusability. We bring the boosters back and refly them. Only the upper stage is expended. Falcon 9 is not rapidly reusable because most boosters land on a ship in the ocean. It takes a while to bring it back, fuel, and reuse it. Falcon 9 first had reusability measured in months, then weeks, and now finally days. But, the efficiency is limited. We can’t bring it lower than several days.662

SpaceX的猎鹰9号是首个具有任何可重复使用性的火箭。我们回收助推器并复飞，只有上面级是一次性的。猎鹰9号不是快速可重复使用，因为大多数助推器降落在海上的船上。回收、加注、复用需要时间。猎鹰9号的可重复使用时间从几个月到几周，再到现在的几天，但效率有限，无法低于几天。662

Knock on wood, Falcon 9 is the most reliable rocket in the world and launches about every two to three days.663 Every one has come home safely, which is the most important thing. We learned a tremendous amount from the Falcon program that fed into the Starship program.664 We wouldn’t have been able to make Starship without the benefit of Falcon 9.665

敲敲木头，猎鹰9号是世界上最可靠的火箭，发射频率约每两到三天一次。663 每次都安全返回，这是最重要的。我们从猎鹰项目学到了大量经验，为星舰项目提供了支持。664 没有猎鹰9号，我们无法制造星舰。665

Nobody thought this was possible. But we’re not breaking any laws of physics, so we knew it was possible.666

“没人认为这可能。但我们没有违反任何物理定律，所以知道它是可能的。”666

Starship is the largest flying object of any kind. It’s five thousand tons at liftoff, much heavier than any other aircraft, ever. The body diameter is nine meters (roughly fifty feet). It will get taller with newer versions too. And it’s going straight up; aircraft don’t go straight up. It’s an insanely gigantic thing, twice the size of Saturn V, previously the largest rocket ever built.667

星舰是有史以来最大的飞行物体。起飞重量五千吨，远超任何其他飞机。直径九米（约五十英尺）。新版本还会更高。它垂直上升；飞机不会垂直升空。它巨大无比，是土星五号的两倍，后者曾是最大火箭。667

There could be other solutions too, but this will work. The first order of business was to get one that works. Now we optimize.668 Full and rapid reusability will work. It’s just a question of how many attempts we need to make it work, and then make it work really well.669

可能还有其他方案，但这个可行。首要任务是造出一个能用的。现在我们优化。668 完全且快速的重复使用会成功。只是需要多少次尝试才能成功，然后做到非常好。669

It has to be true reuse, which means rapid and complete reuse. The problem with the space shuttle was only a portion of the system came back, and the reusable parts were incredibly difficult to refurbish. Reuse matters more if it’s rapid and complete—if the only thing we do between flights is maintenance and refuel, like an airplane.670

必须是真正的重复使用，即快速且完整的重复使用。航天飞机的问题是只有部分系统回收，且可重复使用部分极难翻新。重复使用更重要的是快速且完整——如果飞行间唯一工作是维护和加注燃料，就像飞机一样。670

If there is no major work required between flights, then the cost of a flight approaches the cost of propellant. Nearly 80 percent of Starship’s propellant is liquid oxygen, and a little over 20 percent methane, which are both very low-cost fuels. The fuel cost of a flight is maybe a million dollars or less.671

如果飞行间无需重大维修，飞行成本接近推进剂成本。星舰近80%的推进剂是液氧，20%以上是甲烷，都是极低成本燃料。一次飞行的燃料成本约一百万美元或更少。671

Full and rapid reusability is the holy grail of rocketry because then you’re only constrained by propellant costs.672

“完全且快速的重复使用是火箭技术的圣杯，因为那时你唯一受限的是推进剂成本。”672

Q: What made you remove the landing legs?

问：你为什么去掉了着陆腿？

We fight to save mass constantly, especially with the reusable upper stage, where nobody has ever succeeded.

我们不断努力节省质量，尤其是可重复使用的上面级，没人成功过。

Again here, we try to think in the limit of physics. The problem with landing legs is they add mass, we have to protect them during reentry, and we have to get a giant rocket from wherever it landed back onto the launch stand. That’s tricky. I was trying to think of the limit. What’s the fastest way to achieve reusability?

我们尝试从物理极限思考。着陆腿的问题是增加质量，重返大气层时必须保护它们，还要把巨大的火箭从降落点带回发射台。这很棘手。我在想极限是什么。实现重复使用最快的方式是什么？

It would be to land on the launch stand. Why not just have it land on the arms of the tower it launches from?673

那就是直接降落在发射台上。为什么不让它降落在发射塔的臂架上呢？673

This is the best-case outcome for rapid reuse. It gets caught by the same arms that placed it in the launch ring. In principle, the superheavy booster can be reflown within an hour of landing. It comes back in about five minutes one way and then it gets caught by the tower arms, placed back in the launch mount, and then we refill propellant in about thirty to forty minutes and place a ship on top of it.674

这是快速重复使用的最佳方案。它被放置在发射环的同一臂架抓住。原则上，超级重型助推器着陆后一小时内可复飞。它单程大约五分钟返回，然后被塔臂抓住，放回发射架，再加注推进剂约三十到四十分钟，顶部装上飞船。674

When we first talked about it, it sounded batshit crazy. To custom-build a giant tower to catch the heaviest flying object ever made with mechanical arms. Pluck it out of the air. But we did it.675

刚开始谈论时，听起来疯狂。定制建造一个巨型塔，用机械臂抓住有史以来最重的飞行物体。空中抓取。但我们做到了。675

It’s an epic sight: giant robot arms catching a giant rocket. This is much more efficient than having landing legs on the rocket itself.676

这是一幅史诗般的画面：巨型机器人臂抓住巨型火箭。这比火箭自身装着着陆腿高效得多。676

I call it rapidly reusable, reliable rockets. RRRR. Space pirates.677

“我称之为快速可重复使用、可靠的火箭。RRRR。太空海盗。”677

OPTIMIZING FOR MASS TO MARS优化火星运载质量

The thing we optimize for at SpaceX is cost per ton to orbit. When the goal is low cost per ton of payload to orbit, you can’t cheat.

SpaceX优化的目标是每吨运载到轨道的成本。当目标是降低每吨有效载荷运载到轨道的成本时，不能有任何投机取巧。

All early rockets were a test program. We expected them to explode. It’s weird if they don’t explode, frankly. To get a lot of payload to orbit at low cost, you have to run everything close to the edge.

所有早期火箭都是试验项目。我们预期它们会爆炸。坦率地说，如果不爆炸反而很奇怪。要以低成本将大量有效载荷送入轨道，必须让所有系统都运行在极限边缘。

To get a meaningful payload to orbit, scale is important. We need to make things big. There is value to scale here. You don’t see everything shipped by small pickup trucks; you see semi trailers. You see giant ocean cargo ships, not a bunch of small boats with outboard motors. Scale has value in itself. For example, the same computer that controls a tiny rocket controls the big rocket. The percentage weight of the electronics is significant in a small rocket but becomes vanishingly small in a big rocket.678

要将有意义的有效载荷送入轨道，规模非常重要。我们需要做大。规模本身就有价值。你不会看到所有货物都用小皮卡运输，而是用半挂车。你会看到巨型海洋货船，而不是一堆装有舷外机的小船。规模本身有价值。例如，控制小型火箭的同一台计算机也控制大型火箭。电子设备的重量占比在小火箭中显著，但在大火箭中几乎可以忽略不计。678

The focus is to minimize cost per ton of payload to orbit, the surface of the moon, or Mars. I’ll give you a sense of just how much we need to improve it. Right now, the cost per landed ton to the surface of Mars is more than a billion dollars. You can’t count the heat shield, parachute, or landing systems—only the useful stuff. In the case of the Mars rovers, it’s really just the rover. That is the useful thing. The rover weighs about a ton and costs a billion dollars to get to Mars. So currently it costs roughly a billion dollars per ton to Mars.679

我们的重点是最小化每吨有效载荷送入轨道、月球表面或火星的成本。我来给你一个改进幅度的概念。现在，将每吨有效载荷送到火星表面的成本超过十亿美元。你不能算入热防护罩、降落伞或着陆系统——只算有用的部分。以火星探测车为例，真正有用的就是探测车本身。探测车重约一吨，送到火星的成本是十亿美元。所以目前送一吨货物到火星的成本大约是一亿美元。679

To build a self-sustaining city on Mars, that cost will have to be less than one hundred thousand dollars a ton. That would be ten thousand times better than the current state of the art, to put things into perspective.680

要在火星上建立一个自给自足的城市，这个成本必须低于每吨十万美元。换句话说，这比当前技术水平要好一万倍。680

That’s how much improvement is needed. Not a 10,000 percent increase, a 10,000-times increase. That’s what Starship is intended to do: be ten thousand times better than the current state of the art. Orders and orders and orders of magnitude better. But, we’re not breaking any laws of physics. This is possible.681

这就是所需的改进幅度。不是百分之万的提升，而是万倍的提升。这正是星舰的目标：比现有技术好一万倍。数量级上的巨大飞跃。但我们并没有违反任何物理定律，这是可能实现的。681

When we started the Starship design it seemed utterly insane. Now it’s gone from utterly insane to merely late.682

“当我们开始设计星舰时，这看起来完全疯狂。现在它已经从完全疯狂变成了只是迟到而已。682”

With full reusability, Starship 3 will cost significantly less per flight than tiny Falcon 1. That’s the difference between a fully reusable rocket and an expendable rocket. The fully reusable rocket with low-cost propellant actually costs less than a tiny expendable rocket.683 By analogy, the cost of flying a 747 is obviously much less than a small airplane that gets thrown away.

通过完全可重复使用，星舰3号每次飞行的成本将远低于小型猎鹰1号。这就是完全可重复使用火箭和一次性火箭的区别。完全可重复使用的火箭加上低成本推进剂，实际上比小型一次性火箭还便宜。683 类比来说，驾驶一架747的成本显然远低于一架被丢弃的小型飞机。

Falcon 1 gets about half a ton to orbit. The Starship 3 will send four hundred times more payload for less than the cost of a Falcon 1. It’s mind-boggling that the giant thing can cost so much less than the small thing.684

猎鹰1号能将约半吨货物送入轨道。星舰3号将以低于猎鹰1号成本的价格运送四百倍的有效载荷。这个庞然大物竟然比小型火箭便宜这么多，令人难以置信。684

A lot of people talk a lot about the number of launches to orbit per year, but this is not really what matters. What really matters is the total useful payload to orbit per year. If these were ocean ships, you’d be comparing a dinghy to a supertanker. They’re not the same.685

很多人热衷于讨论每年发射到轨道的次数，但这其实并不重要。真正重要的是每年送入轨道的有效载荷总量。如果这些是海洋船只，你是在比较小艇和超级油轮，它们根本不是一回事。685

There must be things you’re excited about, that you’re glad to be alive for.

“一定有让你兴奋的事情，让你庆幸自己活着。”

For me, this is the most important reason to pursue the establishment of life on Mars.686

“对我来说，这就是追求在火星建立生命的最重要原因。686”

There are various “great filters” that have the potential to end civilizations. One of the “great filters” to pass is whether we become a multiplanet species. Will humanity be one of those species that passes the great filter of going beyond one planet?687

存在各种“伟大过滤器”，可能会终结文明。其中一个“伟大过滤器”是我们是否能成为多星球物种。人类能否成为那个跨越单一星球限制的物种？687

To become multiplanetary, the breakthrough we need to create is a rapidly reusable interplanetary transport system. This is right on the edge of impossible.688

要成为多星球物种，我们需要实现的突破是快速可重复使用的星际运输系统。这几乎是极限挑战。688

That’s the breakthrough SpaceX is really trying to achieve. What we’ve done so far is good; it’s better. But it has been evolutionary—not yet revolutionary. We need the revolutionary thing to work.689

这正是SpaceX真正努力实现的突破。到目前为止，我们所做的很好，甚至更好，但仍是渐进式的——还不是革命性的。我们需要革命性的成果成功实现。689

PART IV第四部分

Part IV: On Behalf of Humanity

第四部分：为了人类

There is something special—far more rewarding than money—about working with an epic team to make breakthroughs.690

与一支史诗般的团队合作取得突破，有一种特别的意义——远比金钱更有价值。690

BUILDING OUR FUTURE建设我们的未来

I can’t emphasize this enough: As long as we push hard and are not complacent, the future is going to be great.691

我无法再三强调这一点：只要我们努力不懈，不自满，未来一定会很美好。691

COMPANIES ARE PHILANTHROPY公司即慈善

If you care about the reality of goodness instead of the perception of it, philanthropy is extremely difficult.

如果你关心善良的现实而非其表象，慈善是极其困难的。

If philanthropy is acting from a love of humanity—my companies are philanthropy.692

如果慈善是出于对人类的爱——那么我的公司就是慈善。692

Q: What do you say to people who criticize you for not “having your name on a hospital wing” or making other nonprofit donations?

问：你如何回应那些批评你没有“把名字挂在医院病房”或做其他非营利捐赠的人？

I do have a small foundation. I am giving some money away. But generally, if there’s a way to fix something within the market system, building a company is the better way to do it. Sometimes there isn’t or there are complications to it.693

我确实有一个小型基金会，也在捐出一些钱。但通常来说，如果能通过市场体系内的方式解决问题，建立一家公司是更好的方法。有时这不可行，或者存在复杂情况。693

If it’s possible to solve a problem with a profitable venture then that’s the best thing to do. In the grand scheme of things, there are a few failures in the market that have to be addressed with a nonprofit. There are some, but not many.694

如果能通过盈利性企业解决问题，那是最好的选择。从整体来看，市场中确实存在一些失败，必须通过非营利组织来解决。这些情况有，但不多。694

SpaceX, Tesla, Neuralink, and the Boring Company are philanthropy. SpaceX is trying to ensure the long-term survival of humanity as a multiple-planet species. We also provide global internet through Starlink. That is love of humanity. Tesla is accelerating sustainable energy. This is a love-based mission. Neuralink is trying to help solve brain injuries and the existential risk of AI. That is love of humanity. The Boring Company is trying to save people’s time by solving traffic, which is hell for most people. That is also love of humanity.695

SpaceX、特斯拉、Neuralink 和 The Boring Company 都是慈善。SpaceX 正在努力确保人类作为多星球物种的长期生存。我们还通过 Starlink 提供全球互联网服务。这就是对人类的爱。特斯拉正在加速可持续能源的发展，这是一项基于爱的使命。Neuralink 致力于帮助解决脑损伤和人工智能带来的生存风险，这也是对人类的爱。The Boring Company 试图通过解决交通问题来节省人们的时间，而交通对大多数人来说是地狱，这同样是对人类的爱。695

My fundamental intent is to improve the probability of the future being good. SpaceX and Tesla will do more good for humanity than anything I could do with philanthropy. It is difficult to give away money effectively if you care about the money actually doing good, not merely having the perception of doing good.696

我的根本意图是提高未来美好发生的可能性。SpaceX 和特斯拉将为人类带来比我通过慈善所能做的更多的益处。如果你真正关心钱能带来实际的善果，而不仅仅是善的表象，那么有效地捐钱是很难的。696

Tesla sells twice as many electric vehicles as the rest of electric car makers in the United States combined. Tesla has done more to help the environment than all other companies combined. Therefore, it would be fair to say as a leader of that company I’ve done more for the environment than any other single human on Earth. I care about the reality of goodness, not the perception of it.

特斯拉在美国销售的电动车数量是其他所有电动车制造商总和的两倍。特斯拉为环境所做的贡献超过了所有其他公司总和。因此，作为该公司的领导者，可以公平地说，我为环境所做的贡献超过了地球上任何其他个人。我关心的是善的现实，而非其表象。

What I see all over the place is people who care about looking good while doing evil. Fuck them.697

我看到到处都是那些关心自己看起来好看却做着恶事的人。去他的。697

I care about reality. Perception be damned.698

我关心现实。表象见鬼去吧。698

COMPANIES CREATE WEALTH FOR ALL公司为所有人创造财富

I don’t disrupt something for the sake of disrupting it. My focus is making a product that improves quality of life for people.699

我不会为了颠覆而颠覆。我的重点是制造能够改善人们生活质量的产品。699

If the output is more valuable than the inputs, that creates a profit. Profit shows you have a useful company. But in a high-growth scenario, you need a lot more inputs for future output, so for a while you have negative cash flow and lack of profitability, which we had early on at Tesla. In the long term, of course, that has to be fixed. There can’t be a negative cash flow in the long term.700

如果产出比投入更有价值，就会产生利润。利润表明你拥有一家有用的公司。但在高速增长的情况下，你需要更多的投入以换取未来的产出，所以一段时间内会出现负现金流和无利润的情况，特斯拉早期就是如此。当然，从长远来看，这必须得到解决。长期不可能出现负现金流。700

If you create a company that produces products and services better than what existed before, wealth is created. If you have some ownership in that company, wealth accrues to you. Doing good work gives you the right to allocate more capital.701

如果你创建了一家生产比以往更好产品和服务的公司，就会创造财富。如果你拥有这家公司的部分所有权，财富就会归属于你。做好工作会赋予你分配更多资本的权利。701

Sometimes people equate wealth with consumption, but they’re obviously not the same thing. Consumption is fun, but capital allocation is a job.

有时人们将财富等同于消费，但显然两者不同。消费是享受，而资本分配是一项工作。

If people want to get upset about something, excess consumption is more reasonable than being upset about someone having a high net worth.702

“如果人们想要生气，过度消费比有人拥有高净资产更合理。”702

Warren Buffett has a high net worth because he’s doing a useful job allocating capital for the economy. He’s very skilled at it, and he should probably keep doing it. But he is not engaged in insane, conspicuous consumption.703

沃伦·巴菲特拥有高净资产，是因为他在为经济分配资本方面做着有用的工作。他非常擅长这项工作，可能应该继续做下去。但他并没有进行疯狂的炫耀性消费。703

Warren Buffett sits there and reads all these super boring annual reports. Does anybody want that job? He is constantly reading tedious annual reports of companies, including the minutiae of accounting and deciding whether to allocate capital into Coke or Pepsi. I don’t want that job.704

沃伦·巴菲特坐在那里阅读那些极其无聊的年报。谁想要那份工作？他不断阅读公司繁琐的年报，包括会计细节，并决定是投资可口可乐还是百事可乐。我不想要那份工作。704

I’m not claiming I have no money. But when people hear a big number for my net worth, they think I have that much cash, and I’m sitting on the cash doing nothing, hoarding resources. No, that value is the stock of these companies. Net worth just calculates my ownership of these companies, if you add them up. It’s not as though I’ve got billions of dollars sitting in a bank account.705

我并不是说我没有钱。但当人们听到我的净资产是个大数字时，他们以为我有那么多现金，坐拥现金无所事事，囤积资源。不是的，那些价值是这些公司的股票。净资产只是计算我对这些公司的所有权总和。并不是我有数十亿美元躺在银行账户里。705

Just as my money was the first into Tesla, it will be the last out.706

“正如我的钱是特斯拉的第一笔投入，它也将是最后一笔撤出。”706

The alternative to keeping the stock would be to say, “Okay, let’s give the stock to the government or someone else.” Then the government would control the companies and be running things.707

不持有股票的替代方案是说，“好吧，把股票交给政府或其他人。”那么政府就会控制这些公司并管理一切。707

A lot of the push for higher government involvement or expropriation of assets to the government is from politicians. What they are saying is those resources shouldn’t be in control of individuals who built them, but instead should be under government control. They’re basically saying they want control of the assets.708

推动政府更多介入或将资产征收归政府的很多声音来自政治家。他们的意思是这些资源不应该由创造它们的个人控制，而应该由政府掌控。他们基本上是在说他们想控制这些资产。708

I think the profit motive is good if the rules of an industry are properly set up. There’s nothing wrong with profit. Profit just means people are paying you more for whatever you’re creating than you’re spending to create it. That’s a good thing. If that’s not the case, you’ll soon be out of business, and rightfully so, because you’re not adding enough value.709

我认为如果一个行业的规则设置得当，利润动机是好的。利润没有错。利润意味着人们为你创造的东西支付的比你创造它所花费的更多。这是好事。如果不是这样，你很快就会倒闭，这是理所当然的，因为你没有创造足够的价值。709

A few people do bad things to achieve profit, but that’s quite unusual, because the rules are mostly set up correctly. Not perfect, but mostly correct.710

少数人为了利润做坏事，但这很少见，因为规则大多设置得正确。虽然不完美，但大体正确。710

It is insanely hard to build and ship useful products to a large number of people. There’s a hell of a difference between a company that has shipped a product and one that hasn’t. It’s night and day. Once you ship products, is the value of the output worth more than the cost of the inputs? Again insanely difficult, especially with hardware.711

为大量人群打造并交付有用产品极其困难。已经交付产品的公司和未交付的公司差别巨大，天壤之别。一旦你交付了产品，产出的价值是否超过投入成本？这同样极其困难，尤其是硬件领域。711

If you create great products and services that create wealth, that should be applauded.

“如果你创造了伟大的产品和服务，创造了财富，这应该受到赞扬。”

You increased the standard of living of the country and perhaps of the world.

“你提高了国家甚至世界的生活水平。”

COMPANIES TO START创业公司

Work on things that you find interesting, fulfilling, and that contribute some good to the rest of society.712

做你觉得有趣、充实且能为社会带来益处的事情。712

Q: What are some areas you see where people could build important new technology?

问：你认为人们在哪些领域可以开发重要的新技术？

For five years people asked me where I saw opportunities and I’d say “tunneling.” But nobody did anything. It was initially a joke, but we created the Boring Company and did a test tunnel in LA. People still didn’t believe us. Then we did our first operational tunnel in Vegas.713

五年来人们一直问我哪里有机会，我总说“隧道掘进”。但没人付诸行动。最初这只是个玩笑，但我们成立了无聊公司，并在洛杉矶做了一个测试隧道。人们仍然不相信。后来我们在拉斯维加斯建成了第一个运营隧道。713

There are still tremendous opportunities in tunneling. The world needs tunnels. All major cities have traffic, and tunnels can massively improve people’s quality of life by making it easy to travel from one place to another. They can be further expanded to long-distance travel. If you draw a vacuum on the tunnel, you can go extremely fast. Faster than a plane or high-speed rail. I’d still recommend someone—please—start a tunneling company.714

隧道掘进仍有巨大机会。世界需要隧道。所有大城市都有交通问题，隧道能极大改善人们的生活质量，让出行变得轻松。隧道还能扩展到长途旅行。如果在隧道内抽真空，可以极快地行驶，比飞机或高速铁路还快。我仍然建议有人——拜托——创办一家隧道公司。714

Another company to start would be anything to do with genetics. If you can solve genetic diseases, you can prevent dementia or Alzheimer’s with genetic reprogramming. That would be wonderful.715

另一个创业方向是与基因相关的任何领域。如果能解决遗传病，就能通过基因重编程预防痴呆或阿尔茨海默症，那将非常棒。715

Synthetic RNA is revolutionary in medicine. Most people are not aware just how much of a revolution this is.

合成RNA在医学上是革命性的。大多数人还没意识到这场革命有多大。

This is like medicine going from analog to digital.716

这就像医学从模拟时代进入数字时代。716

RNA (ribonucleic acid) or MRNA (messenger RNA) are basically synthetic viruses. I think people don’t appreciate what’s going on—this is the digitization of medicine. You can create an RNA or DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) sequence like a computer program and encapsulate it in a lipid shell, so it looks like a tasty treat for your cells. This is the future of medicine.717

RNA（核糖核酸）或mRNA（信使RNA）基本上是合成病毒。我觉得人们还没真正理解这意味着什么——这是医学的数字化。你可以像写电脑程序一样设计RNA或DNA（脱氧核糖核酸）序列，并将其封装在脂质壳中，看起来像细胞的美味佳肴。这就是医学的未来。717

You can do almost anything. You could probably figure out how to turn someone into a literal butterfly. Your cells are tiny biological computers. They execute just like old-school computers, where you feed it a tape or punch card. Your cells do whatever their punch card says. That was a big eye-opener, understanding the potential of RNA.718

你几乎可以做任何事。你甚至可能弄明白如何把人变成真正的蝴蝶。你的细胞是微小的生物计算机。它们的执行方式就像老式计算机，输入磁带或穿孔卡。你的细胞按穿孔卡指令行动。理解RNA的潜力让我大开眼界。718

You could start a company for high-speed travel. Reading about the California high-speed rail was depressing. California taxpayers are paying to build the most expensive high-speed rail per mile in the world—and the slowest. Those are not the superlatives you want.719

你可以创办一家高速出行公司。读加州高速铁路的报道令人沮丧。加州纳税人正在为世界上最昂贵且最慢的高速铁路买单。那绝不是你想要的“最”。719

It’s California! We make super high-tech stuff! Why are we spending—the estimates are around $100 billion—for something that will take two hours to go from LA to San Francisco? You can get on a plane and do that in forty-five minutes.720

这是加州！我们制造超级高科技产品！为什么我们要花大约1000亿美元，建一个从洛杉矶到旧金山需要两小时的交通工具？坐飞机只需四十五分钟。720

There has to be a better way to do this. What would you ideally want in a transportation system? You want something faster than existing modes of transportation. Let’s say twice as fast, costs half as much per ticket, can’t crash, is immune to weather, and is not energy intensive. You can self-power the whole thing with solar panels or something. That would be pretty good. The devil is in the details of making the thing work, but something like this could work and be practical.721

一定有更好的方式。理想的交通系统是什么样？比现有交通方式快两倍，票价减半，不会出事故，不受天气影响，且不耗能。可以用太阳能板自供电。那会非常好。关键是让它真正可行，但这样的东西是可能且实用的。721

There’s the possibility of this fifth mode of transport; I call it the Hyperloop.722

这就是我所说的第五种交通方式；我称之为超级高铁（Hyperloop）。722

For long-distance travel, you can do tunnels or tubes. If you remove the air or most of the air, you can get rid of the air friction and go supersonic. You can do so with no dependence on weather and no need to get to high altitude or create any sonic boom issues. That’s what I envision for the Hyperloop. It’s basically a pressurized electric car in a vacuum tube. This will be our next evolution in transport.723

长途旅行可以用隧道或管道。如果抽出空气或大部分空气，就能消除空气阻力，实现超音速。无需依赖天气，也不必升高到高空或产生音爆。这就是我对超级高铁的设想。它基本上是一个加压的电动车在真空管中运行。这将是交通的下一次进化。723

We want the future to be better than the past. If we had something like the Hyperloop, you’d look forward to the day that was working. Even if it was only in one place—from LA to San Francisco, or New York to DC—it would be a tourist attraction and show it is possible.724

我们希望未来比过去更好。如果有了超级高铁，你会期待它投入运营的那一天。即使只在一条线路上——比如洛杉矶到旧金山，或纽约到华盛顿——它也会成为旅游景点，证明这是可能的。724

Even if some of the initial assumptions didn’t work out or the economics didn’t work out quite as expected, it would still be cool. If you come up with a new technology, it should feel like that. If you told a stranger, would they look forward to the day this new thing became available?725

即使最初的一些假设没实现，或者经济效益不如预期，它依然很酷。如果你开发出一项新技术，应该让人有这种感觉。如果你告诉陌生人，他们会期待这项新技术的到来吗？725

Q: What is the most important piece of advice you have for anyone who wants to start a company on behalf of humanity?

问：你对想为人类创办公司的任何人最重要的建议是什么？

The final thing I would encourage you to do is to take risks. Especially before you have kids and other obligations. As you get older, your obligations start to increase. Once you have a family, taking risks affects not just yourself, but your family as well. It gets harder to do things that might not work out. It is easiest to start before you have those obligations. Take risks now, and do something bold. You won’t regret it.726

最后我鼓励你冒险。尤其是在你还没有孩子和其他责任之前。随着年龄增长，责任会增加。一旦有了家庭，冒险不仅影响自己，也影响家人。做可能失败的事情会更难。最容易创业的时机是在没有这些责任之前。现在就冒险，做些大胆的事。你不会后悔的。726

Go do it. Just go out there and do it. People are far too afraid to try. Fear is the biggest reason for failure. Don’t be afraid to fail. Just go.727

去做吧。就去做。人们太害怕尝试了。恐惧是失败的最大原因。别怕失败。就去做。727

If you don’t push for radical breakthroughs, you’re not going to get radical outcomes.728

如果你不追求激进的突破，就不会有激进的成果。728

COMPANIES DRIVE PROGRESS公司推动进步

The rate of innovation is not constant. This year, we’re either going to increase the rate of innovation or it will slow down.729

创新的速度并非恒定。今年，我们要么加快创新速度，要么它将放缓。729

Some people think technology automatically gets better every year. It does not. It only gets better if smart people work like crazy to make it better. We need strong engineering talent applied to problems. That’s how any technology actually gets better. If people don’t work on technology, it actually will decline.730

有些人认为技术每年都会自动进步。事实并非如此。只有聪明的人拼命努力，技术才会变得更好。我们需要强大的工程人才来解决问题。任何技术的真正进步，都是这样实现的。如果没人致力于技术，它实际上会退步。730

Look at the history of civilizations and you’ll see this happen many times. Ancient Egypt was able to build incredible pyramids and forgot how to build pyramids. Then they forgot how to read hieroglyphics. In Rome, they were able to build incredible roadways, aqueducts, and indoor plumbing—they forgot how to do all those things. There are many examples in history. You should always bear in mind that entropy is not on our side.731

看看文明的历史，你会多次看到这种情况。古埃及曾建造了令人难以置信的金字塔，却后来忘记了如何建造金字塔。然后他们也忘记了如何阅读象形文字。在罗马，他们曾建造了出色的道路、水道和室内管道系统——后来却忘记了如何做到这些。历史上有许多这样的例子。你应时刻记住，熵并不站在我们这边。731

More recently, if you look at American manned space missions: We were able to go to the moon in 1969, then the space shuttle could only go to low earth orbit. Then the space shuttle retired and for almost a decade America had no access to space for humans.

更近一些，如果你看看美国载人航天任务：1969年我们登上了月球，之后航天飞机只能飞到近地轨道。航天飞机退役后，美国近十年没有载人航天的能力。

This is a pretty bad trend, trending to zero. We need a strong trend in the other direction to have any chance whatsoever of making life multiplanetary. That’s the reason for the extreme sense of urgency. If we operate with extreme urgency, we have a chance of making life multiplanetary. Still just a chance; it’s not certain. If we don’t act with extreme urgency, that chance is probably zero.

这是一个相当糟糕的趋势，趋近于零。我们需要朝相反方向形成强劲趋势，才有可能实现多星球生活。这就是极度紧迫感的原因。如果我们以极度紧迫感行动，就有机会实现多星球生活。仍然只是机会，不是确定。如果不以极度紧迫感行动，这个机会几乎为零。

I am not one of the doomsday people. I think we’re on a good path. But at the same time, I want to caution against complacency. If we are not complacent, and we have a high sense of urgency, things will be fine.732

我不是末日论者。我认为我们走在正确的道路上。但同时，我想提醒大家不要自满。如果我们不自满，保持高度紧迫感，一切都会好起来的。732

If you want the future to be good, you must make it so.

如果你希望未来美好，就必须让它美好。

Take action to make it good, and it will be.733

采取行动让未来变好，它就会变好。733

THE AGE OF ABUNDANCE丰裕时代

AI and robotics will bring about what might be termed “the age of abundance.”

人工智能和机器人技术将带来所谓的“丰裕时代。”

Other people have used this word, and that is my prediction: It will be an age of abundance for everyone.734

其他人也使用过这个词，这也是我的预测：这将是人人丰裕的时代。734

THE END OF SCARCITY稀缺的终结

People get confused sometimes; they think an economy is money. Money is just a database.

人们有时会混淆；他们认为经济就是金钱。金钱不过是一个数据库。

The actual economy is stuff. Goods and services. What limits the output of goods and services? The limiter is labor. Even capital is distilled labor, so the limiting factor for the economy is labor.735

真正的经济是物质。商品和服务。是什么限制了商品和服务的产出？限制因素是劳动。即使资本也是凝结的劳动，所以经济的限制因素是劳动。735

We can massively increase labor by building humanoid robots. If you remove labor as the limiting factor for the economy, it’s not clear that an economy in the traditional sense has any meaning anymore because you have no constraint on goods and services. There will be no shortage of goods and services.736

我们可以通过制造类人机器人大幅增加劳动。如果你消除了劳动作为经济限制因素的地位，那么传统意义上的经济是否还有意义就不明确了，因为你对商品和服务没有任何限制。商品和服务将不会短缺。736

The first units we intend to make are for jobs that are dangerous, boring, repetitive things that people don’t want to do.737

我们打算制造的第一批机器人是用于危险、无聊、重复的工作，这些是人们不愿意做的事情。737

In the future, the only forms of scarcity will be artificial scarcity (where we decide to make it scarce, like a particular piece of art) or unique items like a particular home in an exact location.738

未来，唯一存在的稀缺将是人为稀缺（我们决定让它稀缺，比如某件艺术品）或独一无二的物品，比如某个特定地点的某栋房子。738

Don’t worry about robots putting people out of jobs. We already have a massive shortage of labor. We will still have a shortage of labor even in the future. Robotics will create a world of abundance—goods and services will be available to anyone who wants them, so cheap it’ll be ridiculous.739

不用担心机器人会让人们失业。我们现在已经严重缺乏劳动力。即使在未来，我们仍将面临劳动力短缺。机器人技术将创造一个丰裕的世界——商品和服务将对任何想要的人都触手可及，价格低得令人难以置信。739

Our goal at Tesla is to make a useful humanoid robot as quickly as possible. Eventually, there will be millions of them. This means a future of abundance, a future where there is no poverty. We can afford to have a universal basic income for people. It really is a fundamental transformation of civilization.740

特斯拉的目标是尽快制造出有用的类人机器人。最终，将会有数百万台。这意味着一个丰裕的未来，一个没有贫困的未来。我们可以负担得起为人们提供普遍基本收入。这确实是文明的根本变革。740

People have no idea; the market for humanoid robots will be bigger than that of cars.741

“人们根本不知道；类人机器人的市场将比汽车市场更大。741”

There will be humanoid robots throughout factories. Cars will also be entirely automatic. Anywhere intelligence can be applied will be automated. That’s maybe 2033–2043.742

工厂里将遍布类人机器人。汽车也将完全自动化。任何可以应用智能的地方都将实现自动化。大约在2033年至2043年之间。742

As we go further into the future, things are gonna change a lot. Everything will be automatic. There will be household robots that you can talk to as if they are people. It will help you, be a companion, or whatever the case may be. It will be able to pick up your kids from school or stay with them at school if you want. It will be able to teach kids anything and support any language.

随着时间推移，事情将发生巨大变化。一切都将自动化。将会有你可以像与人交谈一样交流的家用机器人。它会帮助你，成为你的伴侣，或者其他任何角色。它可以接送你的孩子上下学，或者如果你愿意，可以陪伴他们在学校。它能够教孩子任何知识，支持任何语言。

Long term, I think the ratio of humanoid robots will be more than one-to-one. There might be two humanoid robots per person or more, maybe ten for every one. Well in excess of ten billion humanoid robots. At volume production, a humanoid robot will cost less than a car.743

从长远来看，我认为类人机器人的比例将超过一比一。可能每个人有两个类人机器人，甚至更多，可能每个人有十个。类人机器人数量远超百亿。在大规模生产时，类人机器人的成本将低于汽车。743

EXPONENTIAL INTELLIGENCE指数智能

Computing power is going to be crazy. The big change is the cost of computing power, not so much the circuit density (known as Moore’s Law). If you look at dollars per instruction, cost is dropping exponentially.

计算能力将变得疯狂。最大的变化是计算能力的成本，而不是电路密度（即摩尔定律）。如果你看每条指令的成本，成本正呈指数下降。

We’ll see massively parallel computers, computing power, and storage become extremely available. There will be ubiquitous computing everywhere.744

我们将看到大规模并行计算机、计算能力和存储变得极其普及。无处不在的计算将成为现实。744

I expect AI to be incredibly sophisticated by the 2040s. Predicting trends in an exponential curve is tricky, because up close it often looks linear. But actually, it’s not linear. The pace of AI development itself appears to be accelerating.745

我预计到2040年代，人工智能将非常复杂。预测指数曲线的趋势很棘手，因为近距离看它通常呈线性。但实际上，它并非线性。人工智能发展的速度本身似乎在加快。745

The ratio of total digital compute to total biological compute is the key metric to watch. This is perhaps the most fundamental ratio defining technological progress. It is rising incredibly fast.746

总数字计算量与总生物计算量的比率是关键指标。这或许是定义技术进步的最基本比率。它正在以惊人的速度上升。746

We’re building progressively greater intelligences, and the percentage of nonhuman intelligence is increasing. Eventually we humans will represent a small percentage of total intelligence. It might feel like we’re the biological bootloader for AI.747

我们正在构建越来越强大的智能，非人类智能的比例在增加。最终，我们人类将只占总智能的一小部分。感觉上我们像是人工智能的生物启动程序。747

We’re fairly close to artificial general intelligence, perhaps only a few years away. It’s possible we are on the event horizon of the black hole that is artificial superintelligence. Over a twenty- to thirty-year time frame, things could be transformed beyond belief. We probably won’t recognize society in thirty years.748

我们离通用人工智能相当接近，可能只差几年。我们可能正处于人工超级智能黑洞的事件视界上。在二三十年的时间范围内，事情可能会发生难以置信的变化。三十年后，我们可能认不出社会的模样。748

UPGRADING THE HUMAN MIND升级人类大脑

The internet is a great leveler for information and education. You can learn anything online for free.749

互联网是信息和教育的伟大平衡器。你可以在网上免费学习任何东西。749

A thousand years ago there were very few books.750 Even if you had a thirst for knowledge you couldn’t do much about it, because books were incredibly expensive and rare, and only a few people knew how to read. Books were difficult to get until the Gutenberg Press. Technology is what causes these big step-changes in civilization.751

一千年前，书籍非常稀少。750 即使你渴望知识，也无能为力，因为书籍极其昂贵且罕见，只有少数人会阅读。直到古腾堡印刷术出现之前，书籍都很难获得。技术才是推动文明发生巨大飞跃的原因。751

Even if you could read and were in the Library of Congress, you still didn’t have access to all the world’s information, and you certainly couldn’t search it. And, of course, very few people could be in the Library of Congress at once.752

即使你会读书，身处国会图书馆，你也无法接触到全世界的信息，更别说搜索它了。当然，能同时进入国会图书馆的人也非常少。752

The internet is something beyond the Gutenberg press. When the general public started using the internet, it felt like humanity was developing a nervous system. Any part of humanity got access to almost all human knowledge. You could be deep in the Amazon jungle with a Starlink terminal and have access to more information than the US president did in 1980.753

互联网超越了古腾堡印刷术。当大众开始使用互联网时，感觉人类正在发展一个神经系统。人类的任何一部分都能接触到几乎所有的人类知识。你可以身处亚马逊丛林深处，使用Starlink终端，获得的信息比1980年美国总统还多。753

To transfer data before the digital age, you had to write a letter. Someone would have to carry the letter to another person with a bunch of physical work in between. That’s insanely slow when you think about it. Now, you can access any book instantly; you can learn anything. It’s incredible.754

在数字时代之前传输数据，你必须写信。有人得把信送到另一个人手中，中间还要经过大量体力劳动。想想看，这速度简直慢得不可思议。现在，你可以瞬间访问任何书籍；你可以学习任何东西。真是难以置信。754

Any student of history would agree: The internet has been the biggest equalizer in history for access to information and knowledge.755

任何历史学者都会同意：互联网是历史上信息和知识获取的最大平等器。755

We already have a digital layer to our minds, in a sense, with our computer or our phone. You can access any book or song. You can ask a question on Google and get an answer instantly. With your laptop, you can outcompute an Empire State Building filled with people and calculators. These are incredible superpowers, which even the president of the United States didn’t have back in the year 2000.756

从某种意义上说，我们的大脑已经有了数字层面，通过我们的电脑或手机。你可以访问任何书籍或歌曲。你可以在谷歌上提问，立即得到答案。用你的笔记本电脑，你的计算能力超过了满是人和计算器的帝国大厦。这些都是令人难以置信的超能力，甚至2000年的美国总统都没有。756

People don’t appreciate this yet—they are already a cyborg. You’re a different creature than you would have been twenty years ago, or even ten years ago.

人们还没有意识到这一点——他们已经是半机械人了。你已经不是二十年前，甚至十年前的那个你了。

They do surveys asking, “How long can you be away from your phone?” Particularly for younger people, even a day is painful. If you leave your phone behind, it’s like missing a limb. People have already merged with their phones.757

有调查问，“你能离开手机多久？”尤其是年轻人，哪怕一天都很痛苦。如果你忘带手机，就像失去了一条肢体。人们已经和手机合为一体了。757

You’re already digitally superhuman.758

你已经是数字超级人类了。758

Q: Will the brain–computer interface change humans and how we use computers?

问：脑机接口会改变人类以及我们使用计算机的方式吗？

Yes, an intertwined idea is having a higher-bandwidth interface between computers and the brain. We’re currently bandwidth limited. The connection is bottlenecked in this tiny straw of an interface, poking glass with your meat sticks.759

会的，一个相关的想法是实现计算机与大脑之间更高带宽的接口。我们目前带宽受限。连接瓶颈在于这个极小的接口，就像用你的肉体棍戳玻璃一样。759

Ten-finger typing on a keyboard used to be the most common input to a computer. Now, it’s usually two-thumb typing. Our output quality has gone backward.

过去，十指盲打是最常见的计算机输入方式。现在通常是用两个拇指打字。我们的输出质量倒退了。

The sustained output of a human over the course of a day is less than one bit per second. There are 86,400 seconds in a day. It is extremely rare for a human to output more than that number of symbols per day, certainly for several days in a row. We should be able to improve that by many orders of magnitude with a direct neural interface—a high-bandwidth interface to your digital enhancements.760

人类一天持续输出的信息量不到每秒一比特。一天有86,400秒。人类连续几天输出超过这个符号数极为罕见。通过直接神经接口——与你的数字增强设备的高带宽接口——我们应该能将其提升数个数量级。760

Noland Arbaugh was the very first Neuralink patient. After he got the Neuralink implant, he spent all night playing Civilization, which is awesome. That’s exactly what I’d do too. Even with only roughly 10 to 15 percent of the electrodes working, we were able to achieve a bit per second. That was twice the previous world record. Maybe five years from now, we might be at a megabit—faster than any human could possibly communicate by typing or speaking.761

诺兰·阿博是Neuralink的第一位患者。植入Neuralink后，他整晚玩《文明》游戏，太棒了。这正是我会做的。即使只有大约10%到15%的电极工作，我们也能达到每秒一比特。这是之前世界纪录的两倍。也许五年后，我们能达到兆比特速度——比任何人通过打字或说话交流都快。761

The Neuralink interface can massively increase your output bandwidth and your input bandwidth. Input being written operations to the brain, where the brain is reading signals.762 If we achieve tight symbiosis, AI wouldn’t be “other”—it would be integrated with you. Imagine it has a relationship to your conscious mind similar to your unconscious mind.763

Neuralink接口可以大幅提升你的输出带宽和输入带宽。输入是写入大脑的操作，大脑读取信号。762 如果实现紧密共生，人工智能将不再是“他者”——它将与你融为一体。想象它与你的意识心灵的关系类似于你的潜意识。763

Your brain has to work to compress a bunch of concepts in your head into this incredibly low data rate format called speech or typing. That’s what language is—a compression algorithm on thought, to transfer a concept. Then it’s got to listen and decompress what’s coming in. Both of these steps are very lossy.

你的大脑必须努力将脑中大量概念压缩成这种极低数据率的格式，称为语言或打字。这就是语言——对思想的压缩算法，用以传递概念。然后它还得听并解压输入内容。这两个步骤都非常有损。

If you have two direct-brain interfaces, you could do an uncompressed direct conceptual communication with another person. Like consensual telepathy. The conversation would be a conceptual interaction on a level that’s difficult to conceive of right now.764

如果你有两个直接脑接口，你可以与另一个人进行无压缩的直接概念交流。就像是双方同意的心灵感应。对话将是难以想象的概念层面互动。764

Conceivably, there’s a way to have a digital layer of your brain feel like part of you. It’s not something you offload to consciously; it’s just “you.”765

可以设想，有一种方式让你大脑的数字层成为你的一部分。这不是你有意识地卸载的东西；它就是“你”。765

To start out, we are really just solving basic neurological damage, like for people who have essentially complete or near complete loss from the brain to the body. For someone like Stephen Hawking, the Neuralink would be incredibly profound. Imagine if Stephen Hawking could communicate as fast as we’re communicating, perhaps faster. And that’s certainly possible. Probable, in fact.

起初，我们主要解决的是基本的神经损伤，比如那些大脑到身体几乎完全丧失功能的人。对于像斯蒂芬·霍金这样的人，Neuralink将极具意义。想象一下，如果霍金能像我们交流一样快，甚至更快。这完全可能，实际上很有可能。

There’s obviously some risk with a new device. You can’t get the risk down to zero; it’s not possible. So, you want to have the highest possible reward, given there’s a certain irreducible risk. And if somebody’s able to have a profound improvement in their communication, that’s worth the risk.766

新设备显然存在一定风险。你无法将风险降至零；这是不可能的。因此，你希望在存在一定不可避免风险的情况下，获得尽可能高的回报。如果有人能显著改善他们的交流能力，那风险是值得的。766

We can deliver new information to the brain too. If somebody is completely blind, we can write directly to the visual cortex to give them sight. At first it will be relatively fairly low resolution, but long term you would have very high resolution.767

我们也可以向大脑传递新信息。如果有人完全失明，我们可以直接写入视觉皮层，赋予他们视觉。起初分辨率相对较低，但长期来看会非常高。767

This is actually our second product, called Blindsight. It enables people who are completely blind, lost both eyes or optic nerve, or just can’t see at all, to be able to see by directly triggering the neurons in the visual cortex.768

这实际上是我们的第二款产品，称为“盲视”。它使完全失明、双眼或视神经丧失，或根本看不见的人能够通过直接刺激视觉皮层的神经元来“看见”。768

At some point, the cybernetic implants wouldn’t simply be correcting things that went wrong but augmenting human capabilities. You could even see multispectral wavelengths like infrared, ultraviolet, and radar. That’s a superpower situation. Augmenting intelligence, senses, and bandwidth dramatically—that’s going to happen at some point.769

某个时候，赛博植入物不仅仅是纠正错误，而是增强人类能力。你甚至可以看到多光谱波段，如红外、紫外和雷达。这就是超能力。大幅增强智能、感官和带宽——这终将发生。769

THE LAST HUMAN DRIVERS最后的人类司机

It’s crazy to let people drive a two-ton death machine manually. They can drive into whatever they want. In the future, it’s going to seem like a mad thing that people once drove cars.770

让人们手动驾驶一台两吨重的致命机器简直疯狂。他们可能会撞上任何东西。未来，人们会觉得曾经有人亲自开车是件疯狂的事。770

In the early days of Tesla, 2013, a guy fell asleep at the wheel in a Model S, and he ran over a cyclist and killed him. Unfortunately, people fall asleep at the wheel often. If we had already built autopilot, this cyclist would still be alive. I thought, We’d better hurry up and get it working.771

在特斯拉早期的2013年，有个人在Model S里开车时睡着了，撞死了一名骑自行车的人。不幸的是，人们经常在开车时打瞌睡。如果我们当时已经有了自动驾驶，这名骑车人就不会死。我当时想，我们最好赶紧让它投入使用。771

There are clearly two massive revolutions coming to the automobile industry; one is the transition to electrification, and then the other is autonomy. It became obvious to me that in the future, any car that does not have autonomy would be about as useful as a horse. That’s not to say it has no use…but it is rare for somebody to have a horse these days. It’s obvious cars will drive themselves completely; it’s just a question of time.772

汽车行业显然将迎来两场巨大革命：一是向电气化转型，二是实现自动驾驶。我很清楚，未来任何没有自动驾驶的汽车，其用处大概和马差不多。这并不是说它毫无用处……但如今拥有马的人已经很少了。汽车完全自动驾驶是显而易见的趋势，只是时间问题。772

An autonomous car is arguably worth five to ten times more than a car that is not autonomous.773

可以说，一辆自动驾驶汽车的价值是非自动驾驶汽车的五到十倍。773

The autonomy system is likely to mitigate crashes except in rare circumstances. Vehicle safety is probabilistic. There’s some chance any time a human driver gets in a car that they will have an accident that is their fault. The odds are never zero. The key threshold for autonomy is: How much better does autonomy need to be than a person before you can rely on it?774

自动驾驶系统很可能会减少事故，除非在极少数情况下。车辆安全是概率性的。每当人类司机上车时，都有可能发生由他们引起的事故，概率永远不会是零。自动驾驶的关键门槛是：自动驾驶需要比人类好多少，才能让人们信赖它？774

About a million people die every year in car accidents and about ten million per year have serious injuries. Bringing the day of self-driving sooner translates directly to lives saved and injuries avoided. The sooner, the better. A lot of lives will be saved and made better.775

每年约有一百万人死于车祸，约一千万人受重伤。让自动驾驶的日子来得更早，直接意味着拯救生命和避免伤害。越早越好。会有很多生命因此得救，生活因此变得更好。775

Tesla deployed partial autonomy immediately because when used correctly, it was already significantly safer than a person driving by themself. It would be morally reprehensible to delay release simply for fear of bad press or some mercantile calculation of legal liability.776

特斯拉立即部署了部分自动驾驶，因为正确使用时，它已经明显比人类单独驾驶更安全。仅仅因为害怕负面报道或法律责任的商业计算而延迟发布，是道德上不可接受的。776

Autonomous cars could maneuver with a reaction time much faster than a human. Over time, autopilot will be capable of impossible maneuvers that a human could never do.777

自动驾驶汽车的反应时间远快于人类。随着时间推移，自动驾驶将能完成人类永远做不到的高难度操作。777

I told the team I want the latest data on miles per intervention to be the starting slide at each of our meetings. If we’re training AI to drive, what do we optimize? The answer is higher miles between interventions. It is motivating to watch each day as the miles per intervention increases. Video games without a score are boring.

我告诉团队，我希望每次会议的开场幻灯片都是最新的每次干预行驶里程数据。如果我们在训练AI驾驶，我们优化的是什么？答案是每次干预之间的行驶里程更长。看到每天每次干预里程增加非常有动力。没有分数的视频游戏很无聊。

We always try to do the right thing. When we make mistakes, it may be because we were being foolish or stupid, but it’s always with the right motivations.778

我们总是努力做正确的事。当我们犯错时，可能是因为愚蠢或糊涂，但动机始终是正确的。778

When true self-driving is approved by regulators, you will be able to summon your Tesla from pretty much anywhere. Once it picks you up, you will be able to sleep, read, or do anything else en route to your destination.779

当真正的自动驾驶获得监管批准时，你几乎可以在任何地方召唤你的特斯拉。它接到你后，你可以睡觉、看书或做任何事情，直到到达目的地。779

Mega-long-term, I think of cars as being effectively carriers for the autonomy software. It’s the vehicle, literally and figuratively for autonomy. It’s the software that rides on the car.780

从超长远来看，我认为汽车实际上是自动驾驶软件的载体。它是自动驾驶的载具，字面和比喻意义上都是。自动驾驶软件运行在汽车上。780

Eventually, self-driving cars will take the form of a shared autonomous fleet. You buy your car, and you could choose to use your car exclusively yourself, have it used only by friends and family, or only by other drivers who are rated five stars. You can choose to share it sometimes but not other times. That’s 100 percent what will occur. It’s just a question of when.781

最终，自动驾驶汽车将以共享自动车队的形式出现。你买车后，可以选择只自己用，或者只让亲友用，或者只让其他五星级司机用。你可以选择有时共享，有时不共享。这百分之百会发生，只是时间问题。781

This autonomy shift is massive, because you could suddenly have five times the utility of the car you currently have. Say a normal passenger car gets ten to twelve hours a week of usage. An autonomous car could be used fifty to sixty hours a week.782

这场自动驾驶的变革非常巨大，因为你突然拥有了当前汽车五倍的使用价值。比如普通乘用车每周使用十到十二小时，而自动驾驶汽车每周可使用五十到六十小时。782

This is all good for the environment, because you need fewer cars to get the same thing done. We would need fewer parking lots and garages to store cars when they’re not in use, because they’re in use a lot more.783

这对环境也有好处，因为完成同样任务需要的汽车更少。我们需要更少的停车场和车库来存放闲置车辆，因为它们的使用率大大提高了。783

If this is true, as autonomous driving capability is turned on for the Tesla fleet, it may be the biggest asset value increase in history…overnight.784

如果这是真的，当特斯拉车队开启自动驾驶功能时，可能会成为历史上最大的一次资产价值跃升……一夜之间。784

So, in short, Master Plan Part Deux is:

简而言之，第二阶段大师计划是：

I can’t say enough about the profundity of full self-driving. It will be one of the biggest changes in history. It’s not just some feature; it is as profound as electrification. We already have millions of cars on the road now able to achieve this with the right software update.786

我无法充分表达全自动驾驶的深远意义。它将是历史上最大的变革之一。这不仅仅是一个功能；它和电气化一样深远。我们现在已有数百万辆车，只需正确的软件更新即可实现这一点。786

Eventually, a vehicle must be designed as a clean Robotaxi. We’re going to take that risk. No mirrors, no pedals, no steering wheel. This will be a historically megarevolutionary product. It will transform everything. This is the product that makes Tesla a ten-trillion-dollar company. People will be talking about this moment in a hundred years.787

最终，车辆必须设计成纯净的机器人出租车。我们将承担这个风险。没有后视镜，没有踏板，没有方向盘。这将是历史上极具革命性的产品。它将改变一切。这款产品将使特斯拉成为一家十万亿美元市值的公司。百年后人们仍会谈论这一时刻。787

SUSTAINABLE ABUNDANCE可持续的丰裕

We’re accelerating the world’s transition to sustainable abundance.788

我们正在加速世界向可持续丰裕的转变。788

In Tesla’s Master Plan, Part 3, the thing we wanted to convey, more importantly than anything else, is that there is a clear path to a sustainable-energy Earth. It doesn’t require destroying natural habitats; it doesn’t require us to be austere and stop using electricity. We have published a detailed white paper with all of our assumptions and calculations of a clear path to a fully sustainable Earth, with abundance.

在特斯拉的《总体规划》第三部分中，我们想传达的最重要的信息是，通往可持续能源地球的道路是清晰的。这不需要破坏自然栖息地；也不需要我们节俭到停止用电。我们已经发布了一份详细的白皮书，列出了所有假设和计算，展示了通往一个完全可持续且丰裕的地球的明确路径。

In fact, you could support a civilization much larger on Earth. Much more than the current eight billion humans could be supported sustainably on Earth.

事实上，地球上可以支持一个规模更大的文明。远远超过目前的八十亿人口可以被可持续地支持。

I’m often shocked by how few people realize this. Most of the smart people I know actually don’t see this clear path. They think there is no path to a sustainable energy future with our current population. Or that we’d have to resort to extreme measures. None of this is true. In Master Plan, Part 3, we walk through the calculations for how to create a sustainable-energy civilization.789

我常常对很少有人意识到这一点感到震惊。我认识的大多数聪明人实际上并没有看到这条清晰的道路。他们认为以我们当前的人口规模，没有通往可持续能源未来的道路。或者认为我们必须采取极端措施。这些都不是真的。在《总体规划》第三部分中，我们详细计算了如何创建一个可持续能源文明的路径。789

A fully electrified and sustainable economy is within reach through the actions in this paper:

通过本文中的行动，实现一个完全电气化和可持续的经济是触手可及的：

Our models show the electrified and sustainable future is technically feasible and requires less investment and less material extraction than continuing today’s unsustainable energy economy.790

我们的模型显示，电气化和可持续的未来在技术上是可行的，并且比继续维持当前不可持续的能源经济需要更少的投资和材料开采。790

The tools we make at Tesla help us build the products that advance human prosperity. Making technologically advanced products that are affordable and available at scale is required to build a flourishing and unconstrained society. It serves to further democratize society while raising everyone’s quality of life in the process.

我们在特斯拉制造的工具帮助我们打造推动人类繁荣的产品。制造技术先进、价格合理且大规模可用的产品，是建设一个繁荣且无限制社会的必要条件。这有助于进一步实现社会民主化，同时提升每个人的生活质量。

The hallmark of meritocracy is creating opportunities that enable each person to use their skills to accomplish whatever they imagine. Everyone deserves access to these opportunities, and technological growth can help ensure that each of us is able to maximize our most limited resource: time.

精英制度的标志是创造机会，使每个人都能运用自己的技能实现他们想象中的目标。每个人都应有机会获得这些机会，技术进步可以帮助确保我们每个人都能最大化我们最有限的资源：时间。

OUR EXISTENTIAL RISKS我们的生存风险

Don’t worry about it.

别担心它。

I mean, worry about it.

我是说，要担心它。

Because if you worry about it, ironically, it will be okay.

因为如果你担心它，具有讽刺意味的是，一切都会好起来。

It will be a self-unfulfilling prophecy.791

这将成为一个自我未实现的预言。791

THIS IS THE BEST TIME TO BE ALIVE这是有史以来最好的时代

Q: What period of history would you prefer to live in the most?

问：你最想生活在哪个历史时期？

Right now. The present is the most interesting time in history—and I read a lot of history.

现在。现在是历史上最有趣的时期——而且我读了很多历史。

Let’s do our best to keep that going.792

让我们尽力保持这种状态。792

I find history fascinating. A lot of incredible things have been done, good and bad. By learning about them, you understand the nature of civilization and individuals.793

我觉得历史非常迷人。许多令人难以置信的事情已经发生过，有好有坏。通过了解它们，你能理解文明和个体的本质。793

There’s a lot of human history, but most of it is people getting on with their lives. Human history is not a nonstop war and disaster. Those are actually intermittent and rare. If they weren’t, humans would soon cease to exist. But historians write more about wars than peace. A normal year where nothing major happened won’t be written about much. Most people just farmed and lived their life as a villager somewhere.794

人类历史很长，但大多数时间人们只是过着自己的生活。人类历史并非持续不断的战争和灾难。那些其实是间歇且罕见的。如果不是这样，人类很快就会灭绝。但历史学家写的更多是战争而非和平。一个平凡的年份，如果没有重大事件发生，通常不会被详细记载。大多数人只是耕种，作为某个村庄的村民过日子。794

Life was tough for most of history. In most of human history, a good year would be one where not that many people in your village died of disease, starvation, freezing, or being killed by a neighboring village. If you only lost 5 percent of your village in a year, that was a good year.795

在大部分历史时期，生活都很艰难。在人类历史的大部分时间里，一个好年景是指你村里死于疾病、饥饿、严寒或被邻村杀害的人不多。如果一年只失去5%的村民，那就是好年景。795

Not starving to death would have been the primary goal of most people throughout history. Just making sure they had enough food to last through the winter and not freeze to death.796

不饿死是历史上大多数人的首要目标。确保有足够的食物度过冬天，不被冻死。796

If you judge history from what is morally acceptable today, you’d give everyone a failing grade. I don’t think anyone would get a passing grade in morality if you look back to even three hundred years ago.797

如果用今天的道德标准来评判历史，你会给所有人不及格。我认为即使回顾三百年前，也没人能在道德上及格。797

Q: How does history help you understand the future?

问：历史如何帮助你理解未来？

I try to understand the rise and fall of civilizations. I’ve read a lot of history to discern the facts of what humans did.798

我试图理解文明的兴衰。我读了很多历史，以辨别人类所做的事实。798

We take for granted that “it’s always going to be there,” but if you study history, you realize civilizations rise and fall.799

我们理所当然地认为“它总会存在”，但如果你研究历史，你会发现文明是兴起又衰落的。799

I was reading about the ancient Sumerians, who were arguably the first civilization. They were the first to develop writing, but eventually they died out. Then, nobody could read their writing at all. They were impressive in their time but they faded out as a civilization. The ancient Egyptians, the same thing. One after another. Ancient Greece had its day. There’ve been ebbs and flows in the Chinese and Indian civilizations over the eons as well.800

我曾读过关于古代苏美尔人的资料，他们可以说是最早的文明。他们是最早发明文字的文明，但最终消亡了。后来没人能读懂他们的文字。他们当时很了不起，但作为一个文明逐渐消失。古埃及也是如此。一个接一个。古希腊曾有辉煌时期。中国和印度文明在漫长岁月中也经历过起伏。800

Only a tiny fraction of what was ever written in history is available to us now. Probably less than 1 percent. If they didn’t chisel it in stone or put it in a clay tablet, we don’t have it. There are a few papyrus scrolls that are thousands of years old because they were deep inside a pyramid. The little information we have shows many civilizations rising and falling. It’s wild. And the basics of human nature are more or less the same today.

历史上写下的文字只有极小一部分流传至今。可能不到1%。如果没有刻在石头上或写在泥板上，我们就没有保存下来。有一些几千年前的纸草卷因为藏在金字塔深处而保存下来。我们掌握的少量信息显示许多文明的兴衰。这非常惊人。人性的基本特征今天大致相同。

We see patterns for civilizations as they go through a life cycle, like an organism does. A human is a zygote, fetus, baby, toddler, teenager, eventually gets older, and dies. Every civilization goes through a life cycle. No civilization will last forever.801

我们看到文明像有机体一样经历生命周期。人类从受精卵、胎儿、婴儿、幼儿、青少年，最终变老并死亡。每个文明也经历生命周期。没有文明能永远存在。801

The birth rate might be the biggest single threat to the future of human civilization. Artificial intelligence (AI) gone wrong is a big concern. Religious extremism is a concern. There are quite a few important problems to solve.802

出生率可能是人类文明未来最大的单一威胁。人工智能（AI）失控也是一个重大担忧。宗教极端主义也是问题之一。还有许多重要问题需要解决。802

We should view our civilization as much more fragile than we think.803

我们应该把文明看得比想象中更脆弱。803

We must keep civilization going onward and upward as much as possible and try to minimize the civilizational threats that occur.804

我们必须尽可能让文明持续向前和向上发展，努力减少文明面临的威胁。804

WORLD WAR III第三次世界大战

I’m not predicting a world war anytime soon. But given enough time, it will likely happen. This has been our pattern in the past. In the last century we had two massive world wars, three if you count the Cold War. It’s unlikely that we’ll never have another world war. We probably will at some point.805

我并不预测近期会发生世界大战。但如果时间足够长，战争很可能会发生。这是我们过去的模式。上个世纪我们经历了两次大规模的世界大战，如果算上冷战则是三次。我们不太可能永远不会再有世界大战。某个时候我们大概率会经历一次。805

Any war is tragic and difficult on a local basis. Then some wars are civilization-ending, or have that potential. Global thermonuclear warfare has a high potential to end civilization, perhaps permanently. But it could severely wound civilization and set back human progress to the Stone Age.

任何战争在局部范围内都是悲惨且艰难的。有些战争甚至可能终结文明，或者具有这种潜力。全球热核战争极有可能终结文明，甚至可能是永久性的。但它也可能严重伤害文明，使人类进步倒退到石器时代。

We want to prioritize avoiding civilizational risks over things that are painful and tragic on a local level, but are not civilizational risks.806

我们希望优先避免文明风险，而不是那些在局部层面上痛苦且悲惨，但并非文明风险的事情。806

Few people alive today viscerally understand the horrors of war, at least in the US.

今天活着的人很少能直观地理解战争的恐怖，至少在美国是这样。

This concerns me.

这让我感到担忧。

Obviously people on the front lines in Ukraine and Russia understand how terrible war is, but how many people in the West understand it? My grandfather was in World War II. He was severely traumatized. For almost six years he was fighting in eastern North Africa and Italy. All his friends were killed in front of him. He would’ve died there too, except they finally gave him an IQ test. After the test, he was transferred to British intelligence in London.

显然，乌克兰和俄罗斯前线的人们理解战争的可怕，但西方有多少人真正理解呢？我祖父参加过第二次世界大战。他受到严重的创伤。将近六年时间里，他一直在北非东部和意大利作战。他所有的朋友都在他面前阵亡。他本来也会死在那里，除非他们最终给他做了智商测试。测试后，他被调到伦敦的英国情报部门。

From the war, he had extreme PTSD (post-traumatic stress disorder). Like next level. He just didn’t talk. Ever. If you tried talking to him, he’d just tell you to shut up. He won a bunch of medals. Never bragged about it once, not even hinted at it. Nothing. I found out by searching his military records online.

战争给他带来了极端的创伤后应激障碍（PTSD）。那是非常严重的。他根本不说话，从不。如果你试图和他说话，他只会让你闭嘴。他获得了许多奖章，但从未炫耀过，甚至没有暗示过。我是通过网上查找他的军队记录才知道的。

He knows the horrors of war. He would say, “No way in hell do you want war. Avoid it at all costs.” But he died thirty years ago. How many people are alive now who viscerally remember World War II? Not many.807

他了解战争的恐怖。他会说：“你绝对不想要战争。无论如何都要避免战争。”但他三十年前就去世了。现在还有多少人能直观地记得第二次世界大战？不多了。807

I recommend people read about World War I warfare in detail. It’s rough. The sheer number of people who died is mind boggling. There’s a saying from World War I, “Young boys who don’t know each other, killing each other on behalf of old men that do know each other.” What the hell is the point of that?808

我建议大家详细阅读第一次世界大战的战争情况。那非常残酷。死亡人数之多令人难以置信。第一次世界大战有句谚语：“年轻的男孩们彼此不认识，却为了彼此认识的老男人而互相残杀。”这到底有什么意义？808

Something must stop the cycle of reciprocal violence. Something must stop it or it will never stop. Just eye for an eye, tooth for a tooth, limb for a limb, life for a life, forever and ever.809
